## When God Laughs / 当上帝发笑的时候

===Original===
*(With Compliments to Harry Cowell)*
> “The gods, the gods are stronger; time
> Falls down before them, all men’s knees
> Bow, all men’s prayers and sorrows climb
> Like incense toward them; yea, for these
> Are gods, Felise.”
Carquinez had relaxed finally. He stole a glance at the rattling windows, looked upward at the beamed roof, and listened for a moment to the savage roar of the southeaster as it caught the bungalow in its bellowing jaws. Then he held his glass between him and the fire and laughed for joy through the golden wine.
“It is beautiful,” he said. “It is sweetly sweet. It is a woman’s wine, and it was made for gray-robed saints to drink.”
“We grow it on our own warm hills,” I said, with pardonable California pride. “You rode up yesterday through the vines from which it was made.”
It was worthwhile to get Carquinez to loosen up. Nor was he ever really himself until he felt the mellow warmth of the vine singing in his blood. He was an artist, it is true, always an artist; but somehow, sober, the high pitch and lilt went out of his thought-processes and he was prone to be as deadly dull as a British Sunday﻿—not dull as other men are dull, but dull when measured by the sprightly wight that Monte Carquinez was when he was really himself.

===Chinese===
*（赠哈里·考威尔，以表敬意）*
> “诸神，诸神更为强有力；时光
> 在他们面前倒伏，众人的膝盖
> 尽皆弯屈，众人的祈祷与忧伤
> 如熏香一般向他们升去；是的，因为这些
> 乃是神，菲莉丝。”
卡基内斯终于松弛了下来。他偷眼瞥了瞥震得咯咯作响的窗子，抬头望了望梁木屋顶，又听了一阵东南风那狂野的咆哮——风正用怒吼的巨颚咬住这座平房。然后他把酒杯举在自己与炉火之间，透过金色的酒液，欢喜地笑了。
“真美，”他说，“甜上加甜。这是女人的酒，是酿给灰袍圣徒喝的。”
“这酒长在我们自己温暖的山坡上，”我说，带着情有可原的加州自豪。“你昨天骑马上山，穿行过的正是酿酒的那片葡萄园。”
能让卡基内斯松弛下来，是值得的。不等到葡萄那醇厚的暖意在他血液里歌唱起来，他从不曾真正显出本相。他是个艺术家，这不假，从来都是艺术家；可是不知怎么，一清醒，他思想进程里的高亢与轻快便没了，他会闷得要命，活像英国的礼拜天——那闷不是常人的闷，而是拿蒙特·卡基内斯真正的自我——那个活泼家伙——来衡量时才显得闷。

===Original===
From all this it must not be inferred that Carquinez, who is my dear friend and dearer comrade, was a sot. Far from it. He rarely erred. As I have said, he was an artist. He knew when he had enough, and enough, with him, was equilibrium﻿—the equilibrium that is yours and mine when we are sober.
His was a wise and instinctive temperateness that savoured of the Greek. Yet he was far from Greek. “I am Aztec, I am Inca, I am Spaniard,” I have heard him say. And in truth he looked it, a compound of strange and ancient races, what with his swarthy skin and the asymmetry and primitiveness of his features. His eyes, under massively arched brows, were wide apart and black with the blackness that is barbaric, while before them was perpetually falling down a great black mop of hair through which he gazed like a roguish satyr from a thicket. He invariably wore a soft flannel shirt under his velvet-corduroy jacket, and his necktie was red. This latter stood for the red flag (he had once lived with the socialists of Paris), and it symbolized the blood and brotherhood of man. Also, he had never been known to wear anything on his head save a leather-banded sombrero. It was even rumoured that he had been born with this particular piece of headgear. And in my experience it was provocative of nothing short of sheer delight to see that Mexican sombrero hailing a cab in Piccadilly or storm-tossed in the crush for the New York Elevated.
As I have said, Carquinez was made quick by wine﻿—“as the clay was made quick when God breathed the breath of life into it,” was his way of saying it. I confess that he was blasphemously intimate with God; and I must add that there was no blasphemy in him. He was at all times honest, and, because he was compounded of paradoxes, greatly misunderstood by those who did not know him. He could be as elementally raw at times as a screaming savage; and at other times as delicate as a maid, as subtle as a Spaniard. And﻿—well, was he not Aztec? Inca? Spaniard?
And now I must ask pardon for the space I have given him. (He is my friend, and I love him.) The house was shaking to the storm, as he drew closer to the fire and laughed at it through his wine. He looked at me, and by the added lustre of his eye, and by the alertness of it, I knew that at last he was pitched in his proper key.

===Chinese===
由此切不可推论说，我亲爱的朋友、更亲爱的伙伴卡基内斯是个酒鬼。差得远呢。他难得失度。我说过，他是艺术家。他知道自己几时喝够，而对他来说，够便是平衡——就是你我清醒时所保有的那种平衡。
他那份节制明智而出自本能，颇有希腊风味。然而他又远非希腊人。“我是阿兹特克人，我是印加人，我是西班牙人，”我听他这么说来着。事实上他看着也真像，是各种古老而奇异的族类的混合体，且看他黝黑的皮肤，看他五官的不匀称与原始气。他一双眼睛在厚重的弓形眉下分得很开，黑得野蛮，眼前又永远垂着一蓬蓬乱的黑发，他从发丛后望出来，活像灌木丛里探头探脑的促狭山精。丝绒灯芯绒上衣底下，他总是穿一件软法兰绒衬衫，领带是红的。这领带代表红旗（他一度同巴黎的社会主义者们住在一起），象征人类的血与手足之情。再者，谁也没见他头上戴过别的什么，只除了那顶皮檐墨西哥阔边帽。甚至有谣传说，他生下来头上就顶着这么一顶。就我的阅历而言，眼看那顶墨西哥帽在皮卡迪利大街上喝叫马车，或者在争挤纽约高架车的乱流里任风颠簸，除了教人纯粹地心花怒放，再无别的作用。
正如我说过的，酒使卡基内斯活络起来——“就像上帝把生命之气吹进泥里，泥便活了，”他惯于这么说。我承认，他同上帝亲昵得近乎渎神；可我还得补一句，他身上没有一丝渎神。他任何时候都坦白，又因为浑身是悖论，凡不曾深识他的人都大大误解了他。他有时能粗粝得原始，像个尖声喊叫的野人；有时又娇细如少女，幽微如西班牙人。而且——可不是么，他难道不是阿兹特克人？印加人？西班牙人？
如今我必须为分给他的这许多篇幅乞恕。（他是我的朋友，我爱他。）屋宇在风暴中震颤，他向炉火凑近些，隔着酒液讥笑风暴。他望着我，凭他眼中平添的光泽，凭那眼的机警，我知道他终于定准了自己的本调。

===Original===
“And so you think you’ve won out against the gods?” he demanded.
“Why the gods?”
“Whose will but theirs has put satiety upon man?” he cried.
“And whence the will in me to escape satiety?” I asked triumphantly.
“Again the gods,” he laughed. “It is their game we play. They deal and shuffle all the cards﻿ ﻿… and take the stakes. Think not that you have escaped by fleeing from the mad cities. You with your vine-clad hills, your sunsets and your sunrises, your homely fare and simple round of living!
“I’ve watched you ever since I came. You have not won. You have surrendered. You have made terms with the enemy. You have made confession that you are tired. You have flown the white flag of fatigue. You have nailed up a notice to the effect that life is ebbing down in you. You have run away from life. You have played a trick, shabby trick. You have balked at the game. You refuse to play. You have thrown your cards under the table and run away to hide, here amongst your hills.”

===Chinese===
“这么说，你以为你已经赢过诸神了？”他质问。
“为什么说诸神？”
“除了他们的意志，还有谁的意志把餍足加在世人身上？”他喊道。
“那么我心里逃脱餍足的意志又是打哪儿来的？”我得意地问。
“还是诸神，”他笑道。“我们玩的是他们的牌局。洗牌发牌全归他们……彩头也归他们收。别以为逃出那些疯狂的都市，你就脱身了。你，守着你那披满藤蔓的山丘，你的落日与朝阳，你那家常的饭食、简朴的营生轮回！
“打从我来那天起，我一直看着你。你没有赢。你投降了。你同敌人讲定了条件。你招认自己累了。你升起了倦怠的白旗。你钉出一方告白，说生命正在你体内退潮。你从人生跟前逃走了。你耍了个花招，个寒碜的花招。你在牌局前却了步。你拒绝再玩。你把牌扔到桌子底下，跑到你的山里藏起来了。”

===Original===
He tossed his straight hair back from his flashing eyes, and scarcely interrupted to roll a long, brown, Mexican cigarette.
“But the gods know. It is an old trick. All the generations of man have tried it﻿ ﻿… and lost. The gods know how to deal with such as you. To pursue is to possess, and to possess is to be sated. And so you, in your wisdom, have refused any longer to pursue. You have elected surcease. Very well. You will become sated with surcease. You say you have escaped satiety! You have merely bartered it for senility. And senility is another name for satiety. It is satiety’s masquerade. Bah!”
“But look at me!” I cried.
Carquinez was ever a demon for haling ones soul out and making rags and tatters of it.
He looked me witheringly up and down.

===Chinese===
他把一头直发从灼灼的目光前甩开去，手上几乎不作停顿，卷了一支长长的褐色墨西哥纸烟。
“可是诸神知道。这是老花招了。人类一代又一代全试过……全输了。诸神知道怎样对付你这号人。追求便是占有，占有便是餍足。于是你，凭你的聪明，再也不肯追求了。你选择了止歇。很好。你将在止歇中餍足。你说你逃脱了餍足！你不过拿它换来了衰朽。衰朽是餍足的又一个名字，是餍足的假面舞会。呸！”
“可你瞧瞧我！”我叫道。
卡基内斯向来是个恶魔，惯于把人的灵魂拖将出来，扯成一片片破布烂絮。
他把我上上下下打量，看得人蔫了。

===Original===
“You see no signs,” I challenged.
“Decay is insidious,” he retorted. “You are rotten ripe.”
I laughed and forgave him for his very deviltry. But he refused to be forgiven.
“Do I not know?” he asked. “The gods always win. I have watched men play for years what seemed a winning game. In the end they lost.”
“Don’t you ever make mistakes?” I asked.
He blew many meditative rings of smoke before replying.

===Chinese===
“你看不出什么迹象，”我激他。
“腐朽来时是悄没声的，”他反唇相讥。“你已经熟得要烂了。”
我笑了，饶恕了他这十足的恶作剧。可他不肯受这饶恕。
“我还会不知道？”他问。“诸神永远赢。我看着一些人打了多年看似稳赢的牌。到头来他们输了。”
“那你就从不失手？”我问。
他吐出一圈圈沉思的烟环，然后才回答。

===Original===
“Yes, I was nearly fooled, once. Let me tell you. There was Marvin Fiske. You remember him? And his Dantesque face and poet’s soul, singing his chant of the flesh, the very priest of Love? And there was Ethel Baird, whom also you must remember.”
“A warm saint,” I said.
“That is she! Holy as Love, and sweeter! Just a woman, made for love; and yet﻿—how shall I say?﻿—drenched through with holiness as your own air here is with the perfume of flowers. Well, they married. They played a hand with the gods﻿—”
“And they won, they gloriously won!” I broke in.
Carquinez looked at me pityingly, and his voice was like a funeral bell.
“They lost. They supremely, colossally lost.”

===Chinese===
“失过，有一回差点上了当。讲给你听。有个马文·菲斯克。你记得吧？还有他那张但丁式的脸、那颗诗人的灵魂，唱着肉体的颂歌，简直是爱情本身的祭司？还有埃塞尔·贝尔德，你也一定记得。”
“一位温暖的圣女，”我说。
“正是她！像爱情一样圣洁，而且更甜！就是个女人，为爱而造；然而——叫我怎么说呢？——通体浸透了圣洁，就像你们这儿的空气浸透了花香。后来他们结了婚。他们同诸神打了一手牌——”
“他们赢了，赢得何等辉煌！”我插嘴道。
卡基内斯怜悯地望着我，他的声音像一口丧钟。
“他们输了。输得彻彻底底、山崩地裂。”

===Original===
“But the world believes otherwise,” I ventured coldly.
“The world conjectures. The world sees only the face of things. But I know. Has it ever entered your mind to wonder why she took the veil, buried herself in that dolorous convent of the living dead?”
“Because she loved him so, and when he died﻿ ﻿…”
Speech was frozen on my lips by Carquinez’s sneer.
“A pat answer,” he said, “machine-made like a piece of cotton-drill. The world’s judgment! And much the world knows about it. Like you, she fled from life. She was beaten. She flung out the white flag of fatigue. And no beleaguered city ever flew that flag in such bitterness and tears.

===Chinese===
“可世人相信的却不是这样，”我冷冷地冒了一句。
“世人不过在瞎猜。世人只看见事物的表面。可我知道。你可曾起过念头，纳闷她为什么蒙起面纱，把自己埋进那座活死人的愁惨修道院？”
“因为她太爱他了，等到他一死……”
卡基内斯一声冷笑，把话冻在我嘴唇上。
“现成的答案，”他说，“机器轧出来的，同一匹斜纹布一样。世人的判词！世人又懂多少。同你一样，她也逃避了人生。她被打垮了。她挂出了倦怠的白旗。被围的城池，还没有一座挂这面旗挂得这样苦、这样泪湿的。

===Original===
“Now I shall tell you the whole tale, and you must believe me, for I know. They had pondered the problem of satiety. They loved Love. They knew to the uttermost farthing the value of Love. They loved him so well that they were fain to keep him always, warm and a-thrill in their hearts. They welcomed his coming; they feared to have him depart.
“Love was desire, they held, a delicious pain. He was ever seeking easement, and when he found that for which he sought, he died. Love denied was Love alive; Love granted was Love deceased. Do you follow me? They saw it was not the way of life to be hungry for what it has. To eat and still be hungry﻿—man has never accomplished that feat. The problem of satiety. That is it. To have and to keep the sharp famine-edge of appetite at the groaning board. This was their problem, for they loved Love. Often did they discuss it, with all Love’s sweet ardours brimming in their eyes; his ruddy blood spraying their cheeks; his voice playing in and out with their voices, now hiding as a tremolo in their throats, and again shading a tone with that ineffable tenderness which he alone can utter.
“How do I know all this? I saw﻿—much. More I learned from her diary. This I found in it, from Fiona Macleod: ‘For, truly, that wandering voice, that twilight-whisper, that breath so dewy-sweet, that flame-winged lute-player whom none sees but for a moment, in a rainbow-shimmer of joy, or a sudden lightning-flare of passion, this exquisite mystery we call Amor, comes, to some rapt visionaries at least, not with a song upon the lips that all may hear, or with blithe viol of public music, but as one wrought by ecstasy, dumbly eloquent with desire.’
“How to keep the flame-winged lute-player with his dumb eloquence of desire? To feast him was to lose him. Their love for each other was a great love. Their granaries were overflowing with plenitude; yet they wanted to keep the sharp famine-edge of their love undulled.
“Nor were they lean little fledglings theorizing on the threshold of Love. They were robust and realized souls. They had loved before, with others, in the days before they met; and in those days they had throttled Love with caresses, and killed him with kisses, and buried him in the pit of satiety.
“They were not cold wraiths, this man and woman. They were warm human. They had no Saxon soberness in their blood. The colour of it was sunset-red. They glowed with it. Temperamentally theirs was the French joy in the flesh. They were idealists, but their idealism was Gallic. It was not tempered by the chill and sombre fluid that for the English serves as blood. There was no stoicism about them. They were Americans, descended out of the English, and yet the refraining and self-denying of the English spirit-groping were not theirs.

===Chinese===
“现在我把整个故事讲给你，你必须信我，因为我知道底细。他们早就掂量过餍足这个问题。他们爱爱情。他们把爱情的价值摸到了最后一文钱。他们太爱他了，一心要永远把他留在心头，暖暖的、颤巍巍的。他们迎他来；他们怕他去。
“爱情便是欲望，他们这样主张，一种甘美的痛楚。他永远在寻求纾解，一旦寻到了所求的东西，他便死了。被拒绝的爱情是活的爱情；被允准的爱情是死了的爱情。你跟得上我的话么？他们看出，生命之道，原不该对自己已有之物还觉饥饿。吃了却仍旧饿着——人从来不曾做成这桩绝技。餍足的难题。就是这个。在杯盘累累的筵席上，既占有，又保住胃口的锋利饥刃。这便是他们的难题，因为他们爱爱情。他们时常讨论它，爱情全部的甜蜜炽热盛满他们眼中；爱情红润的血喷洒上他们的双颊；爱情的声音同他们的声音穿插嬉戏，一会儿化作颤音藏进他们喉咙，一会儿又给一个音调罩上那种唯他才能吐出的、难以言喻的温柔。
“这些我怎么知道？我亲眼见过——见过许多。更多的，是从她的日记里得知。我在里面找到这样一段，出自菲奥娜·麦克劳德：‘的确，那游荡的声音，那暮色中的低语，那露水般甜的气息，那火焰翅膀的鲁特琴手——谁也只在刹那间见他，或在喜悦的虹彩微光里，或在情欲骤亮的电光中——这个我们唤作阿莫尔的精妙奥秘，至少对某些出神的先见者而言，来临时唇上并没有人人听得见的歌，也没有公共音乐的欢悦维奥尔琴，而是像一个被狂喜铸就的人，哑然无言，却满含欲望的雄辩。’
“怎样留住那带着欲望之缄默雄辩的火焰翅膀琴手？宴享他就是失去他。他们俩的爱情是一场大爱。他们的谷仓满得盛不下丰饶；可他们偏要保住爱情的锋利饥刃，不让它钝掉。
“他们也不是站在爱情门槛上发空论的瘦雏儿。他们是健旺而彻悟的灵魂。相遇之前，在各自从前的日子里，他们同别人爱过；在那些日子里，他们曾用爱抚扼杀爱情，用亲吻杀死他，把他埋进餍足的深坑。
“这一男一女不是冷冰冰的幽灵。他们是有体温的活人。他们血液里没有撒克逊式的冷静。那血是落日的红色。他们被这红色照得通亮。气质上，他们所有的是法国人对肉身的欢悦。他们是理想主义者，但他们的理想主义是高卢式的，并未被英国人当作血液的那股阴冷沉郁的液体回火。他们身上没有斯多噶气。他们是美国人，从英格兰一脉传下来，然而英国人摸索灵性时的那种克制与自抑，却没有传给他们。
===Original===
“They were all this that I have said, and they were made for joy, only they achieved a concept. A curse on concepts! They played with logic, and this was their logic.﻿—But first let me tell you of a talk we had one night. It was of Gautier’s Madeline de Maupin. You remember the maid? She kissed once, and once only, and kisses she would have no more. Not that she found kisses were not sweet, but that she feared with repetition they would cloy. Satiety again! She tried to play without stakes against the gods. Now this is contrary to a rule of the game the gods themselves have made. Only the rules are not posted over the table. Mortals must play in order to learn the rules.
“Well, to the logic. The man and the woman argued thus: Why kiss once only? If to kiss once were wise, was it not wiser to kiss not at all? Thus could they keep Love alive. Fasting, he would knock forever at their hearts.
“Perhaps it was out of their heredity that they achieved this unholy concept. The breed will out and sometimes most fantastically. Thus in them did cursed Albion array herself a scheming wanton, a bold, cold-calculating, and artful hussy. After all, I do not know. But this I know: it was out of their inordinate desire for joy that they forewent joy.
“As he said (I read it long afterward in one of his letters to her): ‘To hold you in my arms, close, and yet not close. To yearn for you, and never to have you, and so always to have you.’ And she: ‘For you to be always just beyond my reach. To be ever attaining you, and yet never attaining you, and for this to last forever, always fresh and new, and always with the first flush upon us.
“That is not the way they said it. On my lips their love-philosophy is mangled. And who am I to delve into their soul-stuff? I am a frog, on the dank edge of a great darkness, gazing goggle-eyed at the mystery and wonder of their flaming souls.
“And they were right, as far as they went. Everything is good﻿ ﻿… as long as it is unpossessed. Satiety and possession are Death’s horses; they run in span.

===Chinese===
“我说的这些他们一应俱全，他们生来是享乐的，只是他们造出了一个概念。让概念遭诅咒吧！他们玩弄逻辑，这便是他们的逻辑。——不过先让我讲一晚我们的一场谈话。谈的是戈蒂耶的《莫班小姐》。记得那位姑娘么？她吻过一次，仅此一次，再不肯吻了。不是她觉得吻不甜，而是她怕一重复便会腻。又是餍足！她想同诸神赌一场没有彩头的牌。这就违逆了诸神亲自定下的一条游戏规则。只是规则从不张贴在牌桌上方。凡人必须下场玩过，才能懂得规则。
“好，说逻辑。那男人和那女人是这样推论的：为什么只吻一次？倘若吻一次是明智，那么一次不吻岂不更明智？这样便能留住爱情。他守着斋，便会永远敲他们的心扉。
“也许正是从血统里，他们才造出这不祥的概念。品性总要冒头，有时冒得奇形怪状。于是，在这两人身上，可诅咒的阿尔比恩竟妆扮成一个工于心计的荡妇，一个泼辣、冷算、乖巧的婆娘。说到底，我不知道。可这一点我知道：正是出于对欢乐的漫无节制欲，他们舍弃了欢乐。
“正如他说的（很久以后，我在他写给她的一封信里读到）：‘把你抱在怀里，紧紧的，却又不真紧。渴想你，永远得不到你，于是便永远拥有你。’而她说：‘愿你永远在我将将够不着的地方。永远在抵达你，却又永远抵达不了你，愿这情形绵延不尽，永远新鲜簇新，永远带着初上的红晕。’
“他们原话不是这么说的。他们的爱情哲学经我这张嘴一说便走了样。况且我算什么人，配去开掘他们的灵魂质料？我是一只蛙，蹲在浩大黑暗的湿冷边缘，瞪圆了眼睛，呆望他们烈焰灵魂的奥秘与奇观。
“他们没有错，就他们走到的那一步而言。样样都好……只要还不曾占有。餍足与占有是死神的马；并辔而驰。

===Original===
> “ ‘And time could only tutor us to eke
> Our rapture’s warmth with custom’s afterglow.’
“They got that from a sonnet of Alfred Austin’s. It was called *Love’s Wisdom*. It was the one kiss of Madeline de Maupin. How did it run?
> “ ‘Kiss we and part; no further can we go;
> And better death than we from high to low
> Should dwindle, or decline from strong to weak.’
“But they were wiser. They would not kiss and part. They would not kiss at all, and thus they planned to stay at Love’s topmost peak. They married. You were in England at the time. And never was there such a marriage. They kept their secret to themselves. I did not know, then. Their rapture’s warmth did not cool. Their love burned with increasing brightness. Never was there anything like it. The time passed, the months, the years, and ever the flame-winged lute-player grew more resplendent.

===Chinese===
> “ ‘而时间只能教导我们，拿习惯的余烬
> 去续长狂喜的暖意。’
“这两句得自阿尔弗雷德·奥斯汀的一首十四行诗。诗题《爱的智慧》。这正是莫班小姐的那一个吻。原诗怎么说的？
> “ ‘吻罢分手；再往前已无路可走；
> 与其任我们从高处萎缩到低处、
> 从坚强败落到软弱，宁可死。’
“可他们更明智。他们不肯吻罢分手。他们干脆一吻也不吻，如此打算永驻爱情的绝顶。他们结了婚。你那时在英国。那样的婚姻从来没有过。他们把秘密守在两人之间。我那时并不知道。他们狂喜的暖意不曾冷却。他们的爱情烧得愈来愈亮。那样的事从来没有过。时间过去，一月月，一年年，那火焰翅膀的琴手愈发光华灿烂。

===Original===
“Everybody marvelled. They became the wonderful lovers, and they were greatly envied. Sometimes women pitied her because she was childless; it is the form the envy of such creatures takes.
“And I did not know their secret. I pondered and I marvelled. As first I had expected, subconsciously I imagine, the passing of their love. Then I became aware that it was Time that passed and Love that remained. Then I became curious. What was their secret? What were the magic fetters with which they bound Love to them? How did they hold the graceless elf? What elixir of eternal love had they drunk together as had Tristram and Iseult of old time? And whose hand had brewed the fairy drink?
“As I say, I was curious, and I watched them. They were love-mad. They lived in an unending revel of Love. They made a pomp and ceremonial of it. They saturated themselves in the art and poetry of Love. No, they were not neurotics. They were sane and healthy, and they were artists. But they had accomplished the impossible. They had achieved deathless desire.
“And I? I saw much of them and their everlasting miracle of Love. I puzzled and wondered, and then one day﻿—”
Carquinez broke off abruptly and asked, “Have you ever read, *Love’s Waiting Time*?”

===Chinese===
“人人称奇。他们成了那对奇妙的恋人，大受艳羡。有时女人们怜她没有孩子；这等造物的艳羡，就是这般模样。
“我也不知道他们的秘密。我思量，我称奇。起初，我想是下意识里吧，我等着他们的爱情消逝。后来我察觉，消逝的是时间，留下的是爱情。于是我起了好奇。他们的秘密是什么？他们用什么魔法镣铐把爱情锁在身边？他们怎样拿住那个没规矩的小妖精？他们一起喝的是什么永恒爱情的仙药，像古时的特里斯坦与伊索尔德？那仙酿又是谁的手调成的？
“正如我说过的，我起了好奇，便观察他们。他们爱得发狂。他们活在一场没有穷尽的爱情欢宴里。他们为它摆开仪典与盛饰。他们把自己浸饱在爱情的艺术与诗里。不，他们不是神经病人。他们神志清明、身体康健，而且是艺术家。可他们做成了一件不可能的事。他们得到了不死的欲望。
“而我呢？我常见他们，看他们那桩永不凋残的爱情奇迹。我困惑，我惊叹，后来有一天——”
卡基内斯陡然收住话头，问道：“你读过《爱的等待期》吗？”

===Original===
I shook my head.
“Page wrote it﻿—Curtis Hidden Page, I think. Well, it was that bit of verse that gave me the clue. One day, in the window-seat near the big piano﻿—you remember how she could play? She used to laugh, sometimes, and doubt whether it was for them I came, or for the music. She called me a ‘music-sot’ once, a ‘sound-debauchee.’ What a voice he had! When he sang I believed in immortality, my regard for the gods grew almost patronizing and I devised ways and means whereby I surely could outwit them and their tricks.
“It was a spectacle for God, that man and woman, years married, and singing love-songs with a freshness virginal as newborn Love himself, with a ripeness and wealth of ardour that young lovers can never know. Young lovers were pale and anaemic beside that long-married pair. To see them, all fire and flame and tenderness, at a trembling distance, lavishing caresses of eye and voice with every action, through every silence﻿—their love driving them toward each other, and they withholding like fluttering moths, each to the other a candle-flame, and revolving each about the other in the mad gyrations of an amazing orbit-flight! It seemed, in obedience to some great law of physics, more potent than gravitation and more subtle, that they must corporeally melt each into each there before my very eyes. Small wonder they were called the wonderful lovers.
“I have wandered. Now to the clue. One day in the window-seat I found a book of verse. It opened of itself, betraying long habit, to *Love’s Waiting Time*. The page was thumbed and limp with overhandling, and there I read:﻿—
> “ ‘So sweet it is to stand but just apart,
> To know each other better, and to keep
> The soft, delicious sense of two that touch﻿ ﻿…
> O love, not yet!﻿ ﻿… Sweet, let us keep our love
> Wrapped round with sacred mystery awhile,
> Waiting the secret of the coming years,
> That come not yet, not yet﻿ ﻿… sometime﻿ ﻿… not yet﻿ ﻿…
> Oh, yet a little while our love may grow!
> When it has blossomed it will haply die.
> Feed it with lipless kisses, let it sleep,
> Bedded in dead denial yet some while﻿ ﻿…
> Oh, yet a little while, a little while.’

===Chinese===
我摇摇头。
“佩奇写的——我想是柯蒂斯·希登·佩奇。喏，就是那几行诗给了我线索。有一天，在大钢琴旁的窗座上——还记得她弹得一手好琴么？她从前常笑，有时疑心我到底是为他们来的，还是为音乐来的。她有一回叫我‘音乐醉汉’，一个‘声音的荒宴客’。他那嗓子多好！他一唱，我便信了不朽，我对诸神的敬意几乎变得居高临下，我还盘算出种种办法，笃定能斗赢他们和他们的伎俩。
“那是给上帝看的一景：一男一女，成婚多年，唱起情歌来还带着新生的爱情本人那般童真的新鲜，又带着年轻的恋人们永远无从知晓的成熟与炽烈。在那对结婚多年的夫妇旁边，年轻恋人们显得苍白贫血。看他们俩，浑身是火、是焰、是温存，隔着一段颤巍巍的距离，一举一动、每一阵沉默，都挥霍着眼波与嗓音的爱抚——爱情驱赶他们彼此相就，他们却像扑闪的飞蛾般悬住，各是对方的一炬烛火，又各绕着对方旋转，转出那惊人轨道飞行的疯狂回旋！仿佛遵着某条伟大的物理定律——比引力更强、也更精微——他们必得当着我的面，肉体相融，你中有我我中有你。难怪人们称他们那对奇妙的恋人。
“我扯远了。回到线索。有一天，我在那窗座上找到一册诗集。书自己摊开在《爱的等待期》那一页——一望便知是翻惯了的。那一页叫人翻得又软又塌，我便读到了：——
> “ ‘只是这么稍稍分开站着，多么甜，
> 这样彼此相知得更深，又留住
> 那两个身体相触时柔软甘美的感觉……
> 爱啊，还不要！……亲爱的，且让我们的爱
> 再裹一层神圣的神秘一会儿，
> 等待那来日岁月的秘密，
> 岁月还不来，还不来……哪一天……还不到……
> 哦，再一小会儿我们的爱还能生长！
> 一旦开了花，它多半就要死了。
> 拿无唇的吻喂它，让它睡吧，
> 再卧在死寂的拒绝里一会儿……
> 哦，再一会儿，一小会儿。’

===Original===
“I folded the book on my thumb and sat there silent and without moving for a long time. I was stunned by the clearness of vision the verse had imparted to me. It was illumination. It was like a bolt of God’s lightning in the Pit. They would keep Love, the fickle sprite, the forerunner of young life﻿—young life that is imperative to be born!
“I conned the lines over in my mind﻿—‘Not yet, sometime’﻿—‘O Love, not yet’﻿—‘Feed it with lipless kisses, let it sleep.’ And I laughed aloud, ha, ha! I saw with white vision their blameless souls. They were children. They did not understand. They played with Nature’s fire and bedded with a naked sword. They laughed at the gods. They would stop the cosmic sap. They had invented a system, and brought it to the gaming-table of life, and expected to win out. ‘Beware!’ I cried. ‘The gods are behind the table. They make new rules for every system that is devised. You have no chance to win.’
“But I did not so cry to them. I waited. They would learn that their system was worthless and throw it away. They would be content with whatever happiness the gods gave them and not strive to wrest more away.
“I watched. I said nothing. The months continued to come and go, and still the famine-edge of their love grew the sharper. Never did they dull it with a permitted love-clasp. They ground and whetted it on self-denial, and sharper and sharper it grew. This went on until even I doubted. Did the gods sleep? I wondered. Or were they dead? I laughed to myself. The man and the woman had made a miracle. They had outwitted God. They had shamed the flesh, and blackened the face of the good Earth Mother. They had played with her fire and not been burned. They were immune. They were themselves gods, knowing good from evil and tasting not. ‘Was this the way gods came to be?’ I asked myself. ‘I am a frog,’ I said. ‘But for my mud-lidded eyes I should have been blinded by the brightness of this wonder I have witnessed. I have puffed myself up with my wisdom and passed judgment upon gods.’
“Yet even in this, my latest wisdom, I was wrong. They were not gods. They were man and woman﻿—soft clay that sighed and thrilled, shot through with desire, thumbed with strange weaknesses which the gods have not.”

===Chinese===
“我拇指抵着书页合上，坐在那里，久久沉默，一动不动。那几行诗授给我的清明眼光震得我发懵。那是照亮。像上帝的一记闪电劈进深坑。他们竟要留住爱情，那轻浮的小精灵，那新生生命的前驱——新生生命是断断乎非要诞生不可的！
“我在心里反复默诵那些句子——‘还不到，哪一天’——‘爱啊，还不要’——‘拿无唇的吻喂它，让它睡吧。’我放声笑了，哈，哈！我以清白的洞见看清他们无辜的灵魂。他们是孩子。他们不懂。他们玩着自然的火，同一柄出鞘的剑共枕。他们讥笑诸神。他们要让天地的汁液停流。他们发明了一套体系，捧到人生的赌桌上来，指望赢个通盘。‘当心！’我喊。‘诸神就在桌子后头。每有人想出一套体系，他们便为它另造新规。你们没有赢的机会。’
“可我并没有这样喊给他们听。我等着。他们自会明白那套体系一文不值，把它丢开。诸神给多少幸福，他们便安分领受多少，不再奋力强夺。
“我看着。我一言不发。月份来来去去，他们爱情的饥刃却愈磨愈利。他们从不曾拿一次获准的拥抱去钝它。他们拿自制磨它、砺它，它便一分一分地利起来。这样下去，连我也疑惑了。诸神睡熟了么？我纳闷。还是死掉了？我暗自好笑。这男人和这女人造出了奇迹。他们智胜了上帝。他们羞辱了肉体，抹黑了善良大地母亲的脸。他们玩了她的火而没有烧伤。他们免疫了。他们自己便是神，知善恶而不尝。‘神便是这样来的么？’我问自己。‘我是一只蛙，’我说。‘若不是我这一双泥封的眼，我早叫我亲眼所见的这道奇观亮瞎了。我拿自己那点智慧把自己吹胀起来，竟给诸神下起判词来。’
“然而连这最新的智慧，我也错了。他们不是神。他们是男人和女人——叹息着、战栗着的软泥，欲念贯通其间，还捏满了诸神所没有的种种古怪软弱。”

===Original===
Carquinez broke from his narrative to roll another cigarette and to laugh harshly. It was not a pretty laugh; it was like the mockery of a devil, and it rose over and rode the roar of the storm that came muffled to our ears from the crashing outside world.
“I am a frog,” he said apologetically. “How were they to understand? They were artists, not biologists. They knew the clay of the studio, but they did not know the clay of which they themselves were made. But this I will say﻿—they played high. Never was there such a game before, and I doubt me if there will ever be such a game again.
“Never was lovers’ ecstasy like theirs. They had not killed Love with kisses. They had quickened him with denial. And by denial they drove him on till he was all aburst with desire. And the flame-winged lute-player fanned them with his warm wings till they were all but swooning. It was the very delirium of Love, and it continued undiminished and increasing through the weeks and months.
“They longed and yearned, with all the fond pangs and sweet delicious agonies, with an intensity never felt by lovers before nor since.
“And then one day the drowsy gods ceased nodding. They aroused and looked at the man and woman who had made a mock of them. And the man and woman looked into each other’s eyes one morning and knew that something was gone. It was the flame-winged one. He had fled, silently, in the night, from their anchorites’ board.

===Chinese===
卡基内斯中断了叙述，又卷一支烟，粗声大笑。那笑声并不好听；像魔鬼的讥诮，它高扬起来，骑到外面崩裂的世界闷闷传进我们耳中的风暴咆哮上面。
“我是一只蛙，”他带着歉意说。“他们哪能懂？他们是艺术家，不是生物学家。他们认得雕塑室里的黏土，却不认得捏成他们自己的那把黏土。可这话我要说——他们押的是大注。从前没有那样的牌局，我疑心往后也再不会有。
“恋人的狂喜，从来没有像他们那样的。他们不曾用亲吻杀死爱情。他们用拒绝催活了他。又用拒绝驱他向前，驱到他浑身迸裂着欲望。那火焰翅膀的琴手便用温暖的双翅扇他们，扇得他们几乎晕厥。那正是爱情的谵狂，一周周、一月月地持续，不减，反增。
“他们渴慕，他们焦思，带着全部缠绵的刺痛与甜美的酷刑，那烈度是前前后后的恋人都不曾体味过的。
“后来有一天，打盹的诸神不再点头了。他们醒过来，望着那一对嘲弄了他们的男女。一天早晨，那男人和那女人彼此望进对方的眼睛，知道有什么东西没了。是那火焰翅膀的一位。他已经在夜里悄悄逃走，逃离了他们那苦行者的餐桌。

===Original===
“They looked into each other’s eyes and knew that they did not care. Desire was dead. Do you understand? Desire was dead. And they had never kissed. Not once had they kissed. Love was gone. They would never yearn and burn again. For them there was nothing left﻿—no more tremblings and flutterings and delicious anguishes, no more throbbing and pulsing, and sighing and song. Desire was dead. It had died in the night, on a couch cold and unattended; nor had they witnessed its passing. They learned it for the first time in each other’s eyes.
“The gods may not be kind, but they are often merciful. They had twirled the little ivory ball and swept the stakes from the table. All that remained was the man and woman gazing into each other’s cold eyes. And then he died. That was the mercy. Within the week Marvin Fiske was dead﻿—you remember the accident. And in her diary, written at this time, I long afterward read Mitchell Kennerly’s:﻿—
> “ ‘There was not a single hour
> We might have kissed and did not kiss.’ ”
“Oh, the irony of it!” I cried out.

===Chinese===
“他们彼此望进对方的眼睛，知道自己毫不在意了。欲望死了。你明白么？欲望死了。而他们从不曾吻过。一次也不曾吻过。爱情去了。他们再不会渴慕、再不会燃烧了。留给他们的什么也没有了——再没有战栗、悸动与甜美的苦楚，再没有搏动、突跳、叹息与歌。欲望死了。它夜里死在一张冰冷无人的榻上；他们也不曾目送它断气。他们头一回在彼此的眼睛里知道了这件事。
“诸神未必仁慈，却常常慈悲。他们捻转了那颗小小的象牙球，把桌上的彩头一扫而空。剩下的只有那男人和那女人，彼此凝望进对方冷淡的眼睛。随后他死了。那便是慈悲。不出一个星期，马文·菲斯克死了——你还记得那场意外。在她当时写下的日记里，很久以后我读到米切尔·肯纳利的句子：——
> “ ‘没有一个钟点
> 是我们本可以吻而没有吻的。’ ”
“哦，这真是捉弄人！”我喊出声来。

===Original===
And Carquinez, in the firelight a veritable Mephistopheles in velvet jacket, fixed me with his black eyes.
“And they won, you said? The world’s judgment! I have told you, and I know. They won as you are winning, here in your hills.”
“But you,” I demanded hotly; “you with your orgies of sound and sense, with your mad cities and madder frolics﻿—bethink you that you win?”
He shook his head slowly. “Because you with your sober bucolic regime, lose, is no reason that I should win. We never win. Sometimes we think we win. That is a little pleasantry of the gods.”

===Chinese===
卡基内斯在炉火光里，活脱是个穿丝绒上衣的梅菲斯托费勒斯，用他的黑眼睛定定盯住我。
“你说他们赢了？世人的判词！我已经讲给你听了，我知道底细。他们赢，正像你此刻在你这些山里赢的这样。”
“可你呢，”我激愤地质问；“你，声与色的荒宴不断的你，疯狂都市与更疯狂嬉戏的你——你以为你赢了？”
他缓缓摇头。“你们守着清醒的田园规程会输，并不等于我就该赢。我们从来不赢。有时我们自以为赢了。那是诸神的一点小玩笑。”

## The Apostate / 叛途

===Original===
> “Now I wake me up to work; I pray the Lord I may not shirk. If I should die before the night, I pray the Lord my work’s all right. Amen.”
“If you don’t git up, Johnny, I won’t give you a bite to eat!”
The threat had no effect on the boy. He clung stubbornly to sleep, fighting for its oblivion as the dreamer fights for his dream. The boy’s hands loosely clenched themselves, and he made feeble, spasmodic blows at the air. These blows were intended for his mother, but she betrayed practised familiarity in avoiding them as she shook him roughly by the shoulder.

===Chinese===
> “如今我醒身起来做工；求主保佑，别叫我躲懒贪闲。倘若不等入夜我便死了，求主保佑，我的活计样样做全。阿门。”
“再不起来，约翰尼，一口吃的也不给你！”
这威胁对男孩毫无效力。他死死抓住睡眠不放，像做梦的人捍卫自己的梦那样，为那一方忘却而搏斗。男孩的双手松松地攥起，朝空中虚打出几下软弱而痉挛的拳。这几拳本是冲他母亲去的，可她粗鲁地摇他肩膀的时候，闪避得熟练极了，一看便知是惯了的。

===Original===
“Lemme ’lone!”
It was a cry that began, muffled, in the deeps of sleep, that swiftly rushed upward, like a wail, into passionate belligerence, and that died away and sank down into an inarticulate whine. It was a bestial cry, as of a soul in torment, filled with infinite protest and pain.
But she did not mind. She was a sad-eyed, tired-faced woman, and she had grown used to this task, which she repeated every day of her life. She got a grip on the bedclothes and tried to strip them down; but the boy, ceasing his punching, clung to them desperately. In a huddle, at the foot of the bed, he still remained covered. Then she tried dragging the bedding to the floor. The boy opposed her. She braced herself. Hers was the superior weight, and the boy and bedding gave, the former instinctively following the latter in order to shelter against the chill of the room that bit into his body.

===Chinese===
“别碰我！”
这一声喊，起头闷在睡眠的深处，随即像一声哀号迅猛上冲，冲成激愤的敌意，随后又衰减、沉落，化作一阵含混的呜咽。这是一声兽性的喊叫，仿佛一个受折磨的灵魂，充满了无尽的抗议与痛苦。
可她并不在意。她是个眼含悲苦、一脸倦容的女人，这桩差事她天天要做，早已做惯了。她一把揪住被褥，想往下剥；男孩不再挥拳，拼命抱住不放。他缩作一团，赖在床脚，身上仍旧盖得严严实实。于是她改把铺盖往地板上拖。男孩死顶。她扎稳了身子。论分量她占上风，男孩和铺盖终于让了步，前者出于本能跟着后者走，好躲一躲屋里那咬进他身体的寒气。

===Original===
As he toppled on the edge of the bed it seemed that he must fall headfirst to the floor. But consciousness fluttered up in him. He righted himself and for a moment perilously balanced. Then he struck the floor on his feet. On the instant his mother seized him by the shoulders and shook him. Again his fists struck out, this time with more force and directness. At the same time his eyes opened. She released him. He was awake.
“All right,” he mumbled.
She caught up the lamp and hurried out, leaving him in darkness.

===Chinese===
他从床沿上栽下去时，眼看就要一头栽到地板上。可意识在他体内扑闪了一下。他挺住了身子，有一刹那危险地晃着找平衡。随后他双脚落到了地板上。母亲当即抓住他的双肩摇晃。他的拳头又打了出去，这一回更有力，也更直截。与此同时，他睁开了眼睛。她松开手。他醒了。
“好啦，”他咕哝道。
她抓起灯，匆匆出去，把他撂在黑暗里。

===Original===
“You’ll be docked,” she warned back to him.
He did not mind the darkness. When he had got into his clothes, he went out into the kitchen. His tread was very heavy for so thin and light a boy. His legs dragged with their own weight, which seemed unreasonable because they were such skinny legs. He drew a broken-bottomed chair to the table.
“Johnny,” his mother called sharply.

===Chinese===
“你要被扣工钱的，”她回头警告他。
黑暗他不介意。穿好衣服，他便走进厨房。这么一个又瘦又轻的男孩，脚步却沉得很。两条腿拖着自己的分量挪动，这似乎说不通，因为那是两条多细的腿啊。他拖过一把破了底的椅子，凑到桌边。
“约翰尼，”母亲厉声喊他。

===Original===
He arose as sharply from the chair, and, without a word, went to the sink. It was a greasy, filthy sink. A smell came up from the outlet. He took no notice of it. That a sink should smell was to him part of the natural order, just as it was a part of the natural order that the soap should be grimy with dishwater and hard to lather. Nor did he try very hard to make it lather. Several splashes of the cold water from the running faucet completed the function. He did not wash his teeth. For that matter he had never seen a toothbrush, nor did he know that there existed beings in the world who were guilty of so great a foolishness as tooth washing.
“You might wash yourself wunst a day without bein’ told,” his mother complained.
She was holding a broken lid on the pot as she poured two cups of coffee. He made no remark, for this was a standing quarrel between them, and the one thing upon which his mother was hard as adamant. “Wunst” a day it was compulsory that he should wash his face. He dried himself on a greasy towel, damp and dirty and ragged, that left his face covered with shreds of lint.

===Chinese===
他也同样利落地从椅子上起身，一言不发，走到水槽跟前。那是个油腻肮脏的水槽。一股气味从下水口冒上来。他毫不在意。水槽有味儿，在他看来本属自然秩序，正如肥皂沾满洗碗水的污垢、搓不起泡沫，也属自然秩序。他也并不真下力气去搓。水龙头里放出的冷水泼上几下，这项功能便告完成。他不刷牙。说起来，他这辈子连牙刷都没见过，也不知道世上竟存在这样一些人，犯得下刷牙这般天大的傻事。
“你满可以一天自己洗一回脸，不用人催，”母亲抱怨道。
她按着锅上一只裂了缝的盖子，倒出两杯咖啡。他不吭声，因为这是他们母子间一场常年的口角，也是他母亲寸步不让、坚如金刚石的一件事：一天里必须“洗上一回”脸，是强制执行的。他用一条油腻的毛巾擦干自己，那毛巾又潮又脏又破，擦完，他脸上粘着一丝丝棉绒。

===Original===
“I wish we didn’t live so far away,” she said, as he sat down. “I try to do the best I can. You know that. But a dollar on the rent is such a savin’, an’ we’ve more room here. You know that.”
He scarcely followed her. He had heard it all before, many times. The range of her thought was limited, and she was ever harking back to the hardship worked upon them by living so far from the mills.
“A dollar means more grub,” he remarked sententiously. “I’d sooner do the walkin’ an’ git the grub.”

===Chinese===
“我巴不得咱们不住这么远，”他坐下时她说。“我尽力把日子往好里过，这你知道。可房租上省下一块钱是多大的进项，这儿屋子又宽敞些。这你也知道。”
他几乎没有听。这些话他从前全听过，听过许多遍了。她思想的路数有限，总是翻来覆去，数说住得离工厂太远给他们带来的苦处。
“一块钱就是多点嚼谷，”他一副格言式的口气。“我宁可多走路，换那口吃的。”

===Original===
He ate hurriedly, half chewing the bread and washing the unmasticated chunks down with coffee. The hot and muddy liquid went by the name of coffee. Johnny thought it was coffee﻿—and excellent coffee. That was one of the few of life’s illusions that remained to him. He had never drunk real coffee in his life.
In addition to the bread, there was a small piece of cold pork. His mother refilled his cup with coffee. As he was finishing the bread, he began to watch if more was forthcoming. She intercepted his questioning glance.
“Now, don’t be hoggish, Johnny,” was her comment. “You’ve had your share. Your brothers an’ sisters are smaller’n you.”

===Chinese===
他吃得匆忙，面包嚼到一半，便用咖啡把没嚼烂的大块冲下肚去。这热腾腾、泥汤似的液体，名头上叫作咖啡。约翰尼当它就是咖啡——还是上好的咖啡。这是他人生残存的几样幻觉之一。他这辈子从没喝过真正的咖啡。
面包之外，还有一小块冷猪肉。母亲又给他续上一杯咖啡。面包快吃完时，他开始留意还有没有后继。她截住了他那一记探问的目光。
“喂，别贪嘴，约翰尼，”她这样评点。“你那份有了。你弟弟妹妹可比你小。”

===Original===
He did not answer the rebuke. He was not much of a talker. Also, he ceased his hungry glancing for more. He was uncomplaining, with a patience that was as terrible as the school in which it had been learned. He finished his coffee, wiped his mouth on the back of his hand, and started to rise.
“Wait a second,” she said hastily. “I guess the loaf kin stand you another slice﻿—a thin ’un.”
There was legerdemain in her actions. With all the seeming of cutting a slice from the loaf for him, she put loaf and slice back in the bread box and conveyed to him one of her own two slices. She believed she had deceived him, but he had noted her sleight-of-hand. Nevertheless, he took the bread shamelessly. He had a philosophy that his mother, because of her chronic sickliness, was not much of an eater anyway.

===Chinese===
他没有应这顿训斥。他本不是个多话的人。于是也不再饿着眼瞟第二份了。他不抱怨，那份耐心同教会他这份耐心的学校一样可怕。他喝完咖啡，用手背抹了抹嘴，起身要走。
“等一等，”她慌忙说。“我寻思这面包还经得起你再切一片——切薄点儿的一片。”
她的动作里藏着戏法。满像是给他从面包上切下一片的样子，她却把面包连那一片放回了面包箱，把自己两片里的一片递给了他。她自以为骗过了他，可他早看破了她的手法。即便如此，他仍厚着脸皮接了面包。他有一套哲学：母亲常年多病，反正也吃不下多少。

===Original===
She saw that he was chewing the bread dry, and reached over and emptied her coffee cup into his.
“Don’t set good somehow on my stomach this morning,” she explained.
A distant whistle, prolonged and shrieking, brought both of them to their feet. She glanced at the tin alarm-clock on the shelf. The hands stood at half-past five. The rest of the factory world was just arousing from sleep. She drew a shawl about her shoulders, and on her head put a dingy hat, shapeless and ancient.

===Chinese===
她见他干嚼面包，便探过身来，把自己那杯咖啡尽数倒进了他杯里。
“今儿早上不知怎么，这东西搁在胃里不落位，”她解释道。
远处一声汽笛，悠长而尖厉，把两人一齐拽了起来。她瞟了一眼架子上的马口铁闹钟。指针指着五点半。工厂世界里其余的人也正从睡梦中醒来。她往肩上裹了条披巾，头上扣了一顶灰扑扑的帽子，走了形的老古董。

===Original===
“We’ve got to run,” she said, turning the wick of the lamp and blowing down the chimney.
They groped their way out and down the stairs. It was clear and cold, and Johnny shivered at the first contact with the outside air. The stars had not yet begun to pale in the sky, and the city lay in blackness. Both Johnny and his mother shuffled their feet as they walked. There was no ambition in the leg muscles to swing the feet clear of the ground.
After fifteen silent minutes, his mother turned off to the right.

===Chinese===
“咱们得跑着去了，”她说着，把灯芯拧低，俯身顺灯罩往下吹熄了灯。
两人摸着黑出门，摸下楼。天清而冷，约翰尼一沾外面的空气便打了个寒噤。星星还没有开始在天上褪色，整座城黑沉沉地躺着。约翰尼和母亲走路都趿拉着脚。腿上的肌肉没有一点雄心，不肯把脚抬离地面。
默默走了十五分钟，母亲朝右边拐去了。

===Original===
“Don’t be late,” was her final warning from out of the dark that was swallowing her up.
He made no response, steadily keeping on his way. In the factory quarter, doors were opening everywhere, and he was soon one of a multitude that pressed onward through the dark. As he entered the factory gate the whistle blew again. He glanced at the east. Across a ragged skyline of housetops a pale light was beginning to creep. This much he saw of the day as he turned his back upon it and joined his work gang.
He took his place in one of many long rows of machines. Before him, above a bin filled with small bobbins, were large bobbins revolving rapidly. Upon these he wound the jute-twine of the small bobbins. The work was simple. All that was required was celerity. The small bobbins were emptied so rapidly, and there were so many large bobbins that did the emptying, that there were no idle moments.

===Chinese===
“别迟了，”黑暗正把她吞没，她丢下这最后一句告诫。
他没有应声，只管稳稳走自己的路。到了工厂区，家家的门正四处洞开，他很快汇入一大群在黑暗里向前涌去的人。他跨进厂门时，汽笛又响了。他朝东望了一眼。参差的屋脊线那头，一线灰白的光正开始爬动。他背过身去，加入他的工班——白天，他所见的就只有这么多。
他在一长列一长列机器中的一列里占了自己的位置。他面前，一箱小纱管的上方，是一只只急速回旋的大纱管。他把小纱管上的黄麻线绕到这些大纱管上。活儿简单。唯一的要求是快。小纱管空得飞快，负责倒空它们的大纱管又那么多，根本没有闲着的空当。

===Original===
He worked mechanically. When a small bobbin ran out, he used his left hand for a brake, stopping the large bobbin and at the same time, with thumb and forefinger, catching the flying end of twine. Also, at the same time, with his right hand, he caught up the loose twine-end of a small bobbin. These various acts with both hands were performed simultaneously and swiftly. Then there would come a flash of his hands as he looped the weaver’s knot and released the bobbin. There was nothing difficult about weaver’s knots. He once boasted he could tie them in his sleep. And for that matter, he sometimes did, toiling centuries long in a single night at tying an endless succession of weaver’s knots.
Some of the boys shirked, wasting time and machinery by not replacing the small bobbins when they ran out. And there was an overseer to prevent this. He caught Johnny’s neighbour at the trick, and boxed his ears.
“Look at Johnny there﻿—why ain’t you like him?” the overseer wrathfully demanded.

===Chinese===
他机械地干活。一只小纱管跑空了，他便拿左手权当刹车，煞住大纱管，同时用拇指和食指掐住乱飞的线头；同一瞬间，右手又捻起一只小纱管上松脱的线尾。这双手各自的几套动作，同时完成，快得很。接着只见双手一闪，他打好一个织工结，放开了纱管。织工结没有什么难的。他一度夸口，睡着了也打得来。说起来，他也真有过这样的时候——一夜之间熬上几个世纪，打着一连串没完没了的织工结。
有些男孩偷懒，小纱管跑空了也不换新的，白白耗费着时间和机器。于是设了一个监工来防这个。他当场拿住了约翰尼邻座耍的这套把戏，掴了他两记耳光。
“瞧瞧那边的约翰尼——你怎么就不能跟他一样？”监工怒气冲冲地喝问。

===Original===
Johnny’s bobbins were running full blast, but he did not thrill at the indirect praise. There had been a time﻿ ﻿… but that was long ago, very long ago. His apathetic face was expressionless as he listened to himself being held up as a shining example. He was the perfect worker. He knew that. He had been told so, often. It was a commonplace, and besides it didn’t seem to mean anything to him any more. From the perfect worker he had evolved into the perfect machine. When his work went wrong, it was with him as with the machine, due to faulty material. It would have been as possible for a perfect nail-die to cut imperfect nails as for him to make a mistake.
And small wonder. There had never been a time when he had not been in intimate relationship with machines. Machinery had almost been bred into him, and at any rate he had been brought up on it. Twelve years before, there had been a small flutter of excitement in the loom room of this very mill. Johnny’s mother had fainted. They stretched her out on the floor in the midst of the shrieking machines. A couple of elderly women were called from their looms. The foreman assisted. And in a few minutes there was one more soul in the loom room than had entered by the doors. It was Johnny, born with the pounding, crashing roar of the looms in his ears, drawing with his first breath the warm, moist air that was thick with flying lint. He had coughed that first day in order to rid his lungs of the lint; and for the same reason he had coughed ever since.
The boy alongside of Johnny whimpered and sniffed. The boy’s face was convulsed with hatred for the overseer who kept a threatening eye on him from a distance; but every bobbin was running full. The boy yelled terrible oaths into the whirling bobbins before him; but the sound did not carry half a dozen feet, the roaring of the room holding it in and containing it like a wall.

===Chinese===
约翰尼的纱管正开足了转，可这间接的夸奖没能让他心头一热。从前也有过那么一个时候……可那是很久以前了，很久很久以前。他冷漠的脸毫无表情，听着人家把自己树成光灿灿的榜样。他是完美的工人。这他知道。人家常这么对他说。这已成老生常谈，再说，这话在他听来也已经全无所谓了。从完美的工人，他已经进化成了完美的机器。活儿出了岔子，问题也同机器一样，出在材料不佳。要他出错，就跟一只完美的制钉模子铸出歪钉子一样，是办不到的。
这也难怪。从来没有一个时候，他不曾同机器亲密相处。机器差不多已经融进了他的血统，退一步说，他也是机器给带大的。十二年前，正是这座厂的织布车间里起过一小阵骚动。约翰尼的母亲晕倒了。人们把她平放在地板上，四周是尖啸的机器。两个上了年纪的妇女从织机旁被叫了过来。领班也搭了把手。几分钟后，织布车间里多出了一个不是打门里进来的人。那就是约翰尼，落地时耳中便是织机撞击轰砸的咆哮，第一口气吸进去的，便是饱含飞絮的温热潮气。头一天他就咳嗽，为的是把肺里的飞絮赶出去；打那以后，他也一直为同一个缘故咳嗽。
约翰尼旁边的男孩抽抽噎噎，吸着鼻子。男孩的脸因恨那监工而扭曲——监工正远远拿眼睛威吓着他；可他名下的纱管只只满转。男孩对着眼前乱转的纱管破口大骂，咒誓狠极；可那声音传不出六尺远，车间的轰鸣把它围住、关住，像一堵墙。

===Original===
Of all this Johnny took no notice. He had a way of accepting things. Besides, things grow monotonous by repetition, and this particular happening he had witnessed many times. It seemed to him as useless to oppose the overseer as to defy the will of a machine. Machines were made to go in certain ways and to perform certain tasks. It was the same with the overseer.
But at eleven o’clock there was excitement in the room. In an apparently occult way the excitement instantly permeated everywhere. The one-legged boy who worked on the other side of Johnny bobbed swiftly across the floor to a bin truck that stood empty. Into this he dived out of sight, crutch and all. The superintendent of the mill was coming along, accompanied by a young man. He was well dressed and wore a starched shirt﻿—a gentleman, in Johnny’s classification of men, and also, “the Inspector.”
He looked sharply at the boys as he passed along. Sometimes he stopped and asked questions. When he did so, he was compelled to shout at the top of his lungs, at which moments his face was ludicrously contorted with the strain of making himself heard. His quick eye noted the empty machine alongside of Johnny’s, but he said nothing. Johnny also caught his eye, and he stopped abruptly. He caught Johnny by the arm to draw him back a step from the machine; but with an exclamation of surprise he released the arm.

===Chinese===
这一切约翰尼全不理会。他有他的一套：凡事照单全收。再说，事情一重复便单调了，眼前这一出他见过不知多少回。在他看来，对抗监工，同违抗一台机器的意志一样没用。机器造出来，就是要按一定的路子走、干一定的活计。监工也一样。
可十一点钟，车间里起了一阵骚动。这骚动以某种显然玄妙的方式，眨眼间渗透到了四面八方。在约翰尼另一侧干活的独腿男孩，一颠一颠飞快地横穿地板，钻到一辆空着的运纱车前，连人带拐杖一头扎了进去，没了影。厂长正走过来，身边跟着一位年轻人。那人衣着体面，穿一件浆硬的衬衫——照约翰尼给世人分的那一类，是位绅士，而且还是“视察员”。
他一路走过，锐利地打量着孩子们。有时他停下脚步问几句话。每逢这样，他就不得不扯开嗓门拼命喊，那一刻，他的脸因拼命让人听见的吃力而扭曲得滑稽。他敏锐的眼睛注意到了约翰尼旁边那台空着的机器，却什么也没说。约翰尼也落进了他的眼里，他猛地站住。他抓住约翰尼的胳膊，想把他从机器跟前拉后一步；可一声惊呼，又把胳膊松开了。

===Original===
“Pretty skinny,” the superintendent laughed anxiously.
“Pipe stems,” was the answer. “Look at those legs. The boy’s got the rickets﻿—incipient, but he’s got them. If epilepsy doesn’t get him in the end, it will be because tuberculosis gets him first.”
Johnny listened, but did not understand. Furthermore he was not interested in future ills. There was an immediate and more serious ill that threatened him in the form of the inspector.
“Now, my boy, I want you to tell me the truth,” the inspector said, or shouted, bending close to the boy’s ear to make him hear. “How old are you?”
“Fourteen,” Johnny lied, and he lied with the full force of his lungs. So loudly did he lie that it started him off in a dry, hacking cough that lifted the lint which had been settling in his lungs all morning.
“Looks sixteen at least,” said the superintendent.

===Chinese===
“瘦得够呛，”厂长赔着笑，心里发急。
“烟斗杆儿似的，”对方回答。“瞧那两条腿。这孩子有佝偻病——才起头，可总是有了。到头来要不是癫痫要他的命，就是肺结核先把他带走。”
约翰尼听着，却听不懂。再说他也不关心将来的病痛。眼下就有一桩更急更重的祸事正威胁着他，那祸事的形状，便是视察员。
“喂，孩子，我要你对我说实话，”视察员说，或者说是喊，凑到男孩耳边好让他听见。“你多大了？”
“十四，”约翰尼撒了个谎，而且是拿出肺部的全部力气来撒这个谎。谎撒得那样响，当场勾起他一阵干呛，把一上午沉在他肺里的飞絮都呛了上来。
“看着起码十六，”厂长说。

===Original===
“Or sixty,” snapped the inspector.
“He’s always looked that way.”
“How long?” asked the inspector, quickly.
“For years. Never gets a bit older.”
“Or younger, I dare say. I suppose he’s worked here all those years?”
“Off and on﻿—but that was before the new law was passed,” the superintendent hastened to add.

===Chinese===
“要不就是六十，”视察员厉声道。
“他一向就这副模样。”
“多久了？”视察员紧着问。
“好些年了。一点不见老。”
“也不见小，我料着是。这些年他一直在你这儿做工？”
“断断续续——不过那都是新法令颁布以前的事了，”厂长赶忙补上一句。

===Original===
“Machine idle?” the inspector asked, pointing at the unoccupied machine beside Johnny’s, in which the part-filled bobbins were flying like mad.
“Looks that way.” The superintendent motioned the overseer to him and shouted in his ear and pointed at the machine. “Machine’s idle,” he reported back to the inspector.
They passed on, and Johnny returned to his work, relieved in that the ill had been averted. But the one-legged boy was not so fortunate. The sharp-eyed inspector haled him out at arms length from the bin truck. His lips were quivering, and his face had all the expression of one upon whom was fallen profound and irremediable disaster. The overseer looked astounded, as though for the first time he had laid eyes on the boy, while the superintendent’s face expressed shock and displeasure.

===Chinese===
“这台机器闲置着？”视察员问，指着约翰尼旁边那台无人看管的机器——机器里绕了一半的纱管正疯了一样飞转。
“像是这么回事。”厂长把监工招到跟前，对着他耳朵喊了几句，又指了指那机器。“机器闲着，”他回头向视察员汇报。
他们走过去了，约翰尼回到活计上，祸事绕开了他，他松了口气。可那独腿男孩没有这份运气。眼尖的视察员一把将他提出运纱车，直挺挺伸出胳膊拎出老远。他嘴唇哆嗦着，一张脸上的神情，全像一个天塌地陷、无可挽回的人。监工显出满脸惊愕，好像头一回瞧见这男孩似的，厂长的脸上则写着震惊与不快。

===Original===
“I know him,” the inspector said. “He’s twelve years old. I’ve had him discharged from three factories inside the year. This makes the fourth.”
He turned to the one-legged boy. “You promised me, word and honour, that you’d go to school.”
The one-legged boy burst into tears. “Please, Mr. Inspector, two babies died on us, and we’re awful poor.”
“What makes you cough that way?” the inspector demanded, as though charging him with crime.
And as in denial of guilt, the one-legged boy replied: “It ain’t nothin’. I jes’ caught a cold last week, Mr. Inspector, that’s all.”
In the end the one-legged boy went out of the room with the inspector, the latter accompanied by the anxious and protesting superintendent. After that monotony settled down again. The long morning and the longer afternoon wore away and the whistle blew for quitting time. Darkness had already fallen when Johnny passed out through the factory gate. In the interval the sun had made a golden ladder of the sky, flooded the world with its gracious warmth, and dropped down and disappeared in the west behind a ragged skyline of housetops.

===Chinese===
“我认得他，”视察员说。“他十二岁。今年以来，我已经让他从三家厂里被辞退了。这是第四家。”
他转向独腿男孩。“你应承过我，拿话和名誉担保，说你一定去上学。”
独腿男孩放声大哭。“求求你，视察员先生，家里死了两个娃娃，我们穷得凶。”
“你怎么咳成这样？”视察员质问，仿佛在控他一桩罪。
独腿男孩像是拒不认罪似的，答道：“没大事。上个礼拜着了点凉，视察员先生，就这么回事。”
到头来，独腿男孩跟着视察员走出了车间，视察员身边还跟着那位焦急不安、一路告罪的厂长。此后单调又重新落定。漫长的上午和更漫长的下午磨了过去，下班的汽笛响了。约翰尼走出厂门时，天已经黑透。在这段空当里，太阳在天上搭起了一道金梯，把它温厚的暖意泼满了世界，然后落下去，消失在西边参差的屋脊线后头。

===Original===
Supper was the family meal of the day﻿—the one meal at which Johnny encountered his younger brothers and sisters. It partook of the nature of an encounter, to him, for he was very old, while they were distressingly young. He had no patience with their excessive and amazing juvenility. He did not understand it. His own childhood was too far behind him. He was like an old and irritable man, annoyed by the turbulence of their young spirits that was to him arrant silliness. He glowered silently over his food, finding compensation in the thought that they would soon have to go to work. That would take the edge off of them and make them sedate and dignified﻿—like him. Thus it was, after the fashion of the human, that Johnny made of himself a yardstick with which to measure the universe.
During the meal, his mother explained in various ways and with infinite repetition that she was trying to do the best she could; so that it was with relief, the scant meal ended, that Johnny shoved back his chair and arose. He debated for a moment between bed and the front door, and finally went out the latter. He did not go far. He sat down on the stoop, his knees drawn up and his narrow shoulders drooping forward, his elbows on his knees and the palms of his hands supporting his chin.
As he sat there, he did no thinking. He was just resting. So far as his mind was concerned, it was asleep. His brothers and sisters came out, and with other children played noisily about him. An electric globe at the corner lighted their frolics. He was peevish and irritable, that they knew; but the spirit of adventure lured them into teasing him. They joined hands before him, and, keeping time with their bodies, chanted in his face weird and uncomplimentary doggerel. At first he snarled curses at them﻿—curses he had learned from the lips of various foremen. Finding this futile, and remembering his dignity, he relapsed into dogged silence.

===Chinese===
晚饭是一天里的全家饭——也是约翰尼同弟弟妹妹们打照面的唯一一顿。在他看来，这照面颇有一场遭遇战的性质，因为他已经很老很老了，而他们却小得叫人发愁。他们那股过了头的、惊人的孩子气，他一点耐性也没有。他不懂。他自己的童年已经退到太远的地方去了。他活像一个上了年纪的坏脾气老头，被那班小灵魂的闹腾惹恼——在他看来那纯粹是犯傻。他闷着头、沉着脸吃饭，一想到他们很快也得去干活，便觉得算得了补偿。干活会磨掉他们的锋芒，叫他们稳重体面——像他一样。人就是这么个路数，约翰尼便拿自己做成了一根尺，用来丈量宇宙。
吃饭中间，母亲翻来覆去、变着法子、无限重复地解释，她正尽力把日子往好里过；于是这顿寒素的饭一完，约翰尼推开椅子站起身，倒觉得松了口气。他在床和前门之间掂量了一会，终于选了后一样走了出去。他没走远。他在门前台阶上坐下，膝盖拢起，瘦窄的肩膀向前塌着，胳膊肘支在膝上，两只手掌托住下巴。
他坐在那里，什么也不想。他只是歇着。就他的头脑而言，它睡着了。弟弟妹妹们出来了，同别的孩子在他周围吵吵闹闹地玩。街角一盏电灯照亮他们的嬉闹。他脾气坏、沾火就着，这他们知道；可冒险精神引逗他们去撩他。他们在他面前手拉起手，身子踩着拍子，冲着他的脸唱起古怪又不中听的顺口溜。起先他咬牙切齿地咒骂——那些咒骂，是他从各个领班嘴上学来的。见骂也不顶用，又记起自己的体面，他便沉入倔强的沉默里。

===Original===
His brother Will, next to him in age, having just passed his tenth birthday, was the ringleader. Johnny did not possess particularly kindly feelings toward him. His life had early been embittered by continual giving over and giving way to Will. He had a definite feeling that Will was greatly in his debt and was ungrateful about it. In his own playtime, far back in the dim past, he had been robbed of a large part of that playtime by being compelled to take care of Will. Will was a baby then, and then, as now, their mother had spent her days in the mills. To Johnny had fallen the part of little father and little mother as well.
Will seemed to show the benefit of the giving over and the giving way. He was well-built, fairly rugged, as tall as his elder brother and even heavier. It was as though the lifeblood of the one had been diverted into the other’s veins. And in spirits it was the same. Johnny was jaded, worn out, without resilience, while his younger brother seemed bursting and spilling over with exuberance.
The mocking chant rose louder and louder. Will leaned closer as he danced, thrusting out his tongue. Johnny’s left arm shot out and caught the other around the neck. At the same time he rapped his bony fist to the other’s nose. It was a pathetically bony fist, but that it was sharp to hurt was evidenced by the squeal of pain it produced. The other children were uttering frightened cries, while Johnny’s sister, Jennie, had dashed into the house.

===Chinese===
他的弟弟威尔，年纪仅次于他，刚过完十岁生日，正是领头的。约翰尼对他谈不上有什么特别的友爱。他这一生很早便被没完没了的对威尔的让出与退让弄得心里发苦。他有一种明确的感受：威尔欠了他天大的人情，还不领情。在他自己的玩耍年月——那早已是渺茫的往昔了——他的玩耍时间被夺去了好大一块，就因为不得不看护威尔。那时威尔还是个吃奶的娃娃，而那时同现在一样，他们的母亲整天耗在厂里。小父亲兼小母亲的角色，便落到了约翰尼头上。
威尔倒像是把那些让出与退让的好处，全收到自己身上了。他筋骨匀称，相当结实，个子同哥哥一般高，分量还更重。仿佛一个人的生命之血，被引去了另一个人的血管。精神上也是如此。约翰尼疲惫干瘪、毫无弹性，而弟弟却仿佛浑身涨满了勃勃的生气，直往外溢。
嘲弄的顺口溜越唱越响。威尔一边跳一边凑近，伸出舌头来。约翰尼左臂猛地探出，勾住对方的脖子。同一瞬间，他骨头棱棱的拳头敲在对方的鼻子上。那拳头瘦骨嶙峋得可怜，可它锋利、伤人，一声痛得发出的尖叫便是明证。别的孩子纷纷惊叫起来，约翰尼的妹妹珍妮则一头冲进了屋子。

===Original===
He thrust Will from him, kicked him savagely on the shins, then reached for him and slammed him face downward in the dirt. Nor did he release him till the face had been rubbed into the dirt several times. Then the mother arrived, an anaemic whirlwind of solicitude and maternal wrath.
“Why can’t he leave me alone?” was Johnny’s reply to her upbraiding. “Can’t he see I’m tired?”
“I’m as big as you,” Will raged in her arms, his face a mass of tears, dirt, and blood. “I’m as big as you now, an’ I’m goin’ to git bigger. Then I’ll lick you﻿—see if I don’t.”

===Chinese===
他把威尔一搡，狠狠踢他的小腿，跟着又一把揪住，把他脸朝下掼进土里。那张脸在土里来回蹭了好几遍，他才撒手。这时母亲赶到了——一阵贫血的旋风，裹着牵挂与母性的怒火。
“他怎么就不能放我清静？”对她的责骂，约翰尼这样回。“他看不见我累吗？”
“我跟你一般大了，”威尔在她怀里撒泼，一张脸淌满了眼泪、泥土和血。“我现在就跟你一般大，我还要长得更大。到时候我揍你——瞧我揍不揍。”

===Original===
“You ought to be to work, seein’ how big you are,” Johnny snarled. “That’s what’s the matter with you. You ought to be to work. An’ it’s up to your ma to put you to work.”
“But he’s too young,” she protested. “He’s only a little boy.”
“I was younger’n him when I started to work.”

===Chinese===
“瞧你这个子，就该去做工，”约翰尼吼道。“你的毛病就出在这儿。你就该去做工。送你去做工，是你妈分内的事。”
“可他年纪还小，”她分辩道。“他还只是个小娃娃。”
“我起头做工的时候，比他还小。”

===Original===
Johnny’s mouth was open, further to express the sense of unfairness that he felt, but the mouth closed with a snap. He turned gloomily on his heel and stalked into the house and to bed. The door of his room was open to let in warmth from the kitchen. As he undressed in the semidarkness he could hear his mother talking with a neighbour woman who had dropped in. His mother was crying, and her speech was punctuated with spiritless sniffles.
“I can’t make out what’s gittin’ into Johnny,” he could hear her say. “He didn’t used to be this way. He was a patient little angel.
“An’ he is a good boy,” she hastened to defend. “He’s worked faithful, an’ he did go to work too young. But it wasn’t my fault. I do the best I can, I’m sure.”

===Chinese===
约翰尼张着嘴，还想再诉一诉他心里那股委屈，可嘴啪地一下闭上了。他阴沉沉地转身，大步进屋，上床睡了。他房门敞着，好放进厨房的暖气。他在半明半暗里脱衣服，听得见母亲在同一位串门的邻家女人说话。母亲哭着，话里夹着一声声没精打采的抽鼻子。
“我闹不明白约翰尼这是中了什么邪，”他听得她说。“他从前不这样的。他是个有耐心的小天使来着。
“他也是个好孩子，”她赶忙辩白。“做工做得尽心尽力，也实在是太小就上工了。可那不是我的错。我是尽力往好里过的，真的。”

===Original===
Prolonged sniffling from the kitchen, and Johnny murmured to himself as his eyelids closed down, “You betcher life I’ve worked faithful.”
The next morning he was torn bodily by his mother from the grip of sleep. Then came the meagre breakfast, the tramp through the dark, and the pale glimpse of day across the housetops as he turned his back on it and went in through the factory gate. It was another day, of all the days, and all the days were alike.
And yet there had been variety in his life﻿—at the times he changed from one job to another, or was taken sick. When he was six, he was little mother and father to Will and the other children still younger. At seven he went into the mills﻿—winding bobbins. When he was eight, he got work in another mill. His new job was marvellously easy. All he had to do was to sit down with a little stick in his hand and guide a stream of cloth that flowed past him. This stream of cloth came out of the maw of a machine, passed over a hot roller, and went on its way elsewhere. But he sat always in one place, beyond the reach of daylight, a gas-jet flaring over him, himself part of the mechanism.

===Chinese===
厨房里传来长长一段抽鼻子声，约翰尼的眼皮合下来，喃喃自语道：“这还用说，我做工是尽心尽力的。”
第二天早晨，他又被母亲从睡眠的掌握里整个儿撕了出来。接着是菲薄的早饭，摸黑的长路，以及背过身去走进厂门时、越过屋脊瞥见的那一线惨白的天光。又是一天，混在所有的日子里，而所有的日子全都一样。
然而他这一生里也有过变化——在他换活儿的时候，或是生病的时候。六岁上，他给威尔和更小的孩子们当小妈小爸。七岁他进了纺织厂——绕纱管。八岁上，他在另一家厂寻到了活儿。新活儿轻松得了不得。他只需拿一根小棍儿坐下，引导一股打他身边流过的布。这股布从一台机器的巨口里吐出来，掠过一根热滚筒，再往别处赶它的路。可他永远坐在一个地方，够不着一线天光，头顶上一盏煤气灯嘶嘶地烧着，他本人便是这机械的一部分。

===Original===
He was very happy at that job, in spite of the moist heat, for he was still young and in possession of dreams and illusions. And wonderful dreams he dreamed as he watched the steaming cloth streaming endlessly by. But there was no exercise about the work, no call upon his mind, and he dreamed less and less, while his mind grew torpid and drowsy. Nevertheless, he earned two dollars a week, and two dollars represented the difference between acute starvation and chronic underfeeding.
But when he was nine, he lost his job. Measles was the cause of it. After he recovered, he got work in a glass factory. The pay was better, and the work demanded skill. It was piecework, and the more skilful he was, the bigger wages he earned. Here was incentive. And under this incentive he developed into a remarkable worker.
It was simple work, the tying of glass stoppers into small bottles. At his waist he carried a bundle of twine. He held the bottles between his knees so that he might work with both hands. Thus, in a sitting position and bending over his own knees, his narrow shoulders grew humped and his chest was contracted for ten hours each day. This was not good for the lungs, but he tied three hundred dozen bottles a day.

===Chinese===
那份活儿他干得很快乐，尽管湿热难当，因为他那时还年轻，手里还握着梦和幻觉。他望着那蒸汽腾腾的布无休无止地流过，做过种种了不起的美梦。可这活儿用不着活动身子，也用不着动脑子，他的梦越做越少，头脑则越长越麻木、越长越昏沉。话虽如此，他一星期挣两块钱，而这两块钱，便是急性饿死和长期吃不饱之间的那点分别。
可他九岁上，活儿丢了。起因是麻疹。病好之后，他在一家玻璃厂寻到活儿。工钱好些，活儿也要手艺。那是计件活，手艺越精，进项越大。这就有了奔头。在这奔头底下，他长成了一名出挑的工人。
活儿很简单：往小瓶子里系玻璃瓶塞。他腰里别着一捆麻线。他把瓶子夹在两膝之间，好腾出双手干活。就这样，坐着，弓身伏在自己的膝盖上，他窄窄的肩膀一天十个小时往下驼，胸膛一天十个小时受压内缩。这对肺没有好处，可他一天系三百打瓶子。

===Original===
The superintendent was very proud of him, and brought visitors to look at him. In ten hours three hundred dozen bottles passed through his hands. This meant that he had attained machine-like perfection. All waste movements were eliminated. Every motion of his thin arms, every movement of a muscle in the thin fingers, was swift and accurate. He worked at high tension, and the result was that he grew nervous. At night his muscles twitched in his sleep, and in the daytime he could not relax and rest. He remained keyed up and his muscles continued to twitch. Also he grew sallow and his lint-cough grew worse. Then pneumonia laid hold of the feeble lungs within the contracted chest, and he lost his job in the glassworks.
Now he had returned to the jute mills where he had first begun with winding bobbins. But promotion was waiting for him. He was a good worker. He would next go on the starcher, and later he would go into the loom room. There was nothing after that except increased efficiency.
The machinery ran faster than when he had first gone to work, and his mind ran slower. He no longer dreamed at all, though his earlier years had been full of dreaming. Once he had been in love. It was when he first began guiding the cloth over the hot roller, and it was with the daughter of the superintendent. She was much older than he, a young woman, and he had seen her at a distance only a paltry half-dozen times. But that made no difference. On the surface of the cloth stream that poured past him, he pictured radiant futures wherein he performed prodigies of toil, invented miraculous machines, won to the mastership of the mills, and in the end took her in his arms and kissed her soberly on the brow.

===Chinese===
厂长以他为荣，领着来参观的人去看他。十个钟头里，三百打瓶子经过他的手。这意味着他已经达到了机器般的完美。一切多余的动作都剔除净尽了。他那两条细胳膊的每一动，细手指上每一块肌肉的每一动，全都又快又准。他常年绷到极限地干活，结果便是人变得神经质了。夜里睡着了肌肉抽搐，白天他松不下来、歇不过来。他始终上着弦，肌肉始终在跳。他还发了黄，飞絮咳嗽也重了。后来，肺炎攥住了他那压瘪的胸腔里的一对衰肺，玻璃厂的活儿也就丢了。
如今他回到了黄麻厂，回到他最初绕纱管起家的地方。可提升正等着他。他是把好手。下一步他要上上浆机，再往后，就要进织布车间了。再往后，除了效率更增，就什么也没有了。
机器比他初上工时转得快了，他的头脑却转得慢了。他再不做梦了，虽然早年间他满心都是梦。他恋爱过一回。那是在他刚开始引导布掠过热滚筒的时候，对象是厂长的女儿。她比他大得多，是位年轻姑娘，他远远地统共只见过她寒碜的六次面。可这不要紧。在他身边倾泻而过的布流表面上，他描画出一幅幅光灿灿的未来：在未来里，他干出苦干的奇迹，发明神妙的机器，一路爬到厂主的位子，到末了，把她搂进怀里，庄重地吻她的额头。

===Original===
But that was all in the long ago, before he had grown too old and tired to love. Also, she had married and gone away, and his mind had gone to sleep. Yet it had been a wonderful experience, and he used often to look back upon it as other men and women look back upon the time they believed in fairies. He had never believed in fairies nor Santa Claus; but he had believed implicitly in the smiling future his imagination had wrought into the steaming cloth stream.
He had become a man very early in life. At seven, when he drew his first wages, began his adolescence. A certain feeling of independence crept up in him, and the relationship between him and his mother changed. Somehow, as an earner and breadwinner, doing his own work in the world, he was more like an equal with her. Manhood, full-blown manhood, had come when he was eleven, at which time he had gone to work on the night shift for six months. No child works on the night shift and remains a child.
There had been several great events in his life. One of these had been when his mother bought some California prunes. Two others had been the two times when she cooked custard. Those had been events. He remembered them kindly. And at that time his mother had told him of a blissful dish she would sometime make﻿—“floating island,” she had called it, “better than custard.” For years he had looked forward to the day when he would sit down to the table with floating island before him, until at last he had relegated the idea of it to the limbo of unattainable ideals.

===Chinese===
可那全是很久很久以前的事了，早在他老得、累得爱不动之前。再说，她也嫁了人走远了，他的头脑也睡了。然而那终究是一段奇妙的经历，他惯常回首它，就像别的男女回首自己信仙子的年月那样。他从不曾信过仙子，也不曾信过圣诞老人；可他曾经笃信不疑那个微笑的未来——他的想象力把它织进那蒸汽腾腾的布流里的那个未来。
他很早就成了人。七岁头一回领工钱，他的青春期便开始了。某种自立的感觉在他心里悄悄爬起来，他同母亲的关系也变了。不知怎么，作为一个挣钱的人、养家的人，在世上干着他自己的一份活，他同她更像平起平坐了。男子气概，怒放的男子气概，是在他十一岁那年来到的——那时他上了六个月的夜班。没有哪个孩子上了夜班，还仍然是孩子。
他一生中有过几桩大事。其中一桩，是母亲买了一回加州西梅干。另外两桩，是她做过两回蛋奶冻。那都算得上大事。他想起它们，心里是温厚的。也是那时候，母亲同他讲起一道她哪天要做一做的至福美味——她管它叫“漂浮岛”，“比蛋奶冻还好”。一连好几年，他都盼着有朝一日坐到桌前，面前摆着漂浮岛，直到最后，他把这个念头打进了种种够不着的理想共归的幽冥里。

===Original===
Once he found a silver quarter lying on the sidewalk. That, also, was a great event in his life, withal a tragic one. He knew his duty on the instant the silver flashed on his eyes, before even he had picked it up. At home, as usual, there was not enough to eat, and home he should have taken it as he did his wages every Saturday night. Right conduct in this case was obvious; but he never had any spending of his money, and he was suffering from candy hunger. He was ravenous for the sweets that only on red-letter days he had ever tasted in his life.
He did not attempt to deceive himself. He knew it was sin, and deliberately he sinned when he went on a fifteen-cent candy debauch. Ten cents he saved for a future orgy; but not being accustomed to the carrying of money, he lost the ten cents. This occurred at the time when he was suffering all the torments of conscience, and it was to him an act of divine retribution. He had a frightened sense of the closeness of an awful and wrathful God. God had seen, and God had been swift to punish, denying him even the full wages of sin.
In memory he always looked back upon that as the one great criminal deed of his life, and at the recollection his conscience always awoke and gave him another twinge. It was the one skeleton in his closet. Also, being so made, and circumstanced, he looked back upon the deed with regret. He was dissatisfied with the manner in which he had spent the quarter. He could have invested it better, and, out of his later knowledge of the quickness of God, he would have beaten God out by spending the whole quarter at one fell swoop. In retrospect he spent the quarter a thousand times, and each time to better advantage.

===Chinese===
有一回，他在人行道上捡着一枚两毛五的银币。那也是他一生的一桩大事，而且是一桩悲剧性的大事。银光刚闪进他眼睛，他还没弯腰去捡，他该尽的义务就已经明白了。家里照例吃不饱，这钱他本该带回家去，像每星期六晚上带回工钱一样。这情形下怎样才算品行端正，明摆着的；可他的钱从来轮不到他花，他正害着一场糖馋。他馋糖馋得发狂——那路甜东西，他一辈子只有大喜的日子才尝到过。
他不打算自欺。他知道那是罪，而当他揣着一毛五分钱去纵情大嚼糖果的时候，他是存心把这罪犯下的。他留出一毛钱，备着将来再纵一回；可不惯带钱，那一毛钱又丢了。这事正赶在他受尽良心煎熬的当口，在他看来便是天谴。他惊惶地觉出，一位可畏而震怒的上帝近在咫尺。上帝看见了，上帝下手飞快，连罪孽的全份报酬都不让他拿足。
在记忆里，他始终把那看作他一生中独一无二的大罪案，一想起来，良心总要醒转，再拧他一把。那是他橱柜里仅有的一具骷髅。再呢，以他这样的人、这样的处境，他回首那桩罪时，竟还带着懊悔。他不满意那两毛五分钱花的路数。他本可以派更好的用场，而且，凭着他后来对上帝手脚之快的见识，他本该一鼓作气，把整枚银币一口气花个精光，赢上帝一着。在回想里，他把那两毛五分钱花了一千遍，一遍有一遍的好处。

===Original===
There was one other memory of the past, dim and faded, but stamped into his soul everlasting by the savage feet of his father. It was more like a nightmare than a remembered vision of a concrete thing﻿—more like the race-memory of man that makes him fall in his sleep and that goes back to his arboreal ancestry.
This particular memory never came to Johnny in broad daylight when he was wide awake. It came at night, in bed, at the moment that his consciousness was sinking down and losing itself in sleep. It always aroused him to frightened wakefulness, and for the moment, in the first sickening start, it seemed to him that he lay crosswise on the foot of the bed. In the bed were the vague forms of his father and mother. He never saw what his father looked like. He had but one impression of his father, and that was that he had savage and pitiless feet.
His earlier memories lingered with him, but he had no late memories. All days were alike. Yesterday or last year were the same as a thousand years﻿—or a minute. Nothing ever happened. There were no events to mark the march of time. Time did not march. It stood always still. It was only the whirling machines that moved, and they moved nowhere﻿—in spite of the fact that they moved faster.

===Chinese===
还有另一桩对往昔的记忆，黯淡而褪色，却被他父亲那双野蛮的脚，永远地印进了他的灵魂。它不像是对一件实体的追忆，倒更像一场噩梦——更像人的种族记忆，那种叫人在睡梦里猛一跌落、一直回溯到树栖祖先的记忆。
这桩特别的记忆，从不在大白天他清醒的时候来找约翰尼。它夜里来，在床上，在他的意识正往下沉、正要沉失在睡眠里的那一刻来。它总把他惊醒成一片恐怖，一时之间，在最初那阵发晕的惊悸里，他觉着自己正横躺在床脚上。床上是他父母两个模糊的形影。他从不曾看清父亲长什么模样。他对父亲只有一个印象，那就是：他有一双野蛮而不留情的脚。
早年的记忆还跟着他，可他没有近来的记忆。所有的日子全一样。昨天也好，去年也好，同一千年没有两样——或者同一分钟。什么都没有发生过。没有一桩事件来给时间的行进打上刻度。时间不行进。它永远站定。只有旋转的机器在动，而它们哪儿也不去——尽管它们越转越快。

===Original===
When he was fourteen, he went to work on the starcher. It was a colossal event. Something had at last happened that could be remembered beyond a night’s sleep or a week’s payday. It marked an era. It was a machine Olympiad, a thing to date from. “When I went to work on the starcher,” or, “after,” or “before I went to work on the starcher,” were sentences often on his lips.
He celebrated his sixteenth birthday by going into the loom room and taking a loom. Here was an incentive again, for it was piecework. And he excelled, because the clay of him had been moulded by the mills into the perfect machine. At the end of three months he was running two looms, and, later, three and four.
At the end of his second year at the looms he was turning out more yards than any other weaver, and more than twice as much as some of the less skilful ones. And at home things began to prosper as he approached the full stature of his earning power. Not, however, that his increased earnings were in excess of need. The children were growing up. They ate more. And they were going to school, and schoolbooks cost money. And somehow, the faster he worked, the faster climbed the prices of things. Even the rent went up, though the house had fallen from bad to worse disrepair.

===Chinese===
十四岁上，他去上浆机上干活。这是一桩天大的事。到底出了一桩记得过一夜瞌睡、一个礼拜发饷的大事了。它标志了一个纪元。那是一场机器界的奥林匹亚盛会，一件可以由此纪年的事。“我上浆机当差的时候”，或者“之后”，或者“我上浆机当差之前”，这些话常挂在他嘴上。
他跨进织布车间、接手一台织机，算是庆祝了自己的十六岁生日。这又是一个奔头，因为那是计件活。他也确实出众，因为他这块黏土，早叫厂子捏成了一台完美的机器。三个月下来，他看两台织机，再往后，三台、四台。
在织机上的第二个年头满期时，他出的布码数比哪个织工都多，比有些手艺欠佳的多出一倍还不止。家里呢，随着他的挣钱本事长到足个头，光景也日渐兴旺。不过，这并不是说他多挣的超过了所需。孩子们在长大。吃得更多了。他们又都上了学，书本子要钱。而且不知怎么，他干得越快，物价也爬得越快。连房租都涨了，尽管那座房子已经破败得一天不如一天。

===Original===
He had grown taller; but with his increased height he seemed leaner than ever. Also, he was more nervous. With the nervousness increased his peevishness and irritability. The children had learned by many bitter lessons to fight shy of him. His mother respected him for his earning power, but somehow her respect was tinctured with fear.
There was no joyousness in life for him. The procession of the days he never saw. The nights he slept away in twitching unconsciousness. The rest of the time he worked, and his consciousness was machine consciousness. Outside this his mind was a blank. He had no ideals, and but one illusion; namely, that he drank excellent coffee. He was a work-beast. He had no mental life whatever; yet deep down in the crypts of his mind, unknown to him, were being weighed and sifted every hour of his toil, every movement of his hands, every twitch of his muscles, and preparations were making for a future course of action that would amaze him and all his little world.
It was in the late spring that he came home from work one night aware of unusual tiredness. There was a keen expectancy in the air as he sat down to the table, but he did not notice. He went through the meal in moody silence, mechanically eating what was before him. The children um’d and ah’d and made smacking noises with their mouths. But he was deaf to them.

===Chinese===
他长高了些；可个子越高，人显得越瘦。他也更神经质了。神经质一重，坏脾气和火气也跟着重。孩子们经过许多回惨痛的教训，学会躲他远远的。母亲敬重他的挣钱本事，可不知怎么，那份敬重里掺着一味恐惧。
生活里没有一点欢乐分给他。日子的行进行列，他从不曾看见。夜晚，他在抽搐的无知觉里睡掉。其余的时间他干活，他的意识便是机器的意识。在这之外，他的头脑一片空白。他没有理想，只剩一个幻觉，那就是：他喝的咖啡好极了。他是一头役畜。他全然没有什么精神生活；然而在他头脑深处的地窖里，在他自己也不知道的地方，他劳苦的每一个钟点、双手的每一个动作、肌肉的每一次抽搐，都在被称量、被筛分，一场未来的行动正在那里酝酿——那行动将吓他自己一跳，也将吓坏他那小小的整个世界。
暮春的一个晚上，他下了工回家，觉出一种不寻常的累。他坐到桌边时，空气里有一股绷紧的期待，可他没有留意。他阴沉着脸，一言不发地吃完了那顿饭，机械地把面前的吃食送进嘴里。孩子们嗯嗯啊啊，嘴巴咂得直响。可他充耳不闻。

===Original===
“D’ye know what you’re eatin’?” his mother demanded at last, desperately.
He looked vacantly at the dish before him, and vacantly at her.
“Floatin’ island,” she announced triumphantly.

===Chinese===
“你知道你吃的是什么吗？”母亲终于按捺不住，问道。
他茫然看看面前的盘子，又茫然看看她。
“漂浮岛，”她凯旋一般地宣布。

===Original===
“Oh,” he said.
“Floating island!” the children chorussed loudly.
“Oh,” he said. And after two or three mouthfuls, he added, “I guess I ain’t hungry tonight.”
He dropped the spoon, shoved back his chair, and arose wearily from the table.
“An’ I guess I’ll go to bed.”
His feet dragged more heavily than usual as he crossed the kitchen floor. Undressing was a Titan’s task, a monstrous futility, and he wept weakly as he crawled into bed, one shoe still on. He was aware of a rising, swelling something inside his head that made his brain thick and fuzzy. His lean fingers felt as big as his wrist, while in the ends of them was a remoteness of sensation vague and fuzzy like his brain. The small of his back ached intolerably. All his bones ached. He ached everywhere. And in his head began the shrieking, pounding, crashing, roaring of a million looms. All space was filled with flying shuttles. They darted in and out, intricately, amongst the stars. He worked a thousand looms himself, and ever they speeded up, faster and faster, and his brain unwound, faster and faster, and became the thread that fed the thousand flying shuttles.

===Chinese===
“哦，”他说。
“漂浮岛！”孩子们扯着嗓子齐声喊。
“哦，”他说。又吃了两三口，他添了一句，“我寻思今晚不饿。”
他放下汤匙，推开椅子，倦怠地从桌边站起身。
“我寻思，该睡了。”
穿过厨房地板时，他的脚比平时拖得更沉了。脱衣服成了一件泰坦的苦役，一桩荒唐透顶的徒劳，他爬上床时还软弱地哭着，一只鞋都没脱。他觉得脑壳里有什么东西正在升起来、胀起来，把他的脑子泡得又稠又浑。他瘦瘦的手指觉着有手腕那么粗，而指尖上是一种又模糊又浑噩的遥远感，同他的脑子一个样。后腰疼得难忍。全身的骨头都疼。他哪儿都疼。随后，他脑袋里起了那一百万台织机的尖啸、撞击、轰砸、咆哮。整个空间挤满了飞梭。它们在星群中间迂回穿进穿出。他本人开着一千台织机，而且越转越快、越转越快，他的脑子也越解越快、越解越快，解成了那根喂进一千只飞梭里的纱。

===Original===
He did not go to work next morning. He was too busy weaving colossally on the thousand looms that ran inside his head. His mother went to work, but first she sent for the doctor. It was a severe attack of la grippe, he said. Jennie served as nurse and carried out his instructions.
It was a very severe attack, and it was a week before Johnny dressed and tottered feebly across the floor. Another week, the doctor said, and he would be fit to return to work. The foreman of the loom room visited him on Sunday afternoon, the first day of his convalescence. The best weaver in the room, the foreman told his mother. His job would be held for him. He could come back to work a week from Monday.
“Why don’t you thank ’im, Johnny?” his mother asked anxiously.

===Chinese===
第二天早晨他没有去上工。他太忙了，忙着在他脑袋里开动的那一千台织机上，宏伟地织布。母亲上工去了，不过先打发人请了医生。医生说，是一场重流感。珍妮充当看护，照他的嘱咐行事。
这场流感委实不轻，过了一个星期，约翰尼才穿上衣服，虚弱地打着晃挪过地板。医生说，再养一星期，就可以回去上工了。星期日下午，织布车间的领班来看他——那是他恢复期的头一天。车间里最好的织工，领班对他母亲这么说。他的位子给他留着。下星期一就能回来上工。
“你怎么不谢谢人家，约翰尼？”母亲着急地问。

===Original===
“He’s ben that sick he ain’t himself yet,” she explained apologetically to the visitor.
Johnny sat hunched up and gazing steadfastly at the floor. He sat in the same position long after the foreman had gone. It was warm outdoors, and he sat on the stoop in the afternoon. Sometimes his lips moved. He seemed lost in endless calculations.
Next morning, after the day grew warm, he took his seat on the stoop. He had pencil and paper this time with which to continue his calculations, and he calculated painfully and amazingly.

===Chinese===
“他病得厉害，人还没缓过来呢，”她又向客人赔着不是解释。
约翰尼弓着背坐着，目不转睛地盯着地板。领班走后许久，他还那样坐着。外头暖和，下午他便坐到门前台阶上。有时他的嘴唇翕动。他像是沉在没完没了的计算里。
第二天早晨，天转暖了，他又在台阶上落座。这一回他带了铅笔和纸，好接着算他的账，他算得又苦，又惊人。

===Original===
“What comes after millions?” he asked at noon, when Will came home from school. “An’ how d’ye work ’em?”
That afternoon finished his task. Each day, but without paper and pencil, he returned to the stoop. He was greatly absorbed in the one tree that grew across the street. He studied it for hours at a time, and was unusually interested when the wind swayed its branches and fluttered its leaves. Throughout the week he seemed lost in a great communion with himself. On Sunday, sitting on the stoop, he laughed aloud, several times, to the perturbation of his mother, who had not heard him laugh for years.
Next morning, in the early darkness, she came to his bed to rouse him. He had had his fill of sleep all the week, and awoke easily. He made no struggle, nor did he attempt to hold on to the bedding when she stripped it from him. He lay quietly, and spoke quietly.

===Chinese===
“百万往后是什么？”中午威尔放学回来，他问道。“那玩意儿怎么个算法？”
那天下午，他算完了。此后每天，只是不带纸笔，他都回到台阶上。他对街对面那棵独一的树着了迷。他一研究就是几个钟头，风摇动枝条、翻动树叶的时候，他兴味格外浓。整整一星期，他都像沉浸在同自己的一场大交流里。星期天，他坐在台阶上，好几回笑出了声，搅得他母亲心慌意乱——她有好多年没听见他笑过了。
第二天清晨，天还黑着，她到他床前来叫醒他。一星期来他睡足了觉，一叫就醒。他没有挣扎，她把铺盖扯走时，他也不曾想抓住不放。他静静地躺着，静静地开口。

===Original===
“It ain’t no use, ma.”
“You’ll be late,” she said, under the impression that he was still stupid with sleep.
“I’m awake, ma, an’ I tell you it ain’t no use. You might as well lemme alone. I ain’t goin’ to git up.”

===Chinese===
“没用的，妈。”
“你要迟到了，”她说，还当他睡得发懵。
“我醒着呢，妈，我告诉你，没用。你干脆别费事了。我不起来了。”

===Original===
“But you’ll lose your job!” she cried.
“I ain’t goin’ to git up,” he repeated in a strange, passionless voice.
She did not go to work herself that morning. This was sickness beyond any sickness she had ever known. Fever and delirium she could understand; but this was insanity. She pulled the bedding up over him and sent Jennie for the doctor.

===Chinese===
“可你的活儿要丢啦！”她喊道。
“我不起来了，”他又说了一遍，声音陌生，没有一点火气。
那天早晨，她自己也没去上工。这是一种病，比她见识过的任何病都深。发烧、说胡话，她能懂；可这个，是疯。她把铺盖给他拉上来盖好，打发珍妮去请医生。

===Original===
When that person arrived, Johnny was sleeping gently, and gently he awoke and allowed his pulse to be taken.
“Nothing the matter with him,” the doctor reported. “Badly debilitated, that’s all. Not much meat on his bones.”
“He’s always been that way,” his mother volunteered.

===Chinese===
那位到的时候，约翰尼正睡得安恬，他也安恬地醒来，听任人家给他号脉。
“他没病，”医生说。“只是虚得厉害。骨头上没多少肉。”
“他向来就那样，”母亲主动搭话。

===Original===
“Now go ’way, ma, an’ let me finish my snooze.”
Johnny spoke sweetly and placidly, and sweetly and placidly he rolled over on his side and went to sleep.
At ten o’clock he awoke and dressed himself. He walked out into the kitchen, where he found his mother with a frightened expression on her face.
“I’m goin’ away, ma,” he announced, “an’ I jes’ want to say goodbye.”
She threw her apron over her head and sat down suddenly and wept. He waited patiently.
“I might a-known it,” she was sobbing.

===Chinese===
“行了，走吧，妈，让我把这觉睡完。”
约翰尼说话的声气又甜又平和，接着他又甜又平和地侧过身去，睡着了。
十点钟他醒来，自己穿好衣服。他走进厨房，见母亲一脸惊惶。
“我要走了，妈，”他宣布道，“就想道个再见。”
她把围裙蒙上头，猛地坐下，哭了。他耐心等着。
“我早该料到的，”她呜咽着。

===Original===
“Where?” she finally asked, removing the apron from her head and gazing up at him with a stricken face in which there was little curiosity.
“I don’t know﻿—anywhere.”
As he spoke, the tree across the street appeared with dazzling brightness on his inner vision. It seemed to lurk just under his eyelids, and he could see it whenever he wished.
“An’ your job?” she quavered.
“I ain’t never goin’ to work again.”
“My God, Johnny!” she wailed, “don’t say that!”

===Chinese===
“去哪儿？”她终于问，把围裙从头上摘下来，仰望着他，一张深受打击的脸上，几乎没有多少好奇。
“不知道——随便哪儿。”
他说话的当儿，街对面那棵树带着耀眼的亮，浮现在他内心的视界里。它像是就埋伏在他的眼皮底下，什么时候想看，就能看见。
“那你的活儿呢？”她声音发颤地问。
“我再也不去做工了。”
“我的天，约翰尼！”她恸哭道，“别这么说！”

===Original===
What he had said was blasphemy to her. As a mother who hears her child deny God, was Johnny’s mother shocked by his words.
“What’s got into you, anyway?” she demanded, with a lame attempt at imperativeness.
“Figures,” he answered. “Jes’ figures. I’ve ben doin’ a lot of figurin’ this week, an’ it’s most surprisin’.”

===Chinese===
他说的那句话，在她听来是渎神。正如一个母亲听见孩子否认上帝那样，约翰尼的母亲被他的话震骇了。
“你到底中了什么邪？”她质问，想拿出点威严来，却软弱得可怜。
“数目字，”他答道。“就是数目字。这个礼拜我算了老大一笔账，算出来的东西吓人得很。”

===Original===
“I don’t see what that’s got to do with it,” she sniffled.
Johnny smiled patiently, and his mother was aware of a distinct shock at the persistent absence of his peevishness and irritability.
“I’ll show you,” he said. “I’m plum’ tired out. What makes me tired? Moves. I’ve ben movin’ ever since I was born. I’m tired of movin’, an’ I ain’t goin’ to move any more. Remember when I worked in the glasshouse? I used to do three hundred dozen a day. Now I reckon I made about ten different moves to each bottle. That’s thirty-six thousan’ moves a day. Ten days, three hundred an’ sixty thousan’ moves. One month, one million an’ eighty thousan’ moves. Chuck out the eighty thousan’ ”﻿—he spoke with the complacent beneficence of a philanthropist﻿—“chuck out the eighty thousan’, that leaves a million moves a month﻿—twelve million moves a year.

===Chinese===
“我看不出这跟那有什么相干，”她抽着鼻子说。
约翰尼耐心地笑了笑，而他那份坏脾气和火气的持续缺席，让他母亲清清楚楚地吃了一惊。
“我算给你听，”他说。“我是彻头彻尾累垮了。什么东西累垮的我？动作。打我生下来，我就一直在动。我动够了，再也不动了。还记得我在玻璃厂干活那阵不？我一天做三百打。我算下来，每只瓶子约莫要做十个不同的动作。一天就是三万六千个动作。十天，三十六万个动作。一个月，一百零八万个动作。抹掉那八万”——他说话的神气，像一个慈善家，洋洋自得、悲天悯人——“抹掉那八万，一个月还剩一百万个动作——一年一千二百万个动作。

===Original===
“At the looms I’m movin’ twic’st as much. That makes twenty-five million moves a year, an’ it seems to me I’ve ben a movin’ that way ’most a million years.
“Now this week I ain’t moved at all. I ain’t made one move in hours an’ hours. I tell you it was swell, jes’ settin’ there, hours an’ hours, an’ doin’ nothin’. I ain’t never ben happy before. I never had any time. I’ve ben movin’ all the time. That ain’t no way to be happy. An’ I ain’t going to do it any more. I’m jes’ goin’ to set, an’ set, an’ rest, an’ rest, and then rest some more.”
“But what’s goin’ to come of Will an’ the children?” she asked despairingly.

===Chinese===
“在织机上，我动的还要多上一倍。一年就是两千五百万个动作，我觉着我这么动下来，差不多有一百万年了。
“可这个礼拜，我一下都没动。好几个钟头、好几个钟头，我一个动作也没做。我告诉你，那才叫美，就那么坐着，一个钟头接一个钟头，什么都不干。我从前从来不曾快活过。我从来一点工夫都没有。我一直在动。那样是快活不了的。我也不再那样了。我就要坐着，坐着，歇着，歇着，歇了再歇。”
“可威尔和孩子们往后怎么办？”她绝望地问。

===Original===
