“You don’t know your own mind for two successive minutes! You haven’t any mind, you spineless, crawling worm!”
“You can’t make me angry.” Al smiled with cunning, and glanced triumphantly at his wife. “You can’t make me angry,” he repeated, as though the idea were thoroughly gratifying to him. “I know your game. It’s my stomach, I tell you. I can’t help it. Before God, I can’t! Isn’t it my stomach, Mary?”
She glanced at George and spoke composedly, though she hid a trembling hand in a fold of her skirt.

===Chinese===
“犯不着因为我改了主意就作践我，”那一个抗议道，声音里带出一丝哭腔。
他的妻子进来了，他向她投去求告的眼色；窗边那人却突然大步抢到他跟前，冲口嚷道——
“你连接连两分钟都拿不稳自己的主意！你压根儿就没有主意，你这没有脊梁的、贴地爬的虫子！”
“你气不着我。”阿尔狡黠地一笑，得意地瞟了妻子一眼。“你气不着我，”他又说一遍，仿佛这个念头让他浑身舒坦。“我知道你那套把戏。是我胃的毛病，我告诉你。我管不住。老天在上，我管不住！玛丽，这不是我胃的毛病是什么？”
她瞟了乔治一眼，神色安详地开了口，只是一只发抖的手藏进了裙褶里。

===Original===
“Isn’t it time?” she asked softly.
Her husband turned upon her savagely. “I’m not going to go!” he cried. “That’s just what I’ve been telling﻿ ﻿… him. And I tell you again, all of you, I’m not going. You can’t bully me.”
“Why, Al, dear, you said﻿—” she began.
“Never mind what I said!” he broke out. “I’ve said something else right now, and you’ve heard it, and that settles it.”
He walked across the room and threw himself with emphasis into a Morris chair. But the other man was swiftly upon him. The talon-like fingers gripped his shoulders, jerked him to his feet, and held him there.

===Chinese===
“时间是不是差不多了？”她轻声问。
她丈夫恶狠狠地朝她转过身去。“我不去！”他嚷道，“我正跟……跟他说这事呢。我再说一遍，你们大家伙儿都听着，我不去。你们吓唬不动我。”
“嗳，阿尔，亲爱的，你方才不是说——”她刚开口。
“管我方才说什么！”他发作了，“我这会儿说了别的，你们都听见了，就这么定了。”
他穿过屋子，一屁股狠狠坐进莫里斯椅。可那人转眼就逼到了他跟前。鹰爪似的手指扣住他的双肩，猛地把他拽起来，钉在原地。

===Original===
“You’ve reached the limit, Al, and I want you to understand it. I’ve tried to treat you like﻿ ﻿… like my brother, but hereafter I shall treat you like the thing that you are. Do you understand?”
The anger in his voice was cold. The blaze in his eyes was cold. It was vastly more effective than any outburst, and Al cringed under it and under the clutching hand that was bruising his shoulder muscles.
“It is only because of me that you have this house, that you have the food you eat. Your position? Any other man would have been shown the door a year ago﻿—two years ago. I have held you in it. Your salary has been charity. It has been paid out of my pocket. Mary﻿ ﻿… her dresses﻿ ﻿… that gown she has on is made over; she wears the discarded dresses of her sisters, of my wife. Charity﻿—do you understand? Your children﻿—they are wearing the discarded clothes of my children, of the children of my neighbours who think the clothes went to some orphan asylum. And it is an orphan asylum﻿ ﻿… or it soon will be.”
He emphasized each point with an unconscious tightening of his grip on the shoulder. Al was squirming with the pain of it. The sweat was starting out on his forehead.
“Now listen well to me,” his brother went on. “In three minutes you will tell me that you are going with me. If you don’t, Mary and the children will be taken away from you﻿—today. You needn’t ever come to the office. This house will be closed to you. And in six months I shall have the pleasure of burying you. You have three minutes to make up your mind.”

===Chinese===
“你已经到头了，阿尔，我要你明白这一点。我一直试着拿你当……当我兄弟看待，可从今往后，我要拿你现在这副东西来对待你。你明白吗？”
他声音里的怒气是冷的。他眼中的火光是冷的。这比任何发作都管用得多，阿尔在那冷光底下、在那掐得他肩膀肌肉生疼的手底下，缩得矮了一截。
“全是因为我，你才有这栋房子住，才有你嘴里的饭吃。你的职位？换了随便哪个旁人，一年前——两年前——就被人扫地出门了。是我把你保下来的。你那份薪水一直是施舍，一直是从我口袋里掏的。玛丽……她的衣裳……她身上那条裙子是旧的改的；她穿的是她姐妹们、我妻子不要的裙子。施舍——你明白吗？你的孩子们——身上穿的是我的孩子们、邻家孩子们不要的衣裳，那些邻居还当衣裳是送进了哪家孤儿院。而这就是孤儿院……要不了多久就是了。”
他每说一句，扣在肩上的手便不由自主地收紧一分。阿尔疼得直扭。汗珠从他额头上沁了出来。
“现在好好听着，”做哥哥的接着说，“三分钟之内，你会告诉我，你跟我一起去。你要是不说，玛丽和孩子们今天就有人从你身边带走。你再也用不着去上班。这栋房子将对你上锁。半年之内，我就会有幸来葬你。给你三分钟拿主意。”

===Original===
Al made a strangling movement, and reached up with weak fingers to the clutching hand.
“My heart﻿ ﻿… let me go﻿ ﻿… you’ll be the death of me,” he gasped.
The hand thrust him down forcibly into the Morris chair and released him.
The clock on the mantle ticked loudly. George glanced at it, and at Mary. She was leaning against the table, unable to conceal her trembling. He became unpleasantly aware of the feeling of his brother’s fingers on his hand. Quite unconsciously he wiped the back of the hand upon his coat. The clock ticked on in the silence. It seemed to George that the room reverberated with his voice. He could hear himself still speaking.
“I’ll go,” came from the Morris chair.

===Chinese===
阿尔做了个噎住似的动作，抬起软弱的手指，去掰那只钳住他的手。
“我的心脏……放开我……你要弄死我了，”他喘着气说。
那只手把他狠狠按回莫里斯椅，松开了。
壁炉架上的钟滴答滴答，响得刺耳。乔治看了看钟，又看了看玛丽。她倚着桌子，遮不住周身的颤抖。他很不自在地觉出弟弟的手指碰过自己手背的触感，竟不由自主地在衣襟上擦了擦手背。钟在寂静里继续滴答。乔治觉得满屋子都荡着他自己的声音。他还能听见自己正在说话。
“我去。”莫里斯椅里送出这么一句。

===Original===
It was a weak and shaken voice, and it was a weak and shaken man that pulled himself out of the Morris chair. He started toward the door.
“Where are you going?” George demanded.
“Suit case,” came the response. “Mary’ll send the trunk later. I’ll be back in a minute.”
The door closed after him. A moment later, struck with sudden suspicion, George was opening the door. He glanced in. His brother stood at a sideboard, in one hand a decanter, in the other hand, bottom up and to his lips, a whisky glass.
Across the glass Al saw that he was observed. It threw him into a panic. Hastily he tried to refill the glass and get it to his lips; but glass and decanter were sent smashing to the floor. He snarled. It was like the sound of a wild beast. But the grip on his shoulder subdued and frightened him. He was being propelled toward the door.

===Chinese===
那是个虚弱而发颤的声音，从莫里斯椅里把自己撑起来的，也是个虚弱而发颤的人。他朝门口走去。
“你去哪儿？”乔治问。
“拿手提箱，”回答传来，“大衣箱玛丽随后托人送。我去去就回。”
门在他身后关上了。片刻之后，乔治心头蓦地起疑，推开了门。他朝里一瞥。弟弟站在餐具柜前，一手拎着细颈酒瓶，另一只手里一只威士忌杯，底儿朝天，正凑在唇边。
隔着杯底，阿尔看见自己被撞破了。这一下叫他慌了神。他急急地想再斟满杯子送到嘴边；可杯子和酒瓶都被扫到地板上，摔了个粉碎。他低吼了一声，那声音像野兽。可肩上那一记钳制慑住了他，吓住了他。他被人推着朝门口去。

===Original===
“The suit case,” he gasped. “It’s there in that room. Let me get it.”
“Where’s the key?” his brother asked, when he had brought it.
“It isn’t locked.”
The next moment the suit case was spread open, and George’s hand was searching the contents. From one side it brought out a bottle of whisky, from the other side a flask. He snapped the case to.
“Come on,” he said. “If we miss one car, we miss that train.”

===Chinese===
“手提箱，”他喘着说，“就在那间屋里。让我去拿。”
等他把箱子拿来，他哥哥问道：“钥匙呢？”
“没上锁。”
转眼间手提箱摊开了，乔治的手在里头翻检。从这一头掏出一瓶威士忌，从那一头掏出一只扁酒壶。他啪的一声扣上箱子。
“走吧，”他说，“误了一班车，就误了那趟火车。”

===Original===
He went out into the hallway, leaving Al with his wife. It was like a funeral, George thought, as he waited.
His brother’s overcoat caught on the knob of the front door and delayed its closing long enough for Mary’s first sob to come to their ears. George’s lips were very thin and compressed as he went down the steps. In one hand he carried the suit case. With the other hand he held his brother’s arm.
As they neared the corner, he heard the electric car a block away, and urged his brother on. Al was breathing hard. His feet dragged and shuffled, and he held back.
“A hell of a brother *you* are,” he panted.
For reply, he received a vicious jerk on his arm. It reminded him of his childhood when he was hurried along by some angry grownup. And like a child, he had to be helped up the car step. He sank down on an outside seat, panting, sweating, overcome by the exertion. He followed George’s eyes as the latter looked him up and down.

===Chinese===
他出到门厅里，把阿尔留给妻子。这像一场葬礼，乔治等着的时候想。
弟弟的大衣挂住了前门的门钮，门迟迟关不上，恰好让玛丽的第一声呜咽送进他们耳朵里。乔治走下台阶时，两片嘴唇抿得极薄。他一手拎着手提箱，另一只手攥着弟弟的胳膊。
快到街角时，他听见隔一个街区外电车的动静，便催弟弟快走。阿尔喘得粗重。他两脚拖拖沓沓，直往后坠。
“*你*这样的哥哥，当得真够呛，”他喘着说。
作为回答，他胳膊上挨了狠狠一拽。这叫他想起小时候被哪个动怒的大人拽着赶路的情形。也像个孩子似的，他得让人搀上电车的踏级。他瘫进外侧的座位，喘着，冒着汗，这么一点力气就把他压垮了。乔治上上下下地打量他，他的目光便随着乔治的目光转。

===Original===
“A hell of a brother *you* are,” was George’s comment when he had finished the inspection.
Moisture welled into Al’s eyes.
“It’s my stomach,” he said with self-pity.
“I don’t wonder,” was the retort. “Burnt out like the crater of a volcano. Fervent heat isn’t a circumstance.”
Thereafter they did not speak. When they arrived at the transfer point, George came to himself with a start. He smiled. With fixed gaze that did not see the houses that streamed across his field of vision, he had himself been sunk deep in self-pity. He helped his brother from the car, and looked up the intersecting street. The car they were to take was not in sight.

===Chinese===
“*你*这样的弟弟，也当得真够呛。”乔治打量完了，只评了这么一句。
阿尔的眼里涌起了湿意。
“是我胃的毛病，”他自怜自艾地说。
“我一点也不奇怪，”对方回敬，“烧得跟火山口似的干净透亮，连‘烈火炽热’都比不上它。”
此后两人再没说话。到了换乘站，乔治蓦地一惊，回过神来。他笑了。方才他目光发直，任一座座房子从眼前流过而视若无睹，原来自家也正深深泡在自怜里。他搀弟弟下了车，朝交叉的街上望去。他们要换乘的那班车还没有影。

===Original===
Al’s eyes chanced upon the corner grocery and saloon across the way. At once he became restless. His hands passed beyond his control, and he yearned hungrily across the street to the door that swung open even as he looked and let in a happy pilgrim. And in that instant he saw the white-jacketed bartender against an array of glittering glass. Quite unconsciously he started to cross the street.
“Hold on.” George’s hand was on his arm.
“I want some whisky,” he answered.
“You’ve already had some.”
“That was hours ago. Go on, George, let me have some. It’s the last day. Don’t shut off on me until we get there﻿—God knows it will be soon enough.”

===Chinese===
阿尔的眼睛恰巧落在街对面的角铺上，杂货带酒馆。他顿时不安生起来。他的两只手不听使唤了，他隔着街，饿狼似的巴望起那扇门来——就在他望着的当口，那门悠地一荡开了，放进去一个快活的香客。也是在那一瞬间，他看见了穿白褂子的酒保，背后立着一排亮闪闪的玻璃瓶。他全然不由自主地抬脚横穿马路去。
“站住。”乔治的手按上他的胳膊。
“我要点威士忌，”他答道。
“你方才已经喝过了。”
“那是几个钟头前的事了。行行好，乔治，让我喝一口。今天是最后一天了。别在到那儿之前就给我掐断——老天知道，反正那一天来得够快的了。”

===Original===
George glanced desperately up the street. The car was in sight.
“There isn’t time for a drink,” he said.
“I don’t want a drink. I want a bottle.” Al’s voice became wheedling. “Go on, George. It’s the last, the very last.”
“No.” The denial was as final as George’s thin lips could make it.
Al glanced at the approaching car. He sat down suddenly on the curbstone.

===Chinese===
乔治绝望地朝街那头望了一眼。电车已经望得见了。
“来不及喝一杯了，”他说。
“我不要喝一杯。我要一瓶。”阿尔的声音软了下来，直打商量，“好乔治，行行好。这是最后一瓶，真是最后一瓶了。”
“不行。”这个“不”字，被乔治那两片薄嘴唇说得再没有半点余地。
阿尔看了看渐渐驶近的电车。他冷不丁坐在了路缘石上。

===Original===
“What’s the matter?” his brother asked, with momentary alarm.
“Nothing. I want some whisky. It’s my stomach.”
“Come on now, get up.”
George reached for him, but was anticipated, for his brother sprawled flat on the pavement, oblivious to the dirt and to the curious glances of the passersby. The car was clanging its gong at the crossing, a block away.
“You’ll miss it,” Al grinned from the pavement. “And it will be your fault.”

===Chinese===
“这是怎么了？”他哥哥问，一时着了慌。
“不怎么了。我要点威士忌。是我胃的毛病。”
“行了，起来。”
乔治伸手去拉他，却慢了一步，弟弟已经直挺挺摊开在人行道上，顾不得脏，也顾不得过路人好奇的眼光。电车正在一个街区外的道口上铛铛地敲着锣。
“你要误车了，”阿尔在人行道上咧着嘴笑，“那可就是你的不是了。”

===Original===
George’s fists clenched tightly.
“For two cents I’d give you a thrashing.”
“And miss the car,” was the triumphant comment from the pavement.
George looked at the car. It was halfway down the block. He looked at his watch. He debated a second longer.
“All right,” he said. “I’ll get it. But you get on that car. If you miss it, I’ll break the bottle over your head.”

===Chinese===
乔治的拳头紧紧攥了起来。
“再有一星半点的由头，我就狠狠揍你一顿。”
“那车就误了，”人行道上传来得意的接话。
乔治看了看电车。它已经开过半个街区。他看了看表。又掂量了一秒钟。
“好吧，”他说，“我去买。可你得给我上那班车。你要是误了车，我就拿瓶子砸你的脑袋。”

===Original===
He dashed across the street and into the saloon. The car came in and stopped. There were no passengers to get off. Al dragged himself up the steps and sat down. He smiled as the conductor rang the bell and the car started. The swinging door of the saloon burst open. Clutching in his hand the suit case and a pint bottle of whisky, George started in pursuit. The conductor, his hand on the bell cord, waited to see if it would be necessary to stop. It was not. George swung lightly aboard, sat down beside his brother, and passed him the bottle.
“You might have got a quart,” Al said reproachfully.
He extracted the cork with a pocket corkscrew, and elevated the bottle.
“I’m sick﻿ ﻿… my stomach,” he explained in apologetic tones to the passenger who sat next to him.
In the train they sat in the smoking-car. George felt that it was imperative. Also, having successfully caught the train, his heart softened. He felt more kindly toward his brother, and accused himself of unnecessary harshness. He strove to atone by talking about their mother, and sisters, and the little affairs and interests of the family. But Al was morose, and devoted himself to the bottle. As the time passed, his mouth hung looser and looser, while the rings under his eyes seemed to puff out and all his facial muscles to relax.

===Chinese===
他飞奔过街，冲进酒馆。电车进站停住。没有乘客下车。阿尔拖起身子登上踏级，坐下了。售票员摇铃，车一动，他就笑了。酒馆的弹簧门砰地弹开。乔治一手拎着手提箱、一手攥着一品脱瓶装的威士忌，拔腿追来。售票员一只手搭在铃绳上，等着看要不要停车。用不着。乔治轻巧地一荡上了车，在弟弟身边坐下，把瓶子递给他。
“你本可以买一夸脱的，”阿尔埋怨道。
他用随身的小开塞钻起出瓶塞，举起了瓶子。
“我不舒服……胃的毛病，”他向邻座的乘客道歉似的解释。
上了火车，他们坐在吸烟车厢里。乔治觉得非如此不可。同时，火车既然赶上了，他的心也软了。他对弟弟多了几分好意，倒怪起自己方才不必要地狠。他竭力想弥补，便谈起他们的母亲、几个姐妹，谈家里那些鸡毛蒜皮的家务和牵挂。可阿尔闷闷的，只一门心思对付那瓶酒。随着时间过去，他的嘴角越挂越松，眼下的圈儿仿佛越发肿起，一脸的肌肉都松懈下来。

===Original===
“It’s my stomach,” he said, once, when he finished the bottle and dropped it under the seat; but the swift hardening of his brother’s face did not encourage further explanations.
The conveyance that met them at the station had all the dignity and luxuriousness of a private carriage. George’s eyes were keen for the ear marks of the institution to which they were going, but his apprehensions were allayed from moment to moment. As they entered the wide gateway and rolled on through the spacious grounds, he felt sure that the institutional side of the place would not jar upon his brother. It was more like a summer hotel, or, better yet, a country club. And as they swept on through the spring sunshine, the songs of birds in his ears, and in his nostrils the breath of flowers, George sighed for a week of rest in such a place, and before his eyes loomed the arid vista of summer in town and at the office. There was not room in his income for his brother and himself.
“Let us take a walk in the grounds,” he suggested, after they had met Doctor Bodineau and inspected the quarters assigned to Al. “The carriage leaves for the station in half an hour, and we’ll just have time.”
“It’s beautiful,” he remarked a moment later. Under his feet was the velvet grass, the trees arched overhead, and he stood in mottled sunshine. “I wish I could stay for a month.”
“I’ll trade places with you,” Al said quickly.

===Chinese===
“是我胃的毛病，”有一回，他喝干了瓶子，把它塞到座位底下，这么说道；可他哥哥的脸霎时一沉，教他没了再往下解释的兴致。
车站上来接他们的车马，气派而奢华，全然一辆私人马车。乔治的眼睛尖得很，一路搜检他们要去的那家机构的记号，可他的疑虑一程一程地被打消。驶进宽阔的大门、穿过宽敞的庭园时，他确信这地方机构的一面不会硌着弟弟。它更像一家夏季旅馆，或者更说得好些，一家乡村俱乐部。马车一路扫过春天的阳光，耳中是鸟啼，鼻端是花香，乔治叹了一口艳羡的气，巴不得在这样的地方歇上一个星期，而眼前浮现的，是城里和办公室里那一整个荒旱的夏天。他的收入里，容不下他和弟弟两个人。
“咱们到园子里走走吧，”见过博迪诺大夫、看过分派给阿尔的住处之后，他提议道，“马车半个钟头后回车站，时间刚刚好。”
“真美，”过了一会儿他说。脚下是天鹅绒似的草地，头顶树冠拱合，他站在花斑的日影里。“但愿我能在这儿住上一个月。”
“我跟你换，”阿尔应得飞快。

===Original===
George laughed it off, but he felt a sinking of the heart.
“Look at that oak!” he cried. “And that woodpecker! Isn’t he a beauty!”
“I don’t like it here,” he heard his brother mutter.
George’s lips tightened in preparation for the struggle, but he said﻿—
“I’m going to send Mary and the children off to the mountains. She needs it, and so do they. And when you’re in shape, I’ll send you right on to join them. Then you can take your summer vacation before you come back to the office.”

===Chinese===
乔治一笑了之，心口却往下沉了一沉。
“瞧那棵橡树！”他喊道，“还有那只啄木鸟！多俊的一只鸟！”
“我不喜欢这地方，”他听见弟弟咕哝。
乔治绷紧了嘴唇，预备迎接一场角力，嘴上却说——
“我打算把玛丽和孩子们送到山里去。她需要，孩子们也需要。等你将养好了，我就送你直接去同他们会合。那样你还能歇个暑假，再回来上班。”

===Original===
“I’m not going to stay in this damned hole, for all you talk about it,” Al announced abruptly.
“Yes you are, and you’re going to get your health and strength back again, so that the look of you will put the colour in Mary’s cheeks where it used to be.”
“I’m going back with you.” Al’s voice was firm. “I’m going to take the same train back. It’s about time for that carriage, I guess.”
“I haven’t told you all my plans,” George tried to go on, but Al cut him off.
“You might as well quit that. I don’t want any of your soapy talking. You treat me like a child. I’m not a child. My mind’s made up, and I’ll show you how long it can stay made up. You needn’t talk to me. I don’t care a rap for what you’re going to say.”

===Chinese===
“任你说得天花乱坠，我也不要待在这个鬼窝里，”阿尔突兀地宣布。
“你要待的，而且你要把身子骨和气力养回来，养到你这副模样，能把玛丽脸颊上原有的血色给带回来。”
“我要跟你回去。”阿尔的口气很硬。“我要坐同一班火车回去。我看，马车该到时候了。”
“我的打算还没跟你说全呢，”乔治还想说下去，阿尔却把他截住了。
“你趁早省了这份心。你那套抹了油的话我不听。你把我当小孩子。我不是小孩子。我的主意定了，我倒要叫你看看这主意能定多久。你用不着跟我说了。你打算说什么，我半个子儿也不在乎。”

===Original===
A baleful light was in his eyes, and to his brother he seemed for all the world like a cornered rat, desperate and ready to fight. As George looked at him he remembered back to their childhood, and it came to him that at last was aroused in Al the same old stubborn strain that had enabled him, as a child, to stand against all force and persuasion.
George abandoned hope. He had lost. This creature was not human. The last fine instinct of the human had fled. It was a brute, sluggish and stolid, impossible to move﻿—just the raw stuff of life, combative, rebellious, and indomitable. And as he contemplated his brother he felt in himself the rising up of a similar brute. He became suddenly aware that his fingers were tensing and crooking like a thug’s, and he knew the desire to kill. And his reason, turned traitor at last, counselled that he should kill, that it was the only thing left for him to do.
He was aroused by a servant calling to him through the trees that the carriage was waiting. He answered. Then, looking straight before him, he discovered his brother. He had forgotten it was his brother. It had been only a thing the moment before. He began to talk, and as he talked the way became clear to him. His reason had not turned traitor. The brute in him had merely orientated his reason.
“You are no earthly good, Al,” he said. “You know that. You’ve made Mary’s life a hell. You are a curse to your children. And you have not made life exactly a paradise for the rest of us.”
“There’s no use your talking,” Al interjected. “I’m not going to stay here.”

===Chinese===
他眼里燃着凶光，在他哥哥看来，他活脱脱是一只被逼进死路的老鼠，绝了望，随时要扑上来咬。乔治望着他，记起他们的童年，蓦地明白了：阿尔身上那股从小就有的拗劲终于又醒了——正是那股拗劲，叫他小时候顶得住一切强逼和哄劝。
乔治死了心。他输了。这个东西已经不是人。人身上最后一点精微的本能已经遁走了。这是一头畜生，迟钝、顽钝、撼不动——只剩下生命的生料子，好斗、反叛、不折不服。而当他注视着弟弟，他觉得自己胸中也升起一头同样的畜生。他猛然察觉自己的手指正绷紧、蜷曲，像刺客的手指，而且他知道了自己起了杀念。他的理智到底也叛变了，竟劝他杀，说那是他仅剩可做的一件事。
一个仆人穿过树丛喊他，说马车在等，把他惊醒了。他应了一声。随后，他直望着前面，重新认出了弟弟。方才他已忘了那是他弟弟。前一刻，那不过是一件东西。他开口说话，说着说着，那条路子在他眼前清楚起来。他的理智并没有叛变。是他体内的畜生，只是给理智拨了个方向。
“你活在世上没有一点用处了，阿尔，”他说，“这你自己知道。你把玛丽的日子做成了地狱。你是你儿女的一场灾殃。而我们其余这些人，你也没把日子给做成天堂。”
“你说什么也没用，”阿尔插嘴，“我不待在这儿。”

===Original===
“That’s what I’m coming to,” George continued. “You don’t have to stay here.” (Al’s face brightened, and he involuntarily made a movement, as though about to start toward the carriage.) “On the other hand, it is not necessary that you should return with me. There is another way.”
George’s hand went to his hip pocket and appeared with a revolver. It lay along his palm, the butt toward Al, and toward Al he extended it. At the same time, with his head, he indicated the nearby thicket.
“You can’t bluff me,” Al snarled.
“It is not a bluff, Al. Look at me. I mean it. And if you don’t do it for yourself, I shall have to do it for you.”
They faced each other, the proffered revolver still extended. Al debated for a moment, then his eyes blazed. With a quick movement he seized the revolver.

===Chinese===
“我正要说的是这个，”乔治接着说，“你不必待在这儿。”（阿尔的脸一下子亮了，他不由自主地一动，像是要朝马车迈步。）“可另一方面，你也不必跟我回去。还有一条路。”
乔治的手探进裤子后袋，出来时握着一支左轮枪。枪横在他的掌心里，枪柄朝着阿尔，他就这样朝阿尔递过去。同时，他拿头朝近旁的树丛点了一点。
“你唬不住我，”阿尔龇牙咧嘴地说。
“这不是唬你，阿尔。看着我。我是当真的。你要是不肯为了你自己动手，我就只好替你动手。”
两人对面立着，那支递出去的左轮枪仍旧递在半空。阿尔踌躇了片刻，随即眼睛里烧起火来。他一个急动作，一把攥住了枪。

===Original===
“My God! I’ll do it,” he said. “I’ll show you what I’ve got in me.”
George felt suddenly sick. He turned away. He did not see his brother enter the thicket, but he heard the passage of his body through the leaves and branches.
“Goodbye, Al,” he called.
“Goodbye,” came from the thicket.
George felt the sweat upon his forehead. He began mopping his face with his handkerchief. He heard, as from a remote distance, the voice of the servant again calling to him that the carriage was waiting. The woodpecker dropped down through the mottled sunshine and lighted on the trunk of a tree a dozen feet away. George felt that it was all a dream, and yet through it all he felt supreme justification. It was the right thing to do. It was the only thing.

===Chinese===
“天哪！我来。”他说，“我让你看看我这条命里还剩着什么。”
乔治突然觉得一阵恶心。他转过脸去。他没有看见弟弟走进树丛，只听见他的身子穿过枝叶的声音。
“再见了，阿尔，”他喊。
“再见，”树丛里应道。
乔治觉出额头上的汗，掏出手帕擦起脸来。仆人的声音又远远传来，喊他马车还在等，像从老远老远的地方。啄木鸟穿过斑驳的日影俯冲下来，停在十来英尺外的一根树干上。乔治觉得这一切都像一场梦，然而贯穿这场梦，他觉出一种至高无上的理直气壮。这样做是对的。只有这一条路。

===Original===
His whole body gave a spasmodic start, as though the revolver had been fired. It was the voice of Al, close at his back.
“Here’s your gun,” Al said. “I’ll stay.”
The servant appeared among the trees, approaching rapidly and calling anxiously. George put the weapon in his pocket and caught both his brother’s hands in his own.
“God bless you, old man,” he murmured; “and”﻿—with a final squeeze of the hands﻿—“good luck!”
“I’m coming,” he called to the servant, and turned and ran through the trees toward the carriage.

===Chinese===
他浑身的筋肉猛地一抽，仿佛左轮枪已经放响。是阿尔的声音，就在他背后。
“枪还你，”阿尔说，“我留下。”
仆人从树间出现了，一路急急走来，一路焦灼地喊。乔治把枪揣进口袋，一双手把弟弟的两只手紧紧握住。
“上帝保佑你，老伙计，”他喃喃地说，“还有”——他把那双手最后紧了一紧——“祝你好运！”
“来了，”他应着仆人，转身穿过树丛，朝马车跑去。

## The Chinago / 中国佬

===Original===
> “The coral waxes, the palm grows, but man departs.”
> — Tahitian proverb

===Chinese===
> “珊瑚滋长，棕榈成荫，唯人易逝。”
> ——塔希提谚语

===Original===
Ah Cho did not understand French. He sat in the crowded court room, very weary and bored, listening to the unceasing, explosive French that now one official and now another uttered. It was just so much gabble to Ah Cho, and he marvelled at the stupidity of the Frenchmen who took so long to find out the murderer of Chung Ga, and who did not find him at all. The five hundred coolies on the plantation knew that Ah San had done the killing, and here was Ah San not even arrested. It was true that all the coolies had agreed secretly not to testify against one another; but then, it was so simple, the Frenchmen should have been able to discover that Ah San was the man. They were very stupid, these Frenchmen.
Ah Cho had done nothing of which to be afraid. He had had no hand in the killing. It was true he had been present at it, and Schemmer, the overseer on the plantation, had rushed into the barracks immediately afterward and caught him there, along with four or five others; but what of that? Chung Ga had been stabbed only twice. It stood to reason that five or six men could not inflict two stab wounds. At the most, if a man had struck but once, only two men could have done it.
So it was that Ah Cho reasoned, when he, along with his four companions, had lied and blocked and obfuscated in their statements to the court concerning what had taken place. They had heard the sounds of the killing, and, like Schemmer, they had run to the spot. They had got there before Schemmer﻿—that was all. True, Schemmer had testified that, attracted by the sound of quarrelling as he chanced to pass by, he had stood for at least five minutes outside; that then, when he entered, he found the prisoners already inside; and that they had not entered just before, because he had been standing by the one door to the barracks. But what of that? Ah Cho and his four fellow-prisoners had testified that Schemmer was mistaken. In the end they would be let go. They were all confident of that. Five men could not have their heads cut off for two stab wounds. Besides, no foreign devil had seen the killing. But these Frenchmen were so stupid. In China, as Ah Cho well knew, the magistrate would order all of them to the torture and learn the truth. The truth was very easy to learn under torture. But these Frenchmen did not torture﻿—bigger fools they! Therefore they would never find out who killed Chung Ga.
But Ah Cho did not understand everything. The English Company that owned the plantation had imported into Tahiti, at great expense, the five hundred coolies. The stockholders were clamouring for dividends, and the Company had not yet paid any; wherefore the Company did not want its costly contract labourers to start the practice of killing one another. Also, there were the French, eager and willing to impose upon the Chinagos the virtues and excellences of French law. There was nothing like setting an example once in a while; and, besides, of what use was New Caledonia except to send men to live out their days in misery and pain in payment of the penalty for being frail and human?
Ah Cho did not understand all this. He sat in the court room and waited for the baffled judgment that would set him and his comrades free to go back to the plantation and work out the terms of their contracts. This judgment would soon be rendered. Proceedings were drawing to a close. He could see that. There was no more testifying, no more gabble of tongues. The French devils were tired, too, and evidently waiting for the judgment. And as he waited he remembered back in his life to the time when he had signed the contract and set sail in the ship for Tahiti. Times had been hard in his seacoast village, and when he indentured himself to labour for five years in the South Seas at fifty cents Mexican a day, he had thought himself fortunate. There were men in his village who toiled a whole year for ten dollars Mexican, and there were women who made nets all the year round for five dollars, while in the houses of shopkeepers there were maidservants who received four dollars for a year of service. And here he was to receive fifty cents a day; for one day, only one day, he was to receive that princely sum! What if the work were hard? At the end of the five years he would return home﻿—that was in the contract﻿—and he would never have to work again. He would be a rich man for life, with a house of his own, a wife, and children growing up to venerate him. Yes, and back of the house he would have a small garden, a place of meditation and repose, with goldfish in a tiny lakelet, and wind bells tinkling in the several trees, and there would be a high wall all around so that his meditation and repose should be undisturbed.

===Chinese===
阿秋听不懂法语。他坐在挤挤挨挨的法庭里，又疲倦又厌烦，听着这个官员一阵、那个官员一阵没完没了、爆豆似的法国话。在阿秋听来，这不过是满耳聒噪，他纳闷这些法国人怎么这样蠢，查了这么久还没查出杀钟嘎的凶手，而且压根儿就没查出来。种植园里五百名苦力都知道是阿三干下的，可这位阿三连抓都没抓。不错，全体苦力是私下串通过，互不作证；可是，事情这么简单，法国人本该能查出那人就是阿三。这些法国人真是蠢透了。
阿秋没有可以害怕的事。那桩凶杀他一丝一毫也没沾手。不错，案发时他在场，种植园的监工施默尔事后立刻冲进苦力棚屋，把他和另外四五个人一并堵在了里头；可那又怎么样？钟嘎身上只挨了两刀。五六个汉子断乎捅不出两个刀口。充其量，就算一个人只捅一刀，也至多是两个人干的。
阿秋就是这样盘算的，所以他和四个同伴就案发经过向法庭供话的时候，撒谎、支吾、搅浑水。他们听见了凶杀的动静，于是像施默尔一样，朝出事的地方跑了去。他们比施默尔先到——如此而已。诚然，施默尔作证说，他恰好路过，被争吵声吸引，在棚屋外头站了至少五分钟；随后他进去时，发现这几名被告已经在里面；而且他们不可能是刚进去的，因为他一直守着棚屋唯一的那扇门。可那又怎么样？阿秋和四个同案犯都作证说，施默尔记岔了。到头来他们总会放人的。这一点他们全都笃定。五个人的脑袋，总不至于为两个刀口搬家。再说，杀人的事没有一个洋鬼子亲眼看见。可这些法国人偏偏这么蠢。要在中国，阿秋知道得清楚，县官会把他们一个个送上大刑，真相立马就有了。在大刑底下，真相容易得很。可这些法国人不用刑——真是蠢上加蠢！所以他们永远查不出是谁杀了钟嘎。
可是这些事阿秋全不明白。拥有这座种植园的那家英国公司，是花了大本钱才把这五百名苦力运进塔希提的。股东们嚷嚷着要分红，公司却一个子儿还没分过；因此公司可不想让它花大价钱买来的契约劳工，开这种自相残杀的头。何况还有法国人，正巴不得把法国法律的种种美德与好处，加到中国佬（Chinago）头上。隔三差五立个榜样，再好不过；再说，新喀里多尼亚这块地方，若不是用来让人在那里苦熬岁月、以偿其为人的软弱之罪，又有什么用处呢？
这些阿秋都不懂。他坐在法庭里，等那份理不出头绪的判决早点下来，好放他和同伴们回种植园做工，把契约熬满。这判决很快就会下的。程序已近尾声，这个他看得出来。不再有人作证，不再有满堂聒噪。法国鬼子也乏了，显然也在等着宣判。等着等着，他回想起自己这一辈子，想起画押立契、上船来塔希提的那段日子。他海边村子里的年景艰难，当他把自己典出去、到南洋做五年工、一天挣五角墨西哥银元的时候，还自认走运。他村里有整年苦干才挣十块墨西哥银元的汉子，有成年累月打渔网才挣五块的女人，商铺人家里的使唤丫头，伺候一年才得四块。而他一天就能挣五角；只要一天，单单一天，就能挣下这笔王爷般的巨款！活计苦些又算什么？五年期满他就回老家——契约上写着的——从此再不用做工了。他会做一辈子富人，有自己的房子，有老婆，有儿女长大成人来孝敬他。对了，屋后还要有一方小园子，一处静心养性的所在，小小一方池塘里养金鱼，几株树上挂风铃叮咚，四周围一道高墙，好教他的静养不受搅扰。

===Original===
Well, he had worked out three of those five years. He was already a wealthy man (in his own country) through his earnings, and only two years more intervened between the cotton plantation on Tahiti and the meditation and repose that awaited him. But just now he was losing money because of the unfortunate accident of being present at the killing of Chung Ga. He had lain three weeks in prison, and for each day of those three weeks he had lost fifty cents. But now judgment would soon be given, and he would go back to work.
Ah Cho was twenty-two years old. He was happy and good-natured, and it was easy for him to smile. While his body was slim in the Asiatic way, his face was rotund. It was round, like the moon, and it irradiated a gentle complacence and a sweet kindliness of spirit that was unusual among his countrymen. Nor did his looks belie him. He never caused trouble, never took part in wrangling. He did not gamble. His soul was not harsh enough for the soul that must belong to a gambler. He was content with little things and simple pleasures. The hush and quiet in the cool of the day after the blazing toil in the cotton field was to him an infinite satisfaction. He could sit for hours gazing at a solitary flower and philosophizing about the mysteries and riddles of being. A blue heron on a tiny crescent of sandy beach, a silvery splatter of flying fish, or a sunset of pearl and rose across the lagoon, could entrance him to all forgetfulness of the procession of wearisome days and of the heavy lash of Schemmer.
Schemmer, Karl Schemmer, was a brute, a brutish brute. But he earned his salary. He got the last particle of strength out of the five hundred slaves; for slaves they were until their term of years was up. Schemmer worked hard to extract the strength from those five hundred sweating bodies and to transmute it into bales of fluffy cotton ready for export. His dominant, ironclad, primeval brutishness was what enabled him to effect the transmutation. Also, he was assisted by a thick leather belt, three inches wide and a yard in length, with which he always rode and which, on occasion, could come down on the naked back of a stooping coolie with a report like a pistol-shot. These reports were frequent when Schemmer rode down the furrowed field.
Once, at the beginning of the first year of contract labour, he had killed a coolie with a single blow of his fist. He had not exactly crushed the man’s head like an eggshell, but the blow had been sufficient to addle what was inside, and, after being sick for a week, the man had died. But the Chinese had not complained to the French devils that ruled over Tahiti. It was their own look out. Schemmer was their problem. They must avoid his wrath as they avoided the venom of the centipedes that lurked in the grass or crept into the sleeping quarters on rainy nights. The Chinagos﻿—such they were called by the indolent, brown-skinned island folk﻿—saw to it that they did not displease Schemmer too greatly. This was equivalent to rendering up to him a full measure of efficient toil. That blow of Schemmer’s fist had been worth thousands of dollars to the Company, and no trouble ever came of it to Schemmer.
The French, with no instinct for colonization, futile in their childish playgame of developing the resources of the island, were only too glad to see the English Company succeed. What matter of Schemmer and his redoubtable fist? The Chinago that died? Well, he was only a Chinago. Besides, he died of sunstroke, as the doctor’s certificate attested. True, in all the history of Tahiti no one had ever died of sunstroke. But it was that, precisely that, which made the death of this Chinago unique. The doctor said as much in his report. He was very candid. Dividends must be paid, or else one more failure would be added to the long history of failure in Tahiti.

===Chinese===
好嘛，五年里他已经熬过了三年。凭挣下的钱，他已经算个财主了（在他本国而言），横在塔希提的棉花种植园和他等着享的那份清福之间的，只剩两年。只是眼下他正亏着钱，谁叫他不巧在场看了钟嘎被杀。他蹲了三个星期大牢，这三个星期的每一天，他都亏五角。可这会儿判决快下来了，他就快回去做工了。
阿秋二十二岁。他快活，和气，天生爱笑。身子是亚洲式的清瘦，脸却圆。圆得像月亮，焕发着一种自得其乐的温厚，一种甜和的善意，在他的同胞中间都算少见。他的相貌也不骗人。他从不惹事，从不吵架拌嘴。他不赌钱。他的性子还没有硬到配得上一副赌徒的心肠。小处知足，简朴的快活，他就能安生。在棉花地里顶着烈日烤了一天之后，傍晚的清凉和寂静，于他就是无边的享受。他能几个钟头坐着，凝望一朵孤花，参详生生死死的玄机和哑谜。蓝鹭立在一弯小小的沙嘴上，飞鱼溅起一片银花，或者环礁湖那头一抹珠色和蔷薇色的落日，都能把他看痴了，忘掉苦日子一天天排着队走过来，忘掉施默尔那条狠鞭子。
施默尔，卡尔·施默尔，是一头畜生，一头如假包换的畜生。可他对得起他的薪水。他能从那五百名奴隶身上榨出最后一丝力气；在年满之前，他们就是奴隶。施默尔拼命苦干，从那五百具汗淋淋的躯体里榨取力气，把它变成一包包松软的、等着出口的棉花。他那种盛气凌人、铁打一般的、原始的畜性，正是他完成这桩转化的本钱。此外他还得着一根厚皮带帮忙，三寸宽，一码长，他人到哪儿皮带跟到哪儿，赶上时机，它能落到弯腰苦力的光脊梁上，脆响得像一声枪响。施默尔纵马巡过犁沟纵横的田地时，这样的枪响此起彼伏。
头一年契约工开头的当儿，他一拳头打死过一个苦力。倒不真是像捏碎蛋壳那样把那人脑袋砸瘪了，可那一拳足够把里头的东西晃成浆子，那人病了一个星期，死了。可中国人没有向统治塔希提的法国鬼子告状。这是他们自己的事。施默尔是他们的难处，他们得躲他的凶焰，就像躲草棵里伏着的、雨夜里爬进卧棚的蜈蚣的毒。中国佬——那些懒散的棕皮肤岛民这样称呼他们——都拿得稳一件事：别把施默尔招惹得太狠。这也就等于给他十足十地交足卖力的活计。施默尔那一拳，给公司挣了几千块钱，他自己却连一点麻烦都没惹上。
法国人天生没有殖民的本事，开发海岛资源的那套小孩子把戏也玩不出名堂，正乐得见英国公司办得兴旺。施默尔和他那只了不得的拳头算得了什么？死了的那个中国佬？嗐，不过是个中国佬。再说，他是死于日射病，医生的证明书写得明白。诚然，塔希提有史以来从没有人死于日射病。可偏偏就是这个，正是这个，才叫这个中国佬的死独一无二。医生在报告里也是这么写的。他坦白得很。红利总得派发，不然塔希提那一长串失败的编年史上，又得添上一桩。

===Original===
There was no understanding these white devils. Ah Cho pondered their inscrutableness as he sat in the court room waiting the judgment. There was no telling what went on at the back of their minds. He had seen a few of the white devils. They were all alike﻿—the officers and sailors on the ship, the French officials, the several white men on the plantation, including Schemmer. Their minds all moved in mysterious ways there was no getting at. They grew angry without apparent cause, and their anger was always dangerous. They were like wild beasts at such times. They worried about little things, and on occasion could out-toil even a Chinago. They were not temperate as Chinagos were temperate; they were gluttons, eating prodigiously and drinking more prodigiously. A Chinago never knew when an act would please them or arouse a storm of wrath. A Chinago could never tell. What pleased one time, the very next time might provoke an outburst of anger. There was a curtain behind the eyes of the white devils that screened the backs of their minds from the Chinago’s gaze. And then, on top of it all, was that terrible efficiency of the white devils, that ability to do things, to make things go, to work results, to bend to their wills all creeping, crawling things, and the powers of the very elements themselves. Yes, the white men were strange and wonderful, and they were devils. Look at Schemmer.
Ah Cho wondered why the judgment was so long in forming. Not a man on trial had laid hand on Chung Ga. Ah San alone had killed him. Ah San had done it, bending Chung Ga’s head back with one hand by a grip of his queue, and with the other hand, from behind, reaching over and driving the knife into his body. Twice had he driven it in. There in the court room, with closed eyes, Ah Cho saw the killing acted over again﻿—the squabble, the vile words bandied back and forth, the filth and insult flung upon venerable ancestors, the curses laid upon unbegotten generations, the leap of Ah San, the grip on the queue of Chung Ga, the knife that sank twice into his flesh, the bursting open of the door, the irruption of Schemmer, the dash for the door, the escape of Ah San, the flying belt of Schemmer that drove the rest into the corner, and the firing of the revolver as a signal that brought help to Schemmer. Ah Cho shivered as he lived it over. One blow of the belt had bruised his cheek, taking off some of the skin. Schemmer had pointed to the bruises when, on the witness-stand, he had identified Ah Cho. It was only just now that the marks had become no longer visible. That had been a blow. Half an inch nearer the centre and it would have taken out his eye. Then Ah Cho forgot the whole happening in a vision he caught of the garden of meditation and repose that would be his when he returned to his own land.
He sat with impassive face, while the magistrate rendered the judgment. Likewise were the faces of his four companions impassive. And they remained impassive when the interpreter explained that the five of them had been found guilty of the murder of Chung Ga, and that Ah Chow should have his head cut off, Ah Cho serve twenty years in prison in New Caledonia, Wong Li twelve years, and Ah Tong ten years. There was no use in getting excited about it. Even Ah Chow remained expressionless as a mummy, though it was his head that was to be cut off. The magistrate added a few words, and the interpreter explained that Ah Chow’s face having been most severely bruised by Schemmer’s strap had made his identification so positive that, since one man must die, he might as well be that man. Also, the fact that Ah Cho’s face likewise had been severely bruised, conclusively proving his presence at the murder and his undoubted participation, had merited him the twenty years of penal servitude. And down to the ten years of Ah Tong, the proportioned reason for each sentence was explained. Let the Chinagos take the lesson to heart, the Court said finally, for they must learn that the law would be fulfilled in Tahiti though the heavens fell.
The five Chinagos were taken back to jail. They were not shocked nor grieved. The sentences being unexpected was quite what they were accustomed to in their dealings with the white devils. From them a Chinago rarely expected more than the unexpected. The heavy punishment for a crime they had not committed was no stranger than the countless strange things that white devils did. In the weeks that followed, Ah Cho often contemplated Ah Chow with mild curiosity. His head was to be cut off by the guillotine that was being erected on the plantation. For him there would be no declining years, no gardens of tranquillity. Ah Cho philosophized and speculated about life and death. As for himself, he was not perturbed. Twenty years were merely twenty years. By that much was his garden removed from him﻿—that was all. He was young, and the patience of Asia was in his bones. He could wait those twenty years, and by that time the heats of his blood would be assuaged and he would be better fitted for that garden of calm delight. He thought of a name for it; he would call it The Garden of the Morning Calm. He was made happy all day by the thought, and he was inspired to devise a moral maxim on the virtue of patience, which maxim proved a great comfort, especially to Wong Li and Ah Tong. Ah Chow, however, did not care for the maxim. His head was to be separated from his body in so short a time that he had no need for patience to wait for that event. He smoked well, ate well, slept well, and did not worry about the slow passage of time.
Cruchot was a gendarme. He had seen twenty years of service in the colonies, from Nigeria and Senegal to the South Seas, and those twenty years had not perceptibly brightened his dull mind. He was as slow-witted and stupid as in his peasant days in the south of France. He knew discipline and fear of authority, and from God down to the sergeant of gendarmes the only difference to him was the measure of slavish obedience which he rendered. In point of fact, the sergeant bulked bigger in his mind than God, except on Sundays when God’s mouthpieces had their say. God was usually very remote, while the sergeant was ordinarily very close at hand.

===Chinese===
这些白鬼子没法子懂。阿秋坐在法庭里等判决，一面捉摸他们的高深莫测。他们脑子后头转的什么念头，谁也说不透。白鬼子他见过几个，全是一路货——船上的官员和水手，法国的官老爷，种植园上的几个白人，连施默尔算在内。他们的心思全走的是些神秘道道，够不着，摸不到。他们没来由地动怒，一动怒就危险。那时候他们跟野兽一个样。他们为鸡毛蒜皮犯愁，赶起活来，有时连中国佬都赛不过。他们不像中国佬那样有节度；他们是饕餮，吃得吓人，喝得更吓人。一个举动，这一回讨了白鬼子的欢心，下一回没准就惹来一场雷霆。中国佬永远拿不准。白鬼子眼睛后头挂着一幅帘子，把他们脑子后头遮得严严的，不叫中国人的目光看进去。而最了不得的，还是白鬼子那种可怕的能耐——办事的能耐，让事情转起来的能耐，做出名堂来的能耐，叫一切爬的蠕的东西、连五行八作的自然力本身，都俯首听命的能耐。是啊，白人又古怪又神通，他们是鬼。瞧瞧施默尔。
阿秋不明白，判决为什么这样难产。受审的人没有一个碰过钟嘎一根指头。单是阿三一个人杀了他。阿三干得干净利落：一只手揪住钟嘎的辫子，把他的头往后一扳，另一只手从背后绕过去，把刀子捅进他身子。捅了两刀。就在这法庭上，阿秋闭上眼，又把那桩凶杀从头看了一遍——口角，难听的话你来我往，脏水泼到列祖列宗头上，咒骂罚到没出世的子子孙孙身上，阿三猛地一扑，揪住钟嘎的辫子，刀子两回没进他的肉，门轰地撞开，施默尔冲进来，众人夺门而逃，阿三脱身走脱，施默尔的皮带横飞，把余下的人抽进墙角，左轮枪朝天一响，召来帮手救应施默尔。阿秋重新过了一遍，浑身发抖。皮带有一抽擦上他的脸颊，揭掉一层皮。施默尔在证人席上指认阿秋的时候，指的就是这些伤痕。到方才，那些印子才算看不出来了。好一抽。再往当中偏半寸，他这只眼睛就没了。想到这儿，阿秋把整桩事忘了，眼前浮起他回乡之后将有的那座静心园。
法官宣判的时候，他面无表情地坐着。四个同伴也一样面无表情。翻译解说的时候，他们依旧不动声色：五人谋杀钟嘎罪名成立，阿周斩首，阿秋发配新喀里多尼亚服苦役二十年，王利十二年，阿通十年。这事犯不上激动。连阿周都木然得像具木乃伊，尽管掉脑袋的正是他。法官又补了几句，翻译解说道：阿周的脸叫施默尔的皮鞭抽伤得最重，指认因此最确凿，反正总得有一个人偿命，那不如就是他。再者，阿秋的脸同样抽伤得不轻，足证他案发在场、断然参与，这才挣下这二十年苦役。一路数到阿通的十年，每一桩判决的分量都解说了个明白。法庭最后说，中国佬们要把这个教训记在心里，叫他们知道，哪怕天塌下来，塔希提这儿的法律也要办到底。
五名中国佬被押回牢里。他们不震惊，也不哀恸。判决出人意料，这原是他们同白鬼子打交道教惯了的。从白鬼子身上，一个中国佬难得指望什么，只指望意外。没犯的罪领了重罚，也不比白鬼子干出的千奇百怪更稀奇。往后的几个星期里，阿秋常常怀着淡淡的猎奇打量阿周。他的脑袋要给断头机切下来，那机器正在种植园上搭起来。他不会再有迟暮之年，不会再有清静的园子。阿秋参详起生死来。至于他自己，他倒不慌。二十年无非二十年。他的园子不过隔着这么些年头——仅此而已。他年轻，亚洲人的耐心长在他骨头里。他等得起这二十年，到那时他血里的燥热也凉了，正合适进那座安然受用的园子。他想好了园子的名字，就叫“晨静园”。这念头叫他快活了一整天，还引得他编出一条讲忍耐之德的格言来。这条格言大见用处，尤其宽慰了王利和阿通。唯独阿周不稀罕这条格言。他的脑袋同身子分家，就在旦夕之间，犯不上再拿出耐性去等。他烟抽得好，饭吃得好，觉睡得好，全不操心日子走得慢。
克吕肖是个宪兵。他在各殖民地当差二十年，从尼日利亚、塞内加尔一路到南洋，那二十年也没能把他那颗愚钝的脑子磨亮几分。他还像在法兰西南部当庄稼汉的年月一样，迟钝、愚鲁。他懂的只有军纪和对官长的惧怕，从上帝一路到宪兵军士，在他眼里差别只在奉承奴颜的分寸多寡。说实在的，宪兵军士在他心里比上帝还魁伟，只除了礼拜天，上帝的代言人们开口的时候。上帝向来远在天边，军士却向来近在眼前。

===Original===
Cruchot it was who received the order from the Chief Justice to the jailer commanding that functionary to deliver over to Cruchot the person of Ah Chow. Now, it happened that the Chief Justice had given a dinner the night before to the captain and officers of the French man-of-war. His hand was shaking when he wrote out the order, and his eyes were aching so dreadfully that he did not read over the order. It was only a Chinago’s life he was signing away, anyway. So he did not notice that he had omitted the final letter in Ah Chow’s name. The order read “Ah Cho,” and, when Cruchot presented the order, the jailer turned over to him the person of Ah Cho. Cruchot took that person beside him on the seat of a wagon, behind two mules, and drove away.
Ah Cho was glad to be out in the sunshine. He sat beside the gendarme and beamed. He beamed more ardently than ever when he noted the mules headed south toward Atimaono. Undoubtedly Schemmer had sent for him to be brought back. Schemmer wanted him to work. Very well, he would work well. Schemmer would never have cause to complain. It was a hot day. There had been a stoppage of the trades. The mules sweated, Cruchot sweated, and Ah Cho sweated. But it was Ah Cho that bore the heat with the least concern. He had toiled three years under that sun on the plantation. He beamed and beamed with such genial good nature that even Cruchot’s heavy mind was stirred to wonderment.
“You are very funny,” he said at last.
Ah Cho nodded and beamed more ardently. Unlike the magistrate, Cruchot spoke to him in the Kanaka tongue, and this, like all Chinagos and all foreign devils, Ah Cho understood.
“You laugh too much,” Cruchot chided. “One’s heart should be full of tears on a day like this.”

===Chinese===
接首席法官给狱监手令的差事，落在克吕肖头上，手令命那一位把阿周这名人犯移交克吕肖。偏巧，首席法官前一晚设宴，招待法国军舰的舰长和军官们。他写手令的时候手直抖，两眼又疼得厉害，写完也没再看一遍。反正签掉的不过是一条中国佬的命。于是他没有留意，“阿周”（Ah Chow）名字末尾的一个字母给漏掉了。手令上写的是“阿秋”（Ah Cho），克吕肖拿手令去提人，狱监便把阿秋这名人犯交到了他手上。克吕肖让这人犯在骡车车座上挨着自己坐下，赶着两头骡子走了。
阿秋乐得回到太阳底下。他挨着宪兵坐，满脸堆笑。待看清两头骡子朝南、奔阿蒂玛奥诺去，他笑得更热络了。不用说，是施默尔派人接他回去的。施默尔要他做工。好啊，他会好好做。保管施默尔挑不出错来。天气热。信风停了。骡子出汗，克吕肖出汗，阿秋也出汗。可数阿秋最不把暑热当回事。他在这日头底下的种植园里苦了三年。他笑啊笑啊，和气得连克吕肖那颗笨脑子都动了好奇。
“你这个人真有意思，”他终于开口。
阿秋点点头，笑得更热络了。同法官不一样，克吕肖跟他说的是卡纳卡土话，这种话，同所有的中国佬、所有的洋鬼子一样，阿秋是听得懂的。
“你笑得太多了，”克吕肖数落他，“赶上今天这样的日子，人心里该装满眼泪才是。”

===Original===
“I am glad to get out of the jail.”
“Is that all?” The gendarme shrugged his shoulders.
“Is it not enough?” was the retort.
“Then you are not glad to have your head cut off?”
Ah Cho looked at him in abrupt perplexity, and said﻿—

===Chinese===
“出了牢房，我高兴。”
“就为这个？”宪兵耸了耸肩膀。
“这还不够吗？”这一位回敬。
“这么说，脑袋要给砍下来，你倒不高兴？”
阿秋猛地一脸茫然，望着他说——

===Original===
“Why, I am going back to Atimaono to work on the plantation for Schemmer. Are you not taking me to Atimaono?”
Cruchot stroked his long moustaches reflectively. “Well, well,” he said finally, with a flick of the whip at the off mule, “so you don’t know?”
“Know what?” Ah Cho was beginning to feel a vague alarm. “Won’t Schemmer let me work for him any more?”
“Not after today.” Cruchot laughed heartily. It was a good joke. “You see, you won’t be able to work after today. A man with his head off can’t work, eh?” He poked the Chinago in the ribs, and chuckled.
Ah Cho maintained silence while the mules trotted a hot mile. Then he spoke: “Is Schemmer going to cut off my head?”

===Chinese===
“咦，我这是回阿蒂玛奥诺，回种植园替施默尔做工呀。你不是送我去阿蒂玛奥诺吗？”
克吕肖若有所思地捋着两撇长髭。“咳，咳，”他终于说，鞭梢朝外档那头骡子虚抽了一下，“敢情你还不知道？”
“知道什么？”阿秋心里起初发懵，渐渐慌起来。“施默尔不肯再叫我给他做工了？”
“过了今天就不成了。”克吕肖放声大笑，这笑话够味。“你瞧，过了今天你就做不成工啦。脑袋掉了的人是做不了工的，嗯？”他伸手指戳戳中国佬的肋条，自己直乐。
骡子热腾腾地小跑了一英里，阿秋一路没作声。然后他开了口：“是要施默尔来砍我的脑袋吗？”

===Original===
Cruchot grinned as he nodded.
“It is a mistake,” said Ah Cho, gravely. “I am not the Chinago that is to have his head cut off. I am Ah Cho. The honourable judge has determined that I am to stop twenty years in New Caledonia.”
The gendarme laughed. It was a good joke, this funny Chinago trying to cheat the guillotine. The mules trotted through a coconut grove and for half a mile beside the sparkling sea before Ah Cho spoke again.
“I tell you I am not Ah Chow. The honourable judge did not say that my head was to go off.”
“Don’t be afraid,” said Cruchot, with the philanthropic intention of making it easier for his prisoner. “It is not difficult to die that way.” He snapped his fingers. “It is quick﻿—like that. It is not like hanging on the end of a rope and kicking and making faces for five minutes. It is like killing a chicken with a hatchet. You cut its head off, that is all. And it is the same with a man. Pouf!﻿—it is over. It doesn’t hurt. You don’t even think it hurts. You don’t think. Your head is gone, so you cannot think. It is very good. That is the way I want to die﻿—quick, ah, quick. You are lucky to die that way. You might get the leprosy and fall to pieces slowly, a finger at a time, and now and again a thumb, also the toes. I knew a man who was burned by hot water. It took him two days to die. You could hear him yelling a kilometre away. But you? Ah! so easy! Chck!﻿—the knife cuts your neck like that. It is finished. The knife may even tickle. Who can say? Nobody who died that way ever came back to say.”

===Chinese===
克吕肖咧嘴笑着点头。
“这是弄错了，”阿秋正色道，“要砍头的不是我这个人。我是阿秋。法官大老爷判的，是我去新喀里多尼亚蹲二十年。”
宪兵哈哈大笑。真是好笑话，这个滑稽的中国佬，竟想赖掉断头机。骡子小跑着穿过一片椰林，又沿着波光潋滟的大海走了半英里，阿秋才再开口。
“我告诉你，我不是阿周。法官大老爷没有说过，我的脑袋要搬家。”
“别害怕，”克吕肖说，存着一片善心，想让他的囚人好受些。“那样死不费难。”他捻了个响指。“快——就这么一下。不像吊在绳子梢上，蹬腿歪脸折腾五分钟。就像拿柴刀杀鸡，剁掉脑袋，完了。人也一样。噗！——就了啦。不疼。你连想疼都来不及想。你不用想了。脑袋没了，想不动了。好得很。我就想这么个死法——快，啊，快。你这样死是造化。没准你得麻风病，慢慢地一块块掉，先掉指头，一根一根来，时不时掉个大拇指，脚趾也掉。我认得一个人，叫开水烫了，整整两天才死，一公里外都听得见他嚎。可你呢？啊！多么省事！咔！——刀切你脖子，就这么一下。完了。刀说不定还痒痒呢。谁说得准？那样死掉的人，没有一个回来说过。”

===Original===
He considered this last an excruciating joke, and permitted himself to be convulsed with laughter for half a minute. Part of his mirth was assumed, but he considered it his humane duty to cheer up the Chinago.
“But I tell you I am Ah Cho,” the other persisted. “I don’t want my head cut off.”
Cruchot scowled. The Chinago was carrying the foolishness too far.
“I am not Ah Chow﻿—” Ah Cho began.

===Chinese===
他把末了那句当成绝妙的笑话，由着自己抽风般笑了半分钟。笑有一半是装的，可他觉得，宽宽中国佬的心，是他一桩人道义务。
“可我告诉你，我是阿秋，”那一个还不死心，“我不想叫人砍脑袋。”
克吕肖沉下脸来。这中国佬浑闹得没边了。
“我不是阿周——”阿秋刚开口。

===Original===
“That will do,” the gendarme interrupted. He puffed up his cheeks and strove to appear fierce.
“I tell you I am not﻿—” Ah Cho began again.
“Shut up!” bawled Cruchot.
After that they rode along in silence. It was twenty miles from Papeete to Atimaono, and over half the distance was covered by the time the Chinago again ventured into speech.
“I saw you in the court room, when the honourable judge sought after our guilt,” he began. “Very good. And do you remember that Ah Chow, whose head is to be cut off﻿—do you remember that he﻿—Ah Chow﻿—was a tall man? Look at me.”

===Chinese===
“够了，”宪兵打断他。他鼓起腮帮子，竭力装出凶相。
“我告诉你我不是——”阿秋又开口。
“闭嘴！”克吕肖大吼。
此后两人闷头赶路。从帕皮提到阿蒂玛奥诺二十英里，走完一多半，中国佬才敢再试着开口。
“法庭上，法官大老爷查问我们罪过的时候，我见过你，”他开腔道，“很好。你还记得阿周吗，就是脑袋要给砍掉的那个——你还记得他——阿周——是个高个子吗？瞧瞧我。”

===Original===
He stood up suddenly, and Cruchot saw that he was a short man. And just as suddenly Cruchot caught a glimpse of a memory picture of Ah Chow, and in that picture Ah Chow was tall. To the gendarme all Chinagos looked alike. One face was like another. But between tallness and shortness he could differentiate, and he knew that he had the wrong man beside him on the seat. He pulled up the mules abruptly, so that the pole shot ahead of them, elevating their collars.
“You see, it was a mistake,” said Ah Cho, smiling pleasantly.
But Cruchot was thinking. Already he regretted that he had stopped the wagon. He was unaware of the error of the Chief Justice, and he had no way of working it out; but he did know that he had been given this Chinago to take to Atimaono and that it was his duty to take him to Atimaono. What if he was the wrong man and they cut his head off? It was only a Chinago when all was said, and what was a Chinago, anyway? Besides, it might not be a mistake. He did not know what went on in the minds of his superiors. They knew their business best. Who was he to do their thinking for them? Once, in the long ago, he had attempted to think for them, and the sergeant had said: “Cruchot, you are a fool! The quicker you know that, the better you will get on. You are not to think; you are to obey and leave thinking to your betters.” He smarted under the recollection. Also, if he turned back to Papeete, he would delay the execution at Atimaono, and if he were wrong in turning back, he would get a reprimand from the sergeant who was waiting for the prisoner. And, furthermore, he would get a reprimand at Papeete as well.
He touched the mules with the whip and drove on. He looked at his watch. He would be half an hour late as it was, and the sergeant was bound to be angry. He put the mules into a faster trot. The more Ah Cho persisted in explaining the mistake, the more stubborn Cruchot became. The knowledge that he had the wrong man did not make his temper better. The knowledge that it was through no mistake of his confirmed him in the belief that the wrong he was doing was the right. And, rather than incur the displeasure of the sergeant, he would willingly have assisted a dozen wrong Chinagos to their doom.
As for Ah Cho, after the gendarme had struck him over the head with the butt of the whip and commanded him in a loud voice to shut up, there remained nothing for him to do but to shut up. The long ride continued in silence. Ah Cho pondered the strange ways of the foreign devils. There was no explaining them. What they were doing with him was of a piece with everything they did. First they found guilty five innocent men, and next they cut off the head of the man that even they, in their benighted ignorance, had deemed meritorious of no more than twenty years’ imprisonment. And there was nothing he could do. He could only sit idly and take what these lords of life measured out to him. Once, he got in a panic, and the sweat upon his body turned cold; but he fought his way out of it. He endeavoured to resign himself to his fate by remembering and repeating certain passages from the Yin Chih Wen; but, instead, he kept seeing his dream-garden of meditation and repose. This bothered him, until he abandoned himself to the dream and sat in his garden listening to the tinkling of the windbells in the several trees. And lo! sitting thus, in the dream, he was able to remember and repeat the passages from *The Tract of the Quiet Way*.

===Chinese===
他霍地站起来，克吕肖这才看清他是个矮个子。也是这么霍地一下，克吕肖脑海里闪过一幅旧画，画里的阿周是个高个子。在宪兵眼里，中国佬长得全一个样，这张脸同那张脸没分别。可高矮之间他还是分得清的，他知道身边车座上坐的是个错拿了的人。他猛地勒住骡子，车杆一下窜到骡子前头去，骡子的颈圈都翘了起来。
“你瞧，是弄错了，”阿秋说，笑得可亲。
可克吕肖在动脑子。他已后悔停了车。首席法官出的差错他毫不知情，也无从推想出来；可他确实知道，上头把这一个中国佬交给他，叫他送去阿蒂玛奥诺，把人送到阿蒂玛奥诺就是他的差事。万一送错了人，砍了脑袋呢？说到底不过是个中国佬，中国佬又算个什么呢？再说，也未必是错。上官们心里打的什么算盘，他不知道。他们自有他们的道理。他算老几，替他们动脑子？多年前有一回，他试着替他们动过脑子，军士说：“克吕肖，你是个蠢货！这话你越早明白，越早得意。轮不着你想；你只管服从，动脑子的事留给你的上头。”想起这事他脸上还火辣辣的。再说，要是掉头回帕皮提，阿蒂玛奥诺那边的行刑就得耽搁，万一回头是回错了，等着收人犯的那位军士就得训他。不但如此，回到帕皮提他还得再挨一顿训。
他扬鞭赶着骡子往前走。他看了看表。照眼下这么走也要迟到半个钟头，军士必定动气。他叫骡子放开小跑。阿秋越是分辩这是场差错，克吕肖越是犟。明明知道自己拿错了人，他的火气也好不了。偏偏这差错又不是自己出的，倒叫他铁了心相信：他正在办的这桩错事，就是对的。与其招军士不痛快，他情愿亲手把一打错拿的中国佬送上死路。
至于阿秋，宪兵拿鞭子把儿在他头上敲了一记，又大喝一声叫他闭嘴之后，他剩下的路只有闭嘴。长路在沉默里走下去。阿秋捉摸洋鬼子这些个古怪行径。没法子解释。他们摆布他的这一手，同他们样样行径是一路的。先是判了五个清白的人有罪，跟着又要砍掉一个人的脑袋，连他们自己，在他们昏天黑地的愚昧里，也才判了这个人顶多二十年监禁。而他毫无办法。他只能干坐着，领受这些生命的主子量给他的份。有一阵他慌了神，身上的汗都凉了；可他自己从慌乱里挣了出来。他试着认命，默诵《阴骘文》里的章句；可诵来诵去，眼前老是那座梦里的静心园。这叫他烦乱，索性由着梦去，坐在园子里，听几株树上风铃叮咚。哈，这样坐在梦里，*《阴骘文》*的章句倒能一句句想起来、诵出来了。

===Original===
So the time passed nicely until Atimaono was reached and the mules trotted up to the foot of the scaffold, in the shade of which stood the impatient sergeant. Ah Cho was hurried up the ladder of the scaffold. Beneath him on one side he saw assembled all the coolies of the plantation. Schemmer had decided that the event would be a good object-lesson, and so he called in the coolies from the fields and compelled them to be present. As they caught sight of Ah Cho they gabbled among themselves in low voices. They saw the mistake; but they kept it to themselves. The inexplicable white devils had doubtlessly changed their minds. Instead of taking the life of one innocent man, they were taking the life of another innocent man. Ah Chow or Ah Cho﻿—what did it matter which? They could never understand the white dogs any more than could the white dogs understand them. Ah Cho was going to have his head cut off, but they, when their two remaining years of servitude were up, were going back to China.
Schemmer had made the guillotine himself. He was a handy man, and though he had never seen a guillotine, the French officials had explained the principle to him. It was on his suggestion that they had ordered the execution to take place at Atimaono instead of at Papeete. The scene of the crime, Schemmer had argued, was the best possible place for the punishment, and, in addition, it would have a salutary influence upon the half-thousand Chinagos on the plantation. Schemmer had also volunteered to act as executioner, and in that capacity he was now on the scaffold, experimenting with the instrument he had made. A banana tree, of the size and consistency of a man’s neck, lay under the guillotine. Ah Cho watched with fascinated eyes. The German, turning a small crank, hoisted the blade to the top of the little derrick he had rigged. A jerk on a stout piece of cord loosed the blade and it dropped with a flash, neatly severing the banana trunk.
“How does it work?” The sergeant, coming out on top the scaffold, had asked the question.
“Beautifully,” was Schemmer’s exultant answer. “Let me show you.”
Again he turned the crank that hoisted the blade, jerked the cord, and sent the blade crashing down on the soft tree. But this time it went no more than two-thirds of the way through.

===Chinese===
日子就这样怪好过地捱到了阿蒂玛奥诺，骡车小跑着赶到刑台底下，台子的阴凉里站着那位等得不耐烦的军士。阿秋被急急押上刑台的梯子。往下一边望，种植园的苦力全叫到了。施默尔认定这桩事是现成的杀一儆百，索性把苦力从地里召回来，逼他们到场。众人一眼看见阿秋，便交头接耳低声嘀咕。他们看出了差错，可他们闷在心里不说。这些没法捉摸的白鬼子，八成又改了主意。不取一条无辜的命，改取另一条无辜的命。是阿周还是阿秋——横竖有什么两样？他们永远摸不透这些白狗，正如白狗也永远摸不透他们。阿秋的脑袋要给砍下来了，可他们，熬满剩下的两年苦工，就要回中国去了。
断头机是施默尔自己造的。他手巧，虽说从没见过断头机，法国官员们给他讲明白了原理。就是依着他的主意，行刑地点才定在阿蒂玛奥诺，不在帕皮提。施默尔论证说，罪案发生之地，正是行刑的最好地方；再者，对种植园上那五百个中国佬，还有惩一儆百的好处。施默尔还自告奋勇充当刽子手，此刻他便以这个身份站在刑台上，试他造出来的这件家伙。断头机底下横着一截香蕉树，粗细软硬都同人的脖子相仿。阿秋看得眼睛发直。那德国人摇着一柄小曲柄，把铡刀吊上他架起来的那副小吊架顶。猛一拽一根粗绳，铡刀脱钩落下，寒光一闪，齐齐切断香蕉树干。
“灵不灵？”军士走上刑台顶，问了一句。
“灵极了，”施默尔得意扬扬地答，“我试给你看。”
他又摇起曲柄吊上铡刀，一拽绳子，铡刀轰然砸在那截软树上。可这一回，只进去了三分之二。

===Original===
The sergeant scowled. “That will not serve,” he said.
Schemmer wiped the sweat from his forehead. “What it needs is more weight,” he announced. Walking up to the edge of the scaffold, he called his orders to the blacksmith for a twenty-five-pound piece of iron. As he stooped over to attach the iron to the broad top of the blade, Ah Cho glanced at the sergeant and saw his opportunity.
“The honourable judge said that Ah Chow was to have his head cut off,” he began.
The sergeant nodded impatiently. He was thinking of the fifteen-mile ride before him that afternoon, to the windward side of the island, and of Berthe, the pretty half-caste daughter of Lafiere, the pearl-trader, who was waiting for him at the end of it.
“Well, I am not Ah Chow. I am Ah Cho. The honourable jailer has made a mistake. Ah Chow is a tall man, and you see I am short.”

===Chinese===
军士皱起眉头。“这可不行。”他说。
施默尔抹了把额头上的汗。“缺的是分量。”他宣布。走到刑台边上，他扬声吩咐铁匠打一块二十五磅的铁。他弯腰把铁块往铡刀宽宽的刀背上拴，阿秋瞟了军士一眼，瞧准了机会。
“法官大老爷判的是，阿周的脑袋要给砍掉，”他开腔道。
军士不耐烦地点点头。他心里想着午后还有十五英里的路程，要赶到岛的向风那一边去，想着拉菲尔——采珠商人拉菲尔——他家那个漂亮的混血女儿贝尔特，正在路程的尽头等他。
“可是，我不是阿周。我是阿秋。大老爷狱监弄错了。阿周是个高个子，您瞧，我是个矮子。”

===Original===
The sergeant looked at him hastily and saw the mistake. “Schemmer!” he called, imperatively. “Come here.”
The German grunted, but remained bent over his task till the chunk of iron was lashed to his satisfaction. “Is your Chinago ready?” he demanded.
“Look at him,” was the answer. “Is he the Chinago?”
Schemmer was surprised. He swore tersely for a few seconds, and looked regretfully across at the thing he had made with his own hands and which he was eager to see work. “Look here,” he said finally, “we can’t postpone this affair. I’ve lost three hours’ work already out of those five hundred Chinagos. I can’t afford to lose it all over again for the right man. Let’s put the performance through just the same. It is only a Chinago.”
The sergeant remembered the long ride before him, and the pearl-trader’s daughter, and debated with himself.

===Chinese===
军士匆匆一看，看出差错来了。“施默尔！”他喝道，“过来。”
德国人哼了一声，可仍旧弯着腰干活，直到那块铁捆扎得他称心满意。“你的中国佬备好了吗？”他问。
“你看看他，”回答是，“他是那个中国佬吗？”
施默尔吃了一惊。他干巴巴地咒骂了几秒钟，又恋恋不舍地望了望那件家伙——他亲手造出来、巴不得看它开张的家伙。“听我说，”他终于道，“这事不能改期。那五百个中国佬的活，我已经搭进去三个钟头了。再为一个对的人重搭一遍，我赔不起。戏照旧唱完吧，不过是个中国佬。”
军士想起自己面前那趟远路，想起采珠商人的女儿，心里盘算起来。

===Original===
“They will blame it on Cruchot﻿—if it is discovered,” the German urged. “But there’s little chance of its being discovered. Ah Chow won’t give it away, at any rate.”
“The blame won’t lie with Cruchot, anyway,” the sergeant said. “It must have been the jailer’s mistake.”
“Then let’s go on with it. They can’t blame us. Who can tell one Chinago from another? We can say that we merely carried out instructions with the Chinago that was turned over to us. Besides, I really can’t take all those coolies a second time away from their labour.”
They spoke in French, and Ah Cho, who did not understand a word of it, nevertheless knew that they were determining his destiny. He knew, also, that the decision rested with the sergeant, and he hung upon that official’s lips.
“All right,” announced the sergeant. “Go ahead with it. He is only a Chinago.”

===Chinese===
“真查出来，他们会推到克吕肖头上，”德国人撺掇道，“可查出来的机会小得很。反正阿周是绝不会说漏的。”
“反正罪过落不到克吕肖头上，”军士说，“必是狱监弄错了。”
“那就照旧办吧。怪不到咱们头上。谁分得清这个中国佬、那个中国佬？咱们只消说，交到咱们手里的是哪个中国佬，咱们就照章办了哪个。再说，叫我再把那帮苦力从活计里拽出来第二回，实在办不到。”
他们说的是法语，阿秋一个字也听不懂，却知道他们正在定他的生死。他也知道，主意捏在军士手里，便眼巴巴地盯着那位官长的嘴唇。
“行，”军士宣布，“照办。不过是个中国佬。”

===Original===
“I’m going to try it once more, just to make sure.” Schemmer moved the banana trunk forward under the knife, which he had hoisted to the top of the derrick.
Ah Cho tried to remember maxims from “The Tract of the Quiet Way.” “Live in concord,” came to him; but it was not applicable. He was not going to live. He was about to die. No, that would not do. “Forgive malice”﻿—yes, but there was no malice to forgive. Schemmer and the rest were doing this thing without malice. It was to them merely a piece of work that had to be done, just as clearing the jungle, ditching the water, and planting cotton were pieces of work that had to be done. Schemmer jerked the cord, and Ah Cho forgot “The Tract of the Quiet Way.” The knife shot down with a thud, making a clean slice of the tree.
“Beautiful!” exclaimed the sergeant, pausing in the act of lighting a cigarette. “Beautiful, my friend.”
Schemmer was pleased at the praise.
“Come on, Ah Chow,” he said, in the Tahitian tongue.

===Chinese===
“我再试最后一回，保险起见。”施默尔把香蕉树干往前挪到刀下，铡刀已经吊上了吊架顶。
阿秋竭力回想《阴骘文》里的格言。“居家和睦”，想起来了；可用不上。他不是要活着，他是要死了。不成，这条不行。“勿记仇怨”——对，可没有仇怨可记。施默尔他们办这件事，不带仇怨。在他们，这不过是一桩非办不可的活计，同垦林、开渠、种棉花一样，是非办不可的活计。施默尔一拽绳子，阿秋把《阴骘文》全忘了。铡刀嗖地一声砸下来，齐齐削过树干。
“好极了！”军士喝彩，点烟点到一半停了下来。“好极了，我的朋友。”
施默尔得了夸奖，受用得很。
“来吧，阿周，”他用塔希提话说。

===Original===
“But I am not Ah Chow﻿—” Ah Cho began.
“Shut up!” was the answer. “If you open your mouth again, I’ll break your head.”
The overseer threatened him with a clenched fist, and he remained silent. What was the good of protesting? Those foreign devils always had their way. He allowed himself to be lashed to the vertical board that was the size of his body. Schemmer drew the buckles tight﻿—so tight that the straps cut into his flesh and hurt. But he did not complain. The hurt would not last long. He felt the board tilting over in the air toward the horizontal, and closed his eyes. And in that moment he caught a last glimpse of his garden of meditation and repose. It seemed to him that he sat in the garden. A cool wind was blowing, and the bells in the several trees were tinkling softly. Also, birds were making sleepy noises, and from beyond the high wall came the subdued sound of village life.
Then he was aware that the board had come to rest, and from muscular pressures and tensions he knew that he was lying on his back. He opened his eyes. Straight above him he saw the suspended knife blazing in the sunshine. He saw the weight which had been added, and noted that one of Schemmer’s knots had slipped. Then he heard the sergeant’s voice in sharp command. Ah Cho closed his eyes hastily. He did not want to see that knife descend. But he felt it﻿—for one great fleeting instant. And in that instant he remembered Cruchot and what Cruchot had said. But Cruchot was wrong. The knife did not tickle. That much he knew before he ceased to know.

===Chinese===
“可我不是阿周——”阿秋刚开口。
“闭嘴！”回答是，“再张嘴，我敲碎你的脑袋。”
监工攥起拳头吓唬他，他便不作声了。分辩又有什么用？洋鬼子从来是要怎么办就怎么办。他任人把他绑上那块立板，板子同他的身子一般长短。施默尔把皮带扣收紧——紧得勒进肉里，生疼。可他没有吭声，这点疼长不了。他觉出板子在半空里朝水平倒下去，便闭上了眼睛。就在这一瞬，他最后一眼望见他那座静心园。他仿佛已经坐在园子里了。凉风习习，几株树上的风铃叮咚轻响，鸟儿发出困倦的啼声，高墙外头，隐隐传来村坊人烟的动静。
随后他觉出板子停住了，从周身筋肉的挤压与牵扯里，他知道自己是仰面躺着。他睁开眼。正头顶上，他看见吊着的铡刀在太阳底下亮得晃眼。他看见新添的那块铁，还看见施默尔打的一个绳结松了。跟着他听见军士厉声喝令。阿秋赶紧又闭上眼。他不想看见铡刀落下来。可他觉出了它——在那电光石火的一大瞬里。就在那一瞬，他想起了克吕肖，想起克吕肖说过的话。可克吕肖错了。刀并不痒。他知道了这一点，就再也不知道什么了。

===Original===
> Endnote: *The Tract of the Quiet Way*.

===Chinese===
> 尾注：《阴骘文》（*The Tract of the Quiet Way*）。

## Make Westing / 赶西程

===Original===
> Whatever you do, make westing! make westing!
> — Sailing directions for Cape Horn
For seven weeks the *Mary Rogers* had been between 50 degrees south in the Atlantic and 50 degrees south in the Pacific, which meant that for seven weeks she had been struggling to round Cape Horn. For seven weeks she had been either in dirt, or close to dirt, save once, and then, following upon six days of excessive dirt, which she had ridden out under the shelter of the redoubtable Terra del Fuego coast, she had almost gone ashore during a heavy swell in the dead calm that had suddenly fallen. For seven weeks she had wrestled with the Cape Horn graybeards, and in return been buffeted and smashed by them. She was a wooden ship, and her ceaseless straining had opened her seams, so that twice a day the watch took its turn at the pumps.

===Chinese===
> 无论做什么，都要赶西程！赶西程！
> ——合恩角航行指南
整整七个星期，*玛丽·罗杰斯*号被夹在大西洋南纬 50 度和太平洋南纬 50 度之间——这就是说，整整七个星期，她都在挣扎着绕过合恩角。整整七个星期，她不是在风暴天里，就是贴着风暴天的边，只有一次例外；而那一次，在整整六天的大风暴之后——那是她躲在令人生畏的火地岛海岸背后熬过去的——风势骤然死绝，一场大涌之中，她差一点上了岸。整整七个星期，她同合恩角的灰髯巨浪角力，换来的也是被它们抽打、被它们砸击。她是一条木船，无休止的绷扯使她的船缝绽开了，于是当值的船员每天两班轮着抽水。

===Original===
The *Mary Rogers* was strained, the crew was strained, and big Dan Cullen, master, was likewise strained. Perhaps he was strained most of all, for upon him rested the responsibility of that titanic struggle. He slept most of the time in his clothes, though he rarely slept. He haunted the deck at night, a great, burly, robust ghost, black with the sunburn of thirty years of sea and hairy as an orangutan. He, in turn, was haunted by one thought of action, a sailing direction for the Horn: *Whatever you do, make westing! make westing!* It was an obsession. He thought of nothing else, except, at times, to blaspheme God for sending such bitter weather.
*Make westing!* He hugged the Horn, and a dozen times lay hove to with the iron Cape bearing east-by-north, or north-north-east, a score of miles away. And each time the eternal west wind smote him back and he made easting. He fought gale after gale, south to 64 degrees, inside the antarctic drift-ice, and pledged his immortal soul to the Powers of Darkness for a bit of westing, for a slant to take him around. And he made easting. In despair, he had tried to make the passage through the Straits of Le Maire. Halfway through, the wind hauled to the north’ard of northwest, the glass dropped to 28.88, and he turned and ran before a gale of cyclonic fury, missing, by a hair’s-breadth, piling up the *Mary Rogers* on the black-toothed rocks. Twice he had made west to the Diego Ramirez Rocks, one of the times saved between two snow-squalls by sighting the gravestones of ships a quarter of a mile dead ahead.
Blow! Captain Dan Cullen instanced all his thirty years at sea to prove that never had it blown so before. The *Mary Rogers* was hove to at the time he gave the evidence, and, to clinch it, inside half an hour the *Mary Rogers* was hove down to the hatches. Her new maintopsail and brand new spencer were blown away like tissue paper; and five sails, furled and fast under double gaskets, were blown loose and stripped from the yards. And before morning the *Mary Rogers* was hove down twice again, and holes were knocked in her bulwarks to ease her decks from the weight of ocean that pressed her down.

===Chinese===
*玛丽·罗杰斯*号绷得变了形，船员绷得变了形，船主丹·卡伦，那条大汉，也同样绷得变了形。也许他绷得最厉害，因为那场泰坦之战的全部责任都压在他一人身上。他多数时候和衣而睡，尽管他难得睡着。夜里他在甲板上出没，一个高大、魁伟、健壮的鬼影，被三十年的海上烈日晒得黢黑，浑身毛发赛过猩猩。而缠住他的，是一个行动的念头，一句给合恩角的航行指南：*无论做什么，都要赶西程！赶西程！*这成了一种魔怔。他别的什么都不想，只是偶尔咒骂上帝，骂他派来这样恶毒的天气。
*赶西程！*他贴住合恩角，十几次顶风停船，让那铁角处在东偏北、或北东北二十来海里外。可每一次，那永恒的西风都把他掼回去，他反倒向东赶了路。他一场大风接一场大风地斗，往南斗到 64 度，斗进南极的浮冰群里，把自己不朽的灵魂典给黑暗诸神，只为换一星西程，换一阵能送他绕过去的斜风。可他偏偏又向东赶了路。绝望之下，他试过取道勒美尔海峡。走到一半，风转到西北偏北，气压表落到 28.88，他掉头在一阵旋风般的狂怒大风前面逃窜，只差一根头发丝，*玛丽·罗杰斯*号就要码在那黑牙般的礁石上了。他有两回往西走到迭戈拉米雷斯礁，其中一回，夹在两场雪暴之间，全靠望见正前方四分之一海里处那些船只的墓碑，才捡回一条船。
刮吧！丹·卡伦船长拿他海上整整三十年的阅历作证，证明从没刮过这么大的风。他作证的时候，*玛丽·罗杰斯*号正顶风停着；而为了把这证词钉死，不出半个钟头，*玛丽·罗杰斯*号就被压得横倾到舱口。她那面崭新的主中桅帆和刚出师的斜桁帆，像绵纸一样被刮跑了；另五面帆，卷好收拢、双道束帆索扎牢，也被吹散，从桁上剥了下去。天亮之前，*玛丽·罗杰斯*号又两度被压到舱口，舷墙上凿出了洞，好卸去压在甲板上那整座大洋的分量。

===Original===
On an average of once a week Captain Dan Cullen caught glimpses of the sun. Once, for ten minutes, the sun shone at midday, and ten minutes afterward a new gale was piping up, both watches were shortening sail, and all was buried in the obscurity of a driving snow-squall. For a fortnight, once, Captain Dan Cullen was without a meridian or a chronometer sight. Rarely did he know his position within half of a degree, except when in sight of land; for sun and stars remained hidden behind the sky, and it was so gloomy that even at the best the horizons were poor for accurate observations. A gray gloom shrouded the world. The clouds were gray; the great driving seas were leaden gray; the smoking crests were a gray churning; even the occasional albatrosses were gray, while the snow-flurries were not white, but gray, under the sombre pall of the heavens.
Life on board the *Mary Rogers* was gray﻿—gray and gloomy. The faces of the sailors were blue-gray; they were afflicted with sea-cuts and sea-boils, and suffered exquisitely. They were shadows of men. For seven weeks, in the forecastle or on deck, they had not known what it was to be dry. They had forgotten what it was to sleep out a watch, and all watches it was, “All hands on deck!” They caught snatches of agonized sleep, and they slept in their oilskins ready for the everlasting call. So weak and worn were they that it took both watches to do the work of one. That was why both watches were on deck so much of the time. And no shadow of a man could shirk duty. Nothing less than a broken leg could enable a man to knock off work; and there were two such, who had been mauled and pulped by the seas that broke aboard.
One other man who was the shadow of a man was George Dorety. He was the only passenger on board, a friend of the firm, and he had elected to make the voyage for his health. But seven weeks of Cape Horn had not bettered his health. He gasped and panted in his bunk through the long, heaving nights; and when on deck he was so bundled up for warmth that he resembled a peripatetic old-clothes shop. At midday, eating at the cabin table in a gloom so deep that the swinging sea-lamps burned always, he looked as blue-gray as the sickest, saddest man for’ard. Nor did gazing across the table at Captain Dan Cullen have any cheering effect upon him. Captain Cullen chewed and scowled and kept silent. The scowls were for God, and with every chew he reiterated the sole thought of his existence, which was *make westing*. He was a big, hairy brute, and the sight of him was not stimulating to the other’s appetite. He looked upon George Dorety as a Jonah, and told him so, once each meal, savagely transferring the scowl from God to the passenger and back again.

===Chinese===
平均一个星期，丹·卡伦船长能瞥见一回太阳。有一回，正午的太阳露了十分钟，十分钟后一场新风已经在呼啸，两班人手都在收帆，转眼一切又埋进劈头雪暴的昏黑里。有一回，整整半个月，丹·卡伦船长既测不到中天，也看不到能对天文钟的星。除了望得见陆地的时候，他难得把自己的船位估准到半度以内；因为日月星辰都藏在天幕后头，天色又那样阴沉，就算最好的时候，水平线也浑浊得没法做精确观测。一片灰蒙蒙的晦暗罩住了世界。云是灰的；奔腾的巨涌是铅灰的；冒烟的浪头是一片灰色的翻搅；连偶尔出现的信天翁也是灰的；满天愁惨的幕布底下，雪片也不是白的，是灰的。
*玛丽·罗杰斯*号上的日子是灰的——灰暗而阴沉。水手们的脸是青灰的；他们害着裂口和疮，痛得钻心。他们都是人的影子。整整七个星期，在水手舱里也好，在甲板上也好，他们不知道干爽是什么滋味。睡满一班觉是什么滋味，他们已经忘了，班班都是“全体上甲板！”他们抓几口苦痛的瞌睡，和衣裹着油布雨衣睡，好应付那没完没了的召唤。他们虚弱耗损到这个地步：两班人手才做得了一班的活。所以两班人手才那么常在甲板上。而人的影子也没有一个能躲掉差事。短不了断条腿，谁也别想歇工；这样的倒有两个，都是被打上船来的浪头捶烂碾碎的。
另一个人的影子，是乔治·多尔蒂。他是船上唯一的乘客，商行老板的朋友，为了养身体，自愿走这一趟海。可是合恩角的七个星期并没有把他的身体养好。漫漫长夜里，船一起一伏，他在铺位上喘气倒气；上了甲板，他又为御寒裹得那么严实，活像一间走动的旧衣铺。正午，他在舱室的餐桌上吃饭——舱里昏暗得连摇晃的海灯都得常点着——他的脸色，同船头那边病得最重、愁得最深的汉子一样青灰。隔着桌子望丹·卡伦船长，也提不起他半点兴致。卡伦船长咀嚼着、皱着眉、一言不发。那眉头是冲上帝皱的，每嚼一口，他都把他在世上唯一的念想重申一遍，那念想就是*赶西程*。他是一头多毛的大畜生，看上一眼就倒人胃口。他把乔治·多尔蒂看作约拿，而且每顿饭都告诉他一遍，凶巴巴地把眉头从上帝脸上转到乘客脸上，再转回去。

===Original===
Nor did the mate prove a first aid to a languid appetite. Joshua Higgins by name, a seaman by profession and pull, but a pot-wolloper by capacity, he was a loose-jointed, sniffling creature, heartless and selfish and cowardly, without a soul, in fear of his life of Dan Cullen, and a bully over the sailors, who knew that behind the mate was Captain Cullen, the lawgiver and compeller, the driver and the destroyer, the incarnation of a dozen bucko mates. In that wild weather at the southern end of the earth, Joshua Higgins ceased washing. His grimy face usually robbed George Dorety of what little appetite he managed to accumulate. Ordinarily this lavatorial dereliction would have caught Captain Cullen’s eye and vocabulary, but in the present his mind was filled with making westing, to the exclusion of all other things not contributory thereto. Whether the mate’s face was clean or dirty had no bearing upon westing. Later on, when 50 degrees south in the Pacific had been reached, Joshua Higgins would wash his face very abruptly. In the meantime, at the cabin table, where gray twilight alternated with lamplight while the lamps were being filled, George Dorety sat between the two men, one a tiger and the other a hyena, and wondered why God had made them. The second mate, Matthew Turner, was a true sailor and a man, but George Dorety did not have the solace of his company, for he ate by himself, solitary, when they had finished.
On Saturday morning, July 24, George Dorety awoke to a feeling of life and headlong movement. On deck he found the *Mary Rogers* running off before a howling southeaster. Nothing was set but the lower topsails and the foresail. It was all she could stand, yet she was making fourteen knots, as Mr. Turner shouted in Dorety’s ear when he came on deck. And it was all westing. She was going around the Horn at last﻿ ﻿… if the wind held. Mr. Turner looked happy. The end of the struggle was in sight. But Captain Cullen did not look happy. He scowled at Dorety in passing. Captain Cullen did not want God to know that he was pleased with that wind. He had a conception of a malicious God, and believed in his secret soul that if God knew it was a desirable wind, God would promptly efface it and send a snorter from the west. So he walked softly before God, smothering his joy down under scowls and muttered curses, and, so, fooling God, for God was the only thing in the universe of which Dan Cullen was afraid.
All Saturday and Saturday night the *Mary Rogers* raced her westing. Persistently she logged her fourteen knots, so that by Sunday morning she had covered three hundred and fifty miles. If the wind held, she would make around. If it failed, and the snorter came from anywhere between southwest and north, back the *Mary Rogers* would be hurled and be no better off than she had been seven weeks before. And on Sunday morning the wind *was* failing. The big sea was going down and running smooth. Both watches were on deck setting sail after sail as fast as the ship could stand it. And now Captain Cullen went around brazenly before God, smoking a big cigar, smiling jubilantly, as if the failing wind delighted him, while down underneath he was raging against God for taking the life out of the blessed wind. *Make westing!* So he would, if God would only leave him alone. Secretly, he pledged himself anew to the Powers of Darkness, if they would let him make westing. He pledged himself so easily because he did not believe in the Powers of Darkness. He really believed only in God, though he did not know it. And in his inverted theology God was really the Prince of Darkness. Captain Cullen was a devil-worshipper, but he called the devil by another name, that was all.

===Chinese===
大副呢，也决不是萎靡胃口的急救药。他名叫约书亚·希金斯，论出身和手劲算个水手，论器量只是个灶下烧火的。他是个浑身关节松垮、整天抽鼻子的东西，狠心、自私、怯懦，没有灵魂，怕丹·卡伦怕得要命，转过头去欺负水手；水手们都知道，大副背后站着卡伦船长——立法者与强迫者，驱赶者与毁灭者，一打恶霸大副的化身。在地球南端这等狂暴的天气里，约书亚·希金斯索性不洗脸了。他那张油污的脸，往往把乔治·多尔蒂好不容易攒下的一点胃口也败光。搁在平常，这桩盥洗上的失职少不了要吃卡伦船长的白眼和训斥，可眼下他满脑子都是赶西程，凡与此无关的事一概进不来。大副的脸干净不干净，同西程没有半点关系。往后，等船到了太平洋南纬 50 度，约书亚·希金斯自会突兀地把脸一洗。眼下呢，在舱室的餐桌边——添灯油的时候，灰蒙蒙的天光同灯光轮换着——乔治·多尔蒂坐在两块骨头中间，一块是老虎，一块是鬣狗，心里纳闷上帝造他们出来做什么。二副马修·特纳是个真正的水手、一条汉子，可多尔蒂享受不到他的陪伴作慰藉，因为他是等他们吃完，独自一个人吃的。
7 月 24 日，星期六早晨，乔治·多尔蒂醒来，觉出一种生机，一股一往无前的冲劲。到了甲板上，他看见*玛丽·罗杰斯*号正乘着呼啸的东南风疾驶。只张着下中桅帆和前帆，再没有别的。这是她挺得住的全部了，可她正跑着十四节——多尔蒂上甲板时，特纳先生是凑着他耳朵喊的。而且全是西程。她终于要绕过合恩角了……只要这风顶得住。特纳先生一脸欢喜。苦斗的尽头已经在望。可卡伦船长的脸不见欢喜。走过多尔蒂身边时，他冲他皱了皱眉。卡伦船长不想让上帝知道他对这风称心。他心里揣着的是一个存心使坏的上帝，并且在灵魂深处相信：上帝一旦知道这是阵好风，立马就会把它抹掉，从西边送来一场狂风。于是他在上帝面前轻手轻脚，把欢喜捂在眉头和咕哝的咒骂底下，就这样骗过了上帝——因为在丹·卡伦害怕的天下万物里，上帝是唯一的一件。
整个星期六和星期六夜里，*玛丽·罗杰斯*号赛着她的西程。她一程不落地跑着十四节，到星期天早晨，已经跑出三百五十海里。风要是顶得住，她就能绕过去。风要是泄了，狂风从西南到北面随便哪个方向压过来，*玛丽·罗杰斯*号就会被掼回去，落得比七个星期前好不了半分。而星期天早晨，风*果然*在泄。大涌正在平下去，越跑越顺。两班人手都在甲板上，船挺得住多快，就多快地一面接一面张帆。这下卡伦船长在上帝面前大摇大摆起来，叼着大雪茄，满面欢腾，仿佛风的衰竭正中下怀，可心底里，他正对上帝发火，恨他抽走了这顺风里的生命。*赶西程！*他就是要赶，只要上帝肯放他一马。他暗地里把自己重新许给黑暗诸神，条件是他们放他赶西程。他许得那样爽快，因为他不信黑暗诸神。他真正信的只有上帝，虽然他自己不知道。而在他那颠倒的神学里，上帝其实就是黑暗之君。卡伦船长是个拜魔鬼的，只是他管魔鬼叫了另一个名字，如此而已。

===Original===
At midday, after calling eight bells, Captain Cullen ordered the royals on. The men went aloft faster than they had gone in weeks. Not alone were they nimble because of the westing, but a benignant sun was shining down and limbering their stiff bodies. George Dorety stood aft, near Captain Cullen, less bundled in clothes than usual, soaking in the grateful warmth as he watched the scene. Swiftly and abruptly the incident occurred. There was a cry from the foreroyal-yard of “Man overboard!” Somebody threw a life-buoy over the side, and at the same instant the second mate’s voice came aft, ringing and peremptory﻿—
“Hard down your helm!”
The man at the wheel never moved a spoke. He knew better, for Captain Dan Cullen was standing alongside of him. He wanted to move a spoke, to move all the spokes, to grind the wheel down, hard down, for his comrade drowning in the sea. He glanced at Captain Dan Cullen, and Captain Dan Cullen gave no sign.

===Chinese===
正午，八击钟敲过，卡伦船长下令张顶帆。弟兄们爬上桅顶，比几个星期以来的哪一次都快。他们腿脚灵便，不单是为了西程，还因为一轮慈和的太阳正照下来，把他们僵硬的身子晒软了。乔治·多尔蒂站在船尾，卡伦船长近旁，身上裹得比平时单薄，一面观望着，一面浸在那叫人感激的暖意里。事情来得又快又突然。前顶帆桁上传来一声喊：“有人落水！”有人从舷边抛下救生圈，同一瞬间，二副的声音传到船尾，响亮而不容分说——
“把舵压死！”
掌舵的人一格都没动。他懂得利害，因为丹·卡伦船长就站在他身旁。他是想动的——想动一格，想把所有的格都动了，把舵轮碾死到底、压死到底，为了他那正在海里淹死的弟兄。他瞟了一眼丹·卡伦船长，丹·卡伦船长毫无表示。

===Original===
“Down! Hard down!” the second mate roared, as he sprang aft.
But he ceased springing and commanding, and stood still, when he saw Dan Cullen by the wheel. And big Dan Cullen puffed at his cigar and said nothing. Astern, and going astern fast, could be seen the sailor. He had caught the life-buoy and was clinging to it. Nobody spoke. Nobody moved. The men aloft clung to the royal yards and watched with terror-stricken faces. And the *Mary Rogers* raced on, making her westing. A long, silent minute passed.
“Who was it?” Captain Cullen demanded.
“Mops, sir,” eagerly answered the sailor at the wheel.
Mops topped a wave astern and disappeared temporarily in the trough. It was a large wave, but it was no graybeard. A small boat could live easily in such a sea, and in such a sea the *Mary Rogers* could easily come to. But she could not come to and make westing at the same time.
For the first time in all his years, George Dorety was seeing a real drama of life and death﻿—a sordid little drama in which the scales balanced an unknown sailor named Mops against a few miles of longitude. At first he had watched the man astern, but now he watched big Dan Cullen, hairy and black, vested with power of life and death, smoking a cigar.

===Chinese===
“压死！把舵压死！”二副吼着，人朝船尾蹿来。
可他一眼看见舵轮旁的丹·卡伦，蹿也停了，命令也停了，直挺挺站住。大个子丹·卡伦喷着雪茄，一言不发。船尾方向，正飞快退向远处的，望得见那个水手。他抓住了救生圈，正抱着它。没有人说话。没有人动。高处的人抱住顶帆桁，一张张脸看得目瞪口呆。*玛丽·罗杰斯*号只管疾驶，赶她的西程。长长的、无声的一分钟过去了。
“是谁？”卡伦船长问。
“莫普斯，船长，”掌舵的水手赶紧答。
莫普斯在船尾爬上一道浪头，又暂时没进浪谷里。那是道大浪，可算不得灰髯巨浪。这样的海面，一条小艇都活得轻松；这样的海面，*玛丽·罗杰斯*号也轻易就能回头救人。只是她不能又回头救人、又赶西程。
活了这一大把年纪，乔治·多尔蒂头一回亲眼看见一场真的生死剧——一出寒碜的小戏：天平一头是个叫莫普斯的无名水手，另一头是几海里经度。起初他望着船尾那个人，现在他望着大个子丹·卡伦，毛茸茸，黑黢黢，一身操着生死予夺之权，嘴里叼着雪茄。

===Original===
Captain Dan Cullen smoked another long, silent minute. Then he removed the cigar from his mouth. He glanced aloft at the spars of the *Mary Rogers*, and overside at the sea.
“Sheet home the royals!” he cried.
Fifteen minutes later they sat at table, in the cabin, with food served before them. On one side of George Dorety sat Dan Cullen, the tiger, on the other side, Joshua Higgins, the hyena. Nobody spoke. On deck the men were sheeting home the skysails. George Dorety could hear their cries, while a persistent vision haunted him of a man called Mops, alive and well, clinging to a life-buoy miles astern in that lonely ocean. He glanced at Captain Cullen, and experienced a feeling of nausea, for the man was eating his food with relish, almost bolting it.

===Chinese===
丹·卡伦船长又吸了长长的一声不响的一分钟。然后他把雪茄从嘴上拿下来。他朝高处望了望*玛丽·罗杰斯*号的桅桁，又朝舷外望了望海。
“顶帆张满！”他喊道。
十五分钟后，他们已在舱室的餐桌前坐定，饭菜摆在面前。乔治·多尔蒂一边坐着丹·卡伦，那只虎；另一边坐着约书亚·希金斯，那条鬣狗。没有人说话。甲板上，弟兄们正把天帆张满。乔治·多尔蒂听得见他们的吆喝，同时一个幻象纠缠着他：一个叫莫普斯的人，活着，好好的，在那片孤零零的大洋上，抱着救生圈，在几海里外的船尾。他瞥了瞥卡伦船长，一阵恶心涌上来，因为那人正吃得津津有味，几乎是往喉咙里吞。

===Original===
“Captain Cullen,” Dorety said, “you are in command of this ship, and it is not proper for me to comment now upon what you do. But I wish to say one thing. There is a hereafter, and yours will be a hot one.”
Captain Cullen did not even scowl. In his voice was regret as he said﻿—
“It was blowing a living gale. It was impossible to save the man.”
“He fell from the royal-yard,” Dorety cried hotly. “You were setting the royals at the time. Fifteen minutes afterward you were setting the skysails.”
“It was a living gale, wasn’t it, Mr. Higgins?” Captain Cullen said, turning to the mate.
“If you’d brought her to, it’d have taken the sticks out of her,” was the mate’s answer. “You did the proper thing, Captain Cullen. The man hadn’t a ghost of a show.”

===Chinese===
“卡伦船长，”多尔蒂说，“这条船归您指挥，眼下我对您的所作所为评头论足，不合规矩。可我想说一句。来世是有的，您那份来世，会烫得很。”
卡伦船长连眉头都没皱。他说这话时，声音里带着惋惜——
“当时刮着货真价实的大风。救那个人是没有可能的。”
“他是从前顶帆桁上掉下去的，”多尔蒂怒声喊。“当时您正在张顶帆。十五分钟后，您就张上天帆了。”
“那可是货真价实的大风，是不是，希金斯先生？”卡伦船长说着，转向大副。
“您要是把船停下来，桅杆就得给拔了，”大副这样回答。“您做得对，卡伦船长。那家伙本来就没有一线生机。”

===Original===
George Dorety made no answer, and to the meal’s end no one spoke. After that, Dorety had his meals served in his stateroom. Captain Cullen scowled at him no longer, though no speech was exchanged between them, while the *Mary Rogers* sped north toward warmer latitudes. At the end of the week, Dan Cullen cornered Dorety on deck.
“What are you going to do when we get to ’Frisco?” he demanded bluntly.
“I am going to swear out a warrant for your arrest,” Dorety answered quietly. “I am going to charge you with murder, and I am going to see you hanged for it.”

===Chinese===
乔治·多尔蒂没有作声，直到那顿饭吃完，谁也没有开口。此后，多尔蒂的饭都送到他舱房里去吃。卡伦船长不再冲他皱眉了，虽然两人之间再没有交谈过一句，而*玛丽·罗杰斯*号一路向北，驶向温暖的纬度。周末，丹·卡伦在甲板上把多尔蒂堵住了。
