“到了旧金山，你打算怎么办？”他直统统地问。
“我要去签发一张逮捕你的拘票，”多尔蒂静静地答。“我要以谋杀罪控告你，还要亲眼看着你为它上绞架。”

===Original===
“You’re almighty sure of yourself,” Captain Cullen sneered, turning on his heel.
A second week passed, and one morning found George Dorety standing in the coach-house companionway at the for’ard end of the long poop, taking his first gaze around the deck. The *Mary Rogers* was reaching full-and-by, in a stiff breeze. Every sail was set and drawing, including the staysails. Captain Cullen strolled for’ard along the poop. He strolled carelessly, glancing at the passenger out of the corner of his eye. Dorety was looking the other way, standing with head and shoulders outside the companionway, and only the back of his head was to be seen. Captain Cullen, with swift eye, embraced the mainstaysail-block and the head and estimated the distance. He glanced about him. Nobody was looking. Aft, Joshua Higgins, pacing up and down, had just turned his back and was going the other way. Captain Cullen bent over suddenly and cast the staysail-sheet off from its pin. The heavy block hurtled through the air, smashing Dorety’s head like an eggshell and hurtling on and back and forth as the staysail whipped and slatted in the wind. Joshua Higgins turned around to see what had carried away, and met the full blast of the vilest portion of Captain Cullen’s profanity.
“I made the sheet fast myself,” whimpered the mate in the first lull, “with an extra turn to make sure. I remember it distinctly.”

===Chinese===
“你倒把自己看得万无一失，”卡伦船长冷笑一声，转身走了。
又过了一个星期。一天早晨，乔治·多尔蒂站在长尾楼前端那道升降口里，头一回放眼四顾甲板。*玛丽·罗杰斯*号正在一股硬风里抢风行驶，贴近着风向。帆张满了全部，连支索帆也在内，面面吃风。卡伦船长顺着尾楼往前踱。他踱得漫不经心，眼角瞟着那位乘客。多尔蒂正望着另一边，头和肩膀探在升降口外，露出来的只有后脑勺。卡伦船长目光一扫，把主支索帆的滑车和那颗人头一齐收进眼里，估了估距离。他朝四下里扫了一眼。没有人看这边。船尾那边，约书亚·希金斯正来回踱步，刚转过背去，往另一头走。卡伦船长猛地弯下腰，把支索帆的帆脚索从桩销上摘了下来。那沉重的滑车破空砸去，像砸蛋壳一样砸碎了多尔蒂的头，又随着支索帆在风里抽打翻飞，一路砸过去、荡回来。约书亚·希金斯转过身来看什么东西飞了，迎面接住的，是卡伦船长满口污言秽语里最毒的那一阵。
“那根帆脚索是我亲手挽牢的，”大副趁头一个间歇呜咽道，“还多挽了一圈保险。我记得清清楚楚。”

===Original===
“Made fast?” the Captain snarled back, for the benefit of the watch as it struggled to capture the flying sail before it tore to ribbons. “You couldn’t make your grandmother fast, you useless hell’s scullion. If you made that sheet fast with an extra turn, why in hell didn’t it stay fast? That’s what I want to know. Why in hell didn’t it stay fast?”
The mate whined inarticulately.
“Oh, shut up!” was the final word of Captain Cullen.
Half an hour later he was as surprised as any when the body of George Dorety was found inside the companionway on the floor. In the afternoon, alone in his room, he doctored up the log.

===Chinese===
“挽牢？”船长龇牙回敬，这话是说给当班人听的，他们正拼命想把那面乱飞的帆抓住，免得它撕成条。“你连你祖母都挽不牢，地狱厨子里的废物。你要真多挽了一圈挽牢了，那它究竟为什么不牢？我就想知道这个。它究竟为什么不牢？”
大副呜呜咽咽，说不出整话。
“嗐，闭嘴！”这是卡伦船长的结案陈词。
半小时后，乔治·多尔蒂的尸体在升降口里被发现时，他同旁人一样吃惊。下午，他独自在房里，把航海日志料理了一番。

===Original===
> “Ordinary seaman, Karl Brun,” he wrote, “lost overboard from foreroyal-yard in a gale of wind. Was running at the time, and for the safety of the ship did not dare come up to the wind. Nor could a boat have lived in the sea that was running.”
On another page, he wrote:
> “Had often warned Mr. Dorety about the danger he ran because of his carelessness on deck. I told him, once, that some day he would get his head knocked off by a block. A carelessly fastened mainstaysail sheet was the cause of the accident, which was deeply to be regretted because Mr. Dorety was a favourite with all of us.”
Captain Dan Cullen read over his literary effort with admiration, blotted the page, and closed the log. He lighted a cigar and stared before him. He felt the *Mary Rogers* lift, and heel, and surge along, and knew that she was making nine knots. A smile of satisfaction slowly dawned on his black and hairy face. Well, anyway, he had made his westing and fooled God.

===Chinese===
> 他写道：“二等水手卡尔·布伦，在大风中自前顶帆桁落水。船当时正在行驶，为船只安全，不敢迎风停船。且当时海况之下，小艇亦难存活。”
在另一页上，他写道：
> “曾屡次告诫多尔蒂先生，他在甲板上疏忽大意，危险堪虞。有一次我告诉过他，总有一天他的头会被滑车敲掉。此次事故，肇因于一根挽得不牢的主支索帆帆脚索；多尔蒂先生深得我等众人爱戴，事故至为痛惜。”
丹·卡伦船长带着赞叹，把自己这篇手笔重读一遍，吸干墨迹，合上日志。他点起一支雪茄，凝望前方。他觉出*玛丽·罗杰斯*号昂起、侧倾、破浪疾驰，知道她正跑着九节。一丝满足的笑，慢慢升上他黢黑多毛的脸。好了，不管怎么说，他赶成了他的西程，也骗过了上帝。

## Semper Idem / 永远如一

===Original===
Doctor Bicknell was in a remarkably gracious mood. Through a minor accident, a slight bit of carelessness, that was all, a man who might have pulled through had died the preceding night. Though it had been only a sailorman, one of the innumerable unwashed, the steward of the receiving hospital had been on the anxious seat all the morning. It was not that the man had died that gave him discomfort, he knew the Doctor too well for that, but his distress lay in the fact that the operation had been done so well. One of the most delicate in surgery, it had been as successful as it was clever and audacious. All had then depended upon the treatment, the nurses, the steward. And the man had died. Nothing much, a bit of carelessness, yet enough to bring the professional wrath of Doctor Bicknell about his ears and to perturb the working of the staff and nurses for twenty-four hours to come.
But, as already stated, the Doctor was in a remarkably gracious mood. When informed by the steward, in fear and trembling, of the man’s unexpected takeoff, his lips did not so much as form one syllable of censure; nay, they were so pursed that snatches of ragtime floated softly from them, to be broken only by a pleasant query after the health of the other’s eldest-born. The steward, deeming it impossible that he could have caught the gist of the case, repeated it.
“Yes, yes,” Doctor Bicknell said impatiently; “I understand. But how about Semper Idem? Is he ready to leave?”

===Chinese===
比克内尔医生这天心情出奇地和悦。前一夜死了个人——不过小小的意外，一点点的疏忽，如此而已，那人本来也许挺得过来的。虽说死的只是个水手，无数不洗澡的下等人中的一个，收容医院的管事却整个上午如坐针毡。叫他不安的并不是人死了——这一层他太了解医生了——他的苦处在那手术做得太漂亮。那是外科里数一数二的精微手术，做得又巧、又大胆，也一样地成功。往后全看调理，看护士，看管事。可人死了。小事一桩，一点疏忽，却足够把比克内尔医生职业上的雷霆引到头上，也够叫职员和护士们在往后二十四小时里不得安生。
可是，如前所述，医生这天心情出奇地和悦。管事战战兢兢来报，说那人出乎意料地一命呜呼了，他嘴唇上连半个责备的音节都没有；非但如此，那嘴唇还撮起来，悠悠地飘出几段拉格泰姆，中间只断了一回，为的是和气地问候一句对方家大公子的贵恙。管事只怕自己听岔了案情的要领，又说了一遍。
"是了，是了，"比克内尔医生不耐烦地说，"我知道了。'永远如一'怎么样了？可以出院了么？"

===Original===
“Yes. They’re helping him dress now,” the steward answered, passing on to the round of his duties, content that peace still reigned within the iodine-saturated walls.
It was Semper Idem’s recovery which had so fully compensated Doctor Bicknell for the loss of the sailorman. Lives were to him as nothing, the unpleasant but inevitable incidents of the profession, but cases, ah, cases were everything. People who knew him were prone to brand him a butcher, but his colleagues were at one in the belief that a bolder and yet a more capable man never stood over the table. He was not an imaginative man. He did not possess, and hence had no tolerance for, emotion. His nature was accurate, precise, scientific. Men were to him no more than pawns, without individuality or personal value. But as cases it was different. The more broken a man was, the more precarious his grip on life, the greater his significance in the eyes of Doctor Bicknell. He would as readily forsake a poet laureate suffering from a common accident for a nameless, mangled vagrant who defied every law of life by refusing to die, as would a child forsake a Punch and Judy for a circus.
So it had been in the case of Semper Idem. The mystery of the man had not appealed to him, nor had his silence and the veiled romance which the yellow reporters had so sensationally and so fruitlessly exploited in divers Sunday editions. But Semper Idem’s throat had been cut. That was the point. That was where his interest had centred. Cut from ear to ear, and not one surgeon in a thousand to give a snap of the fingers for his chance of recovery. But, thanks to the swift municipal ambulance service and to Doctor Bicknell, he had been dragged back into the world he had sought to leave. The Doctor’s co-workers had shaken their heads when the case was brought in. Impossible, they said. Throat, windpipe, jugular, all but actually severed, and the loss of blood frightful. As it was such a foregone conclusion, Doctor Bicknell had employed methods and done things which made them, even in their professional capacities, shudder. And lo! the man had recovered.
So, on this morning that Semper Idem was to leave the hospital, hale and hearty, Doctor Bicknell’s geniality was in nowise disturbed by the steward’s report, and he proceeded cheerfully to bring order out of the chaos of a child’s body which had been ground and crunched beneath the wheels of an electric car.

===Chinese===
"可以了。这会儿他们正伺候他穿衣裳呢，"管事答着，自去巡视他的差事，心里踏实：碘酒气浸透的四面墙里，太平依旧。
全亏"永远如一"康复，才算把水手那条命给比克内尔医生补齐了。人命在他眼里一文不值，不过是这一行免不掉的晦气事；可是病例，啊，病例才是一切。认识他的人动辄骂他屠夫，他的同行却众口一词：站上手术台的，再没有比他更大胆、也更拿得起的人。他不是个富于想象的人。他没有感情，也就容不得感情。他的天性精确、严谨、科学。人在他眼里不过是棋子，没有个性，没有人的价值。可当作病例就不同了。一个人碎得越厉害，攥住性命的把柄越悬，在比克内尔医生眼里分量就越重。丢下一个遭了寻常意外的桂冠诗人，去救一个没名没姓、血肉模糊、偏不肯死、存心违抗一切生命法则的流浪汉，他会像小孩子丢下潘趣与朱迪木偶戏去看马戏一样，说换就换。
"永远如一"这一案也正是如此。这人的神秘打动不了他，他的沉默，还有那些黄色记者在几期礼拜天特刊上大炒特炒而一无所获的、蒙着面纱的罗曼史，也打动不了他。可是"永远如一"的喉咙割开了。要害正在这里。他的兴致全落在这里。从耳朵割到耳朵，一千个外科医生里没有一个肯为他能活下来弹一下指头。可多亏手脚麻利的市立救护车，多亏比克内尔医生，他到底被拖回了这个他一心要离开的世界。病人抬进来的时候，医生的同僚们直摇头。没救，他们说。喉咙、气管、颈静脉，差不多断绝，失血之惨，触目惊心。正因结局早定，比克内尔医生用的法子、做下的事，连他们以同行的身份看了都发抖。可是看哪！这人活过来了。
于是，在这个"永远如一"康复出院的早晨，管事的报告全没有搅扰比克内尔医生的兴致，他快快活活接着干手头的活：从一具叫电车车轮碾得研碎压烂的孩子躯体里，理出个秩序来。

===Original===
As many will remember, the case of Semper Idem aroused a vast deal of unseemly yet highly natural curiosity. He had been found in a slum lodging, with throat cut as aforementioned, and blood dripping down upon the inmates of the room below and disturbing their festivities. He had evidently done the deed standing, with head bowed forward that he might gaze his last upon a photograph which stood on the table propped against a candlestick. It was this attitude which had made it possible for Doctor Bicknell to save him. So terrific had been the sweep of the razor that had he had his head thrown back, as he should have done to have accomplished the act properly, with his neck stretched and the elastic vascular walls distended, he would have of a certainty well-nigh decapitated himself.
At the hospital, during all the time he travelled the repugnant road back to life, not a word had left his lips. Nor could anything be learned of him by the sleuths detailed by the chief of police. Nobody knew him, nor had ever seen or heard of him before. He was strictly, uniquely, of the present. His clothes and surroundings were those of the lowest labourer, his hands the hands of a gentleman. But not a shred of writing was discovered, nothing, save in one particular, which would serve to indicate his past or his position in life.
And that one particular was the photograph. If it were at all a likeness, the woman who gazed frankly out upon the onlooker from the card-mount must have been a striking creature indeed. It was an amateur production, for the detectives were baffled in that no professional photographer’s signature or studio was appended. Across a corner of the mount, in delicate feminine tracery, was written: “*Semper idem; semper fidelis.*” And she looked it. As many recollect, it was a face one could never forget. Clever halftones, remarkably like, were published in all the leading papers at the time; but such procedure gave rise to nothing but the uncontrollable public curiosity and interminable copy to the space-writers.

===Chinese===
许多人还记得，"永远如一"一案曾激起过一大片不成体统、却也极其自然的好奇。他是在贫民窟一间寄宿屋里被人发现的，喉咙如前所述割开了，血一滴一滴漏下去，漏进楼下那间屋住客的欢聚里，搅了他们的兴。他分明是站着行的事，头向前俯着，好把他最后一眼留给桌上那张斜倚烛台立着的照片。正是这个姿势救了他，叫比克内尔医生有了下手之处。那剃刀挥得如此狠，倘若他像要把事办得体面的人那样，把头往后一仰，脖颈绷直，富于弹性的血管壁撑开，那他几乎准定把自己割得身首异处。
在医院里，在他一路走完那条重返人世的可厌长路的全部时日里，他嘴唇上没有漏出一个字。警察局长派来的暗探，也没能从他身上探出任何东西。没人认得他，从前也没人见过他、听说过他。他完完全全、彻头彻尾只属于当下。他的衣裳和周遭是最下等苦力的，他的手却是绅士的手。可是除了一点，再寻不出一星半点文字，寻不出任何足以指明他的过去或身世的东西。
那一点便是那张照片。倘若它还算肖似，那么从相卡上坦然直视观者的那个女人，必定是个惊人的尤物。那是件业余作品，侦探们一筹莫展，因为没有署任何照相馆的字号或印记。相卡一角，一行娟秀的女子笔迹写着："*Semper idem; semper fidelis*——永远如一，永远忠贞。"她当得起这话。许多人还记得，那是一张叫人过目难忘的脸。当时各家大报都登了惟妙惟肖的照相铜版；可是这一手除了撩起公众压不住的好奇、给跑量写手添了没完没了的稿子，一无所成。

===Original===
For want of a better name, the rescued suicide was known to the hospital attendants, and to the world, as Semper Idem. And Semper Idem he remained. Reporters, detectives, and nurses gave him up in despair. Not one word could he be persuaded to utter; yet the flitting conscious light of his eyes showed that his ears heard and his brain grasped every question put to him.
But this mystery and romance played no part in Doctor Bicknell’s interest when he paused in the office to have a parting word with his patient. He, the Doctor, had performed a prodigy in the matter of this man, done what was virtually unprecedented in the annals of surgery. He did not care who or what the man was, and it was highly improbable that he should ever see him again; but, like the artist gazing upon a finished creation, he wished to look for the last time upon the work of his hand and brain.
Semper Idem still remained mute. He seemed anxious to be gone. Not a word could the Doctor extract from him, and little the Doctor cared. He examined the throat of the convalescent carefully, idling over the hideous scar with the lingering, half-caressing fondness of a parent. It was not a particularly pleasing sight. An angry line circled the throat﻿—for all the world as though the man had just escaped the hangman’s noose﻿—and, disappearing below the ear on either side, had the appearance of completing the fiery periphery at the nape of the neck.
Maintaining his dogged silence, yielding to the other’s examination in much the manner of a leashed lion, Semper Idem betrayed only his desire to drop from out of the public eye.

===Chinese===
因为想不出更好的名字，医院上下，连同外面的世界，都管这位救回来的自杀者叫"永远如一"。他也就一直是"永远如一"。记者、侦探、护士们先后绝望地撒了手。任你怎么劝，他一个字也不肯吐；可他眼里掠过的清醒的光表明：他耳朵听得见，你问的每一句话，他脑子里都接得住。
不过，当医生在办公室里停下脚步，同病人作别的时候，这层神秘与罗曼史在他兴味里一点份量也不占。他，这位医生，在这个人身上创造了奇迹，干下了外科编年史上近乎史无前例的事。他不在乎这人是谁、干什么的，往后也极难得再见到他；可是，像艺术家凝望完工的造物一样，他想最后看一眼自己手和脑的作品。
"永远如一"依旧一言不发。他像是急着要走。医生从他嘴里掏不出一个字，也懒得再掏。他细细察看康复者的喉咙，在那道可怕的伤疤上流连不去，带着父母那种半是爱抚的摩挲。那实在不是个悦目的景象。一条怒赤的线环着喉咙——活像这人刚从绞刑吏的索套里逃生——两耳边没入，看那走势，是要在后颈上把这一圈火赤的边界画拢。
"永远如一"死守住他的沉默，由着对方检查，那样子很像一头拴着链子的狮子，只露出一桩心思：从众人眼皮底下退出去。

===Original===
“Well, I’ll not keep you,” Doctor Bicknell finally said, laying a hand on the man’s shoulder and stealing a last glance at his own handiwork. “But let me give you a bit of advice. Next time you try it on, hold your chin up, so. Don’t snuggle it down and butcher yourself like a cow. Neatness and despatch, you know. Neatness and despatch.”
Semper Idem’s eyes flashed in token that he heard, and a moment later the hospital door swung to on his heel.
It was a busy day for Doctor Bicknell, and the afternoon was well along when he lighted a cigar preparatory to leaving the table upon which it seemed the sufferers almost clamoured to be laid. But the last one, an old ragpicker with a broken shoulder-blade, had been disposed of, and the first fragrant smoke wreaths had begun to curl about his head, when the gong of a hurrying ambulance came through the open window from the street, followed by the inevitable entry of the stretcher with its ghastly freight.

===Chinese===
"好吧，不留你了，"比克内尔医生终于开口，一只手按在那人肩上，末了再偷看一眼自己的手笔。"不过送你一句忠告。下回再试的时候，下巴抬起来，这样。别把下巴缩下去，像宰牛似的把自己往烂里剁。要干净，要利落，懂么。干净，利落。"
"永远如一"的眼光一闪，表示听见了。片刻之后，医院的大门在他脚跟后面阖上了。
这一天比克内尔医生忙得很。到下午过半，他点起一支雪茄，预备离开那张手术台——瞧那光景，受苦受难的人们简直是吵着嚷着要往这台子上躺。最后一个，一个肩胛骨摔断的老捡破烂的，已经打发完毕，头几缕芬芳的雪茄烟刚开始在他头上盘旋，一辆飞驰的救护车的锣声就从敞开的窗口由街上闯了进来，跟着照例抬进了担架，连同上面那叫人不敢看的货色。

===Original===
“Lay it on the table,” the Doctor directed, turning for a moment to place his cigar in safety. “What is it?”
“Suicide﻿—throat cut,” responded one of the stretcher bearers. “Down on Morgan Alley. Little hope, I think, sir. He’s ’most gone.”
“Eh? Well, I’ll give him a look, anyway.” He leaned over the man at the moment when the quick made its last faint flutter and succumbed.
“It’s Semper Idem come back again,” the steward said.
“Ay,” replied Doctor Bicknell, “and gone again. No bungling this time. Properly done, upon my life, sir, properly done. Took my advice to the letter. I’m not required here. Take it along to the morgue.”
Doctor Bicknell secured his cigar and relighted it. “That,” he said between the puffs, looking at the steward, “that evens up for the one you lost last night. We’re quits now.”

===Chinese===
"抬上台子，"医生吩咐，转身把雪茄安置到稳当地方。"怎么回事？"
"自杀——割喉咙，"抬担架的一个答话。"摩根巷的事。我看希望不大，先生。人差不多了。"
"哦？那也看一眼罢。"他俯身去看，恰在那一瞬，那点残生最后轻轻一颤，断了。
"'永远如一'又回来了，"管事说。
"是啊，"比克内尔医生答，"这回又走了。这回没有笨手笨脚。办得地道，我对天起誓，先生，办得地道。我的忠告，他一字不差照办了。这儿用不着我了。抬太平间去罢。"
比克内尔医生把雪茄扶稳，重新点着。"这个，"他一口一口喷着烟，望着管事说，"这个正好抵过你们昨夜折掉的那一个。咱们两讫了。"

## A Nose for the King / 为国王找鼻子

===Original===
In the morning calm of Korea, when its peace and tranquillity truly merited its ancient name, “Cho-sen,” there lived a politician by name Yi Chin Ho. He was a man of parts, and﻿—who shall say?﻿—perhaps in no wise worse than politicians the world over. But, unlike his brethren in other lands, Yi Chin Ho was in jail. Not that he had inadvertently diverted to himself public moneys, but that he had inadvertently diverted too much. Excess is to be deplored in all things, even in grafting, and Yi Chin Ho’s excess had brought him to most deplorable straits.
Ten thousand strings of cash he owed the Government, and he lay in prison under sentence of death. There was one advantage to the situation﻿—he had plenty of time in which to think. And he thought well. Then called he the jailer to him.
“Most worthy man, you see before you one most wretched,” he began. “Yet all will be well with me if you will but let me go free for one short hour this night. And all will be well with you, for I shall see to your advancement through the years, and you shall come at length to the directorship of all the prisons of Cho-sen.”

===Chinese===
在朝鲜那清晨般的静谧里——那时节，它四境的太平安宁，当真配得上它那个古老的名号："晨静之国"——住着一位政客，名叫李镇浩。此人颇有几分才干，而且——谁说得准呢？——未必就比天下各国的政客更坏。只是，别国的同行们逍遥自在，李镇浩却蹲在牢里。倒不是他一不留神把公款挪做了私用，而是他一不留神挪得太多了。凡事过犹不及，贪墨一道也不例外，李镇浩这一过度，就把自己弄到了最不堪的绝地。
他欠着官府一万贯钱，蹲在牢里，判了死罪。这境况有一样好处——时间充裕，尽够他动脑筋。他想得很好。于是他把狱卒唤到跟前。
"最可敬的君子，您眼前站着的，是天底下头一号的苦命人，"他开言道。"可只要您今夜放我出去短短一个时辰，我便万事大吉。您也万事大吉，因为往后几十年，我自会照应您步步高升，到头来，普天下朝鲜大小牢狱的典狱之职，都是您的。"

===Original===
“How now?” demanded the jailer. “What foolishness is this? One short hour, and you but waiting for your head to be chopped off! And I, with an aged and much-to-be-respected mother, not to say anything of a wife and several children of tender years! Out upon you for the scoundrel that you are!”
“From the Sacred City to the ends of all the Eight Coasts there is no place for me to hide,” Yi Chin Ho made reply. “I am a man of wisdom, but of what worth my wisdom here in prison? Were I free, well I know I could seek out and obtain the money wherewith to repay the Government. I know of a nose that will save me from all my difficulties.”
“A nose!” cried the jailer.

===Chinese===
"这是什么话？"狱卒喝道。"打的什么糊涂主意！短短一个时辰！你一个等着上砧板掉脑袋的人！再说，我上有年高德劭的老母，更别提老婆和好几个奶气未干的孩子了！好你个泼皮，呸！"
"从王京到八方海岸的尽头，没有我藏身的地方，"李镇浩答道。"我是个有智谋的人，可智谋蹲在牢里有何用处？只要还我自由之身，我深知我寻得来那笔钱，还清官府。我知道一只鼻子，它能救我出一切难关。"
"一只鼻子！"狱卒叫起来。

===Original===
“A nose,” said Yi Chin Ho. “A remarkable nose, if I may say so, a most remarkable nose.”
The jailer threw up his hands despairingly. “Ah, what a wag you are, what a wag,” he laughed. “To think that that very admirable wit of yours must go the way of the chopping-block!”
And so saying, he turned and went away. But in the end, being a man soft of head and heart, when the night was well along he permitted Yi Chin Ho to go.

===Chinese===
"一只鼻子，"李镇浩说。"恕我直言，一只了不得的鼻子，一只极其了不得的鼻子。"
狱卒绝望地摊开双手。"嗐，你这张嘴哟，你这张嘴哟，"他笑道。"想想看，你这一身好才情，居然要往砧板上送！"
说罢，他转身走了。可是到头来，他到底是个脑壳软、心肠也软的人，夜深了，他还是放李镇浩去了。

===Original===
Straight he went to the Governor, catching him alone and arousing him from his sleep.
“Yi Chin Ho, or I’m no Governor!” cried the Governor. “What do you here who should be in prison waiting on the chopping-block?”
“I pray Your Excellency to listen to me,” said Yi Chin Ho, squatting on his hams by the bedside and lighting his pipe from the firebox. “A dead man is without value. It is true, I am as a dead man, without value to the Government, to Your Excellency, or to myself. But if, so to say, Your Excellency were to give me my freedom﻿—”

===Chinese===
他径直去见总督，趁他独自一人，把他从睡梦里唤醒。
"李镇浩！不然我就不是总督！"总督叫道。"你不去牢里候着上砧板，跑到这儿来做什么？"
"卑职恳请大人大驾听禀，"李镇浩说着，蹲踞在床边，就着火盆点起烟袋。"死人一文不值。不错，卑职如今形同死人，于官府、于大人、于自己，都一文不值。可要是，打个比方，大人肯赐卑职自由之身——"

===Original===
“Impossible!” cried the Governor. “Beside, you are condemned to death.”
“Your Excellency well knows that if I can repay the ten thousand strings of cash, the Government will pardon me,” Yi Chin Ho went on. “So, as I say, if Your Excellency were to give me my freedom for a few days, being a man of understanding, I should then repay the Government and be in position to be of service to Your Excellency. I should be in position to be of very great service to Your Excellency.”
“Have you a plan whereby you hope to obtain this money?” asked the Governor.
“I have,” said Yi Chin Ho.

===Chinese===
"万万不能！"总督叫道。"再说，你已是判了死罪的人。"
"大人深知，只要卑职还得上那一万贯钱，官府自会赦免卑职，"李镇浩接着说。"所以，方才说了，大人若肯放卑职几日自由，卑职是个懂事的，随后自会还清官府的债，也就有了力量孝敬大人。有了力量大大地孝敬大人。"
"你可有了筹钱的法子？"总督问。
"有了，"李镇浩说。

===Original===
“Then come with it to me tomorrow night; I would now sleep,” said the Governor, taking up his snore where it had been interrupted.
On the following night, having again obtained leave of absence from the jailer, Yi Chin Ho presented himself at the Governor’s bedside.
“Is it you, Yi Chin Ho?” asked the Governor. “And have you the plan?”

===Chinese===
"那明晚带了法子来见我；这会儿我要睡了，"总督说着，鼾声从方才打断的地方接着响了起来。
第二天夜里，又向狱卒告了假，李镇浩来到总督床前。
"是你么，李镇浩？"总督问。"法子带来了？"

===Original===
“It is I, Your Excellency,” answered Yi Chin Ho, “and the plan is here.”
“Speak,” commanded the Governor.
“The plan is here,” repeated Yi Chin Ho, “here in my hand.”
The Governor sat up and opened his eyes. Yi Chin Ho proffered in his hand a sheet of paper. The Governor held it to the light.

===Chinese===
"是卑职，大人，"李镇浩答道，"法子就在这儿。"
"讲，"总督吩咐。
"法子就在这儿，"李镇浩重复道，"就在我手里。"
总督坐起身，睁开眼。李镇浩双手奉上一页纸。总督把它举到光前。

===Original===
“Nothing but a nose,” said he.
“A bit pinched, so, and so, Your Excellency,” said Yi Chin Ho.
“Yes, a bit pinched here and there, as you say,” said the Governor.
“Withal it is an exceeding corpulent nose, thus, and so, all in one place, at the end,” proceeded Yi Chin Ho. “Your Excellency would seek far and wide and many a day for that nose and find it not!”

===Chinese===
"不过是一只鼻子嘛，"他说。
"鼻翼略略往里夹一点，这样，又这样，大人，"李镇浩说。
"是啊，这儿那儿都夹得瘪瘪的，你说得是，"总督说。
"饶是如此，它又是一只出奇的肥鼻子，这么着，那么着，肥肉全攒在一处，攒在鼻头上，"李镇浩说下去。"大人便是寻遍四方、寻上经年累月，也寻不出这第二只鼻子！"

===Original===
“An unusual nose,” admitted the Governor.
“There is a wart upon it,” said Yi Chin Ho.
“A most unusual nose,” said the Governor. “Never have I seen the like. But what do you with this nose, Yi Chin Ho?”
“I seek it whereby to repay the money to the Government,” said Yi Chin Ho. “I seek it to be of service to Your Excellency, and I seek it to save my own worthless head. Further, I seek Your Excellency’s seal upon this picture of the nose.”

===Chinese===
"一只少见的鼻子，"总督承认。
"鼻子上还有一颗疣子，"李镇浩说。
"一只极其少见的鼻子，"总督说。"生平没见过这样的。可是李镇浩，你要这只鼻子做什么用？"
"卑职寻它，为的是还官府的钱，"李镇浩说。"寻它，为的是孝敬大人；寻它，为的是保全卑职这颗不值钱的脑袋。此外，卑职还恳请大人在这张鼻子的图上，落大人的官印。"

===Original===
And the Governor laughed and affixed the seal of State, and Yi Chin Ho departed. For a month and a day he travelled the King’s Road which leads to the shore of the Eastern Sea; and there, one night, at the gate of the largest mansion of a wealthy city he knocked loudly for admittance.
“None other than the master of the house will I see,” said he fiercely to the frightened servants. “I travel upon the King’s business.”
Straightway was he led to an inner room, where the master of the house was roused from his sleep and brought blinking before him.

===Chinese===
总督哈哈大笑，落了国玺，李镇浩告辞而去。他沿着通到东海之滨的御道，走了一个月零一天；一天夜里，在一座富庶城市里最大的宅邸门前，他高声叩门求见。
"我谁也不见，只见你家主人，"他对吓坏了的仆人声色俱厉地说。"我奉御差出行。"
他随即被引进一间内室，宅子的主人从睡梦中被唤醒，眨巴着眼睛，被搀到他跟前。

===Original===
“You are Pak Chung Chang, head man of this city,” said Yi Chin Ho in tones that were all-accusing. “I am upon the King’s business.”
Pak Chung Chang trembled. Well he knew the King’s business was ever a terrible business. His knees smote together, and he near fell to the floor.
“The hour is late,” he quavered. “Were it not well to﻿—”
“The King’s business never waits!” thundered Yi Chin Ho. “Come apart with me, and swiftly. I have an affair of moment to discuss with you.

===Chinese===
"你是朴忠昌，本城的城主，"李镇浩的声调句句如控诉。"我奉御差而来。"
朴忠昌哆嗦起来。他深知御差从来是可怕的差事。两膝互相磕碰，险些瘫倒在地。
"夜深了，"他颤声说。"是不是不妨——"
"御差从不等人！"李镇浩雷吼一般。"跟我到一边来，快。我有一桩要事同你相商。

===Original===
“It is the King’s affair,” he added with even greater fierceness; so that Pak Chung Chang’s silver pipe dropped from his nerveless fingers and clattered on the floor.
“Know then,” said Yi Chin Ho, when they had gone apart, “that the King is troubled with an affliction, a very terrible affliction. In that he failed to cure, the Court physician has had nothing else than his head chopped off. From all the Eight Provinces have the physicians come to wait upon the King. Wise consultation have they held, and they have decided that for a remedy for the King’s affliction nothing else is required than a nose, a certain kind of nose, a very peculiar certain kind of nose.
“Then by none other was I summoned than His Excellency the Prime Minister himself. He put a paper into my hand. Upon this paper was the very peculiar kind of nose drawn by the physicians of the Eight Provinces, with the seal of State upon it.
“ ‘Go,’ said His Excellency the Prime Minister. ‘Seek out this nose, for the King’s affliction is sore. And wheresoever you find this nose upon the face of a man, strike it off forthright and bring it in all haste to the Court, for the King must be cured. Go, and come not back until your search is rewarded.’

===Chinese===
"这是大王的事体，"他添了一句，声色更厉；朴忠昌的银烟杆当啷一声，从没了力气的指头间掉在了地上。
"那你听着，"李镇浩等两人到了僻静处，说道，"大王染了一种恶疾，一种非常可怕的恶疾。御医没能治好，除了掉脑袋，别无下场。八道的名医都来给大王请过脉。他们会商定了：要治大王的恶疾，别的都不用，只要一只鼻子，一种特别的鼻子，一种非常特别、极其讲究的鼻子。
"召我去的，不是别人，正是首相大人本人。他往我手里放了一页纸。纸上画着八道名医合拟的那只极其特别的鼻子，上面落着国玺。
"'去，'首相大人说。'寻这只鼻子去，大王的病势沉。这鼻子长在哪个人的脸上，你便当场剁下来，火速带回朝廷，大王非治不可。去罢，寻不着，休回来。'"

===Original===
“And so I departed upon my quest,” said Yi Chin Ho. “I have sought out the remotest corners of the kingdom; I have travelled the Eight Highways, searched the Eight Provinces, and sailed the seas of the Eight Coasts. And here I am.”
With a great flourish he drew a paper from his girdle, unrolled it with many snappings and cracklings, and thrust it before the face of Pak Chung Chang. Upon the paper was the picture of the nose.
Pak Chung Chang stared upon it with bulging eyes.
“Never have I beheld such a nose,” he began.

===Chinese===
"就这样，我奉命上路，"李镇浩说。"王国最偏僻的角落我也访到了；我走遍了八条官道，搜遍了八道，航遍了八方海岸的海。如今我到了这里。"
他大张旗鼓地从腰间抽出一页纸，抖开来毕毕剥剥一阵响，直戳到朴忠昌脸前。纸上画着那只鼻子。
朴忠昌眼睛鼓出来，死盯着它看。
"生平从未见过这样的鼻子，"他开口。

===Original===
“There is a wart upon it,” said Yi Chin Ho.
“Never have I beheld﻿—” Pak Chung Chang began again.
“Bring your father before me,” Yi Chin Ho interrupted sternly.
“My ancient and very-much-to-be-respected ancestor sleeps,” said Pak Chung Chang.

===Chinese===
"鼻子上有一颗疣子，"李镇浩说。
"生平从未见过——"朴忠昌又开口。
"把你父亲带到我面前来，"李镇浩厉声打断。
"我家年高德劭的老祖宗睡下了，"朴忠昌说。

===Original===
“Why dissemble?” demanded Yi Chin Ho. “You know it is your father’s nose. Bring him before me that I may strike it off and be gone. Hurry, lest I make bad report of you.”
“Mercy!” cried Pak Chung Chang, falling on his knees. “It is impossible! It is impossible! You cannot strike off my father’s nose. He cannot go down without his nose to the grave. He will become a laughter and a byword, and all my days and nights will be filled with woe. O reflect! Report that you have seen no such nose in your travels. You, too, have a father.”
Pak Chung Chang clasped Yi Chin Ho’s knees and fell to weeping on his sandals.
“My heart softens strangely at your tears,” said Yi Chin Ho. “I, too, know filial piety and regard. But﻿—” He hesitated, then added, as though thinking aloud, “It is as much as my head is worth.”

===Chinese===
"还装什么糊涂？"李镇浩逼问。"你知道这就是你父亲的鼻子。把他带到我面前，我好剁下它就走。快点，免得我回去参你一本。"
"开恩哪！"朴忠昌跪倒在地，高声哀号。"使不得！万万使不得！你不能剁我父亲的鼻子。他不能没了鼻子进坟墓。他要沦为笑柄，落人话柄，我从此日日夜夜，愁云惨雾。啊，三思！你就回禀说，一路访察，并未见着这样的鼻子。您也是有父亲的人哪。"
朴忠昌抱住李镇浩的双膝，伏在他的鞋面上哭起来。
"你这一哭，我的心肠也古怪地软了，"李镇浩说。"孝心孝行，我也是懂的。可是——"他迟疑了一下，又自言自语似的添了一句："这可是拿我这颗脑袋作保的事。"

===Original===
“How much is your head worth?” asked Pak Chung Chang in a thin, small voice.
“A not remarkable head,” said Yi Chin Ho. “An absurdly unremarkable head; but, such is my great foolishness, I value it at nothing less than one hundred thousand strings of cash.”
“So be it,” said Pak Chung Chang, rising to his feet.
“I shall need horses to carry the treasure,” said Yi Chin Ho, “and men to guard it well as I journey through the mountains. There are robbers abroad in the land.”

===Chinese===
"您的脑袋值多少？"朴忠昌声音细细小小地问。
"一颗不怎么样的脑袋，"李镇浩说。"一颗寻常得可笑的脑袋；只是我这个人痴愚得厉害，少于十万贯钱，我是断不肯出手的。"
"就这么办，"朴忠昌说，站起身来。
"驮这些财宝，我得要马，"李镇浩说，"一路过山，还得要人押运妥当。如今这地面上有强人出没。"

===Original===
“There are robbers abroad in the land,” said Pak Chung Chang, sadly. “But it shall be as you wish, so long as my ancient and very-much-to-be-respected ancestor’s nose abide in its appointed place.”
“Say nothing to any man of this occurrence,” said Yi Chin Ho, “else will other and more loyal servants than I be sent to strike off your father’s nose.”
And so Yi Chin Ho departed on his way through the mountains, blithe of heart and gay of song as he listened to the jingling bells of his treasure-laden ponies.
There is little more to tell. Yi Chin Ho prospered through the years. By his efforts the jailer attained at length to the directorship of all the prisons of Cho-sen; the Governor ultimately betook himself to the Sacred City to be Prime Minister to the King, while Yi Chin Ho became the King’s boon companion and sat at table with him to the end of a round, fat life. But Pak Chung Chang fell into a melancholy, and ever after he shook his head sadly, with tears in his eyes, whenever he regarded the expensive nose of his ancient and very-much-to-be-respected ancestor.

===Chinese===
"这地面上是有强人出没，"朴忠昌愁眉苦脸地说。"可是一切照吩咐办，只求我家年高德劭的老祖宗的鼻子，安安稳稳留在原处。"
"今日之事，对谁都休提，"李镇浩说，"不然朝廷会另派差役来——比我更尽忠的差役——来剁你父亲的鼻子。"
于是李镇浩取道山路而去，一路听着驮满财宝的小马叮叮当当的铃铛，心里轻快，嘴上哼着歌。
往后的事不多了。李镇浩这些年官运亨通。狱卒靠了他，到底当上了朝鲜大小牢狱的总典狱；总督后来进了王京，做了大王的首相；李镇浩呢，成了大王的酒肉近臣，陪王伴驾，直到他那圆滚滚、肥滋滋的一生过完。只有朴忠昌郁郁寡欢，打那以后，每望见他家年高德劭的老祖宗那只价值连城的鼻子，就含着眼泪，伤伤心心地摇头。

## The *Francis Spaight* / 弗朗西斯·斯佩特号

===Original===
*(A True Tale Retold)*
The *Francis Spaight* was running before it solely under a mizzentopsail, when the thing happened. It was not due to carelessness so much as to the lack of discipline of the crew and to the fact that they were indifferent seamen at best. The man at the wheel in particular, a Limerick man, had had no experience with salt water beyond that of rafting timber on the Shannon between the Quebec vessels and the shore. He was afraid of the huge seas that rose out of the murk astern and bore down upon him, and he was more given to cowering away from their threatened impact than he was to meeting their blows with the wheel and checking the ship’s rush to broach to.
It was three in the morning when his unseamanlike conduct precipitated the catastrophe. At sight of a sea far larger than its fellows, he crouched down, releasing his hands from the spokes. The *Francis Spaight* sheered as her stern lifted on the sea, receiving the full fling of the cap on her quarter. The next instant she was in the trough, her lee-rail buried till the ocean was level with her hatch-coamings, sea after sea breaking over her weather rail and sweeping what remained exposed of the deck with icy deluges.

===Chinese===
*（据实重述）*
出事的时候，*弗朗西斯·斯佩特号*只凭一面后桅顶帆在风前奔逃。事情与其说坏在疏忽，不如说坏在水手没有纪律，也坏在他们充其量不过是些二流海员。尤其那个掌舵的，利默里克人，同咸水打交道的全部经验，不过是在香农河上、在魁北克的船和岸之间放运木材。船尾后头的黑雾里拔起一座座恶浪，朝他直压过来，他怕了，光顾着缩起身子躲那就要临头的一击，却不肯拿舵轮去迎浪头的打击，刹住船身失控横转的冲势。
凌晨三点，他那副外行做派把大祸引炸了。一眼望见一个比同伙大出许多的浪，他蹲了下去，双手撒开了舵轮辐条。船尾叫那浪抬起来的当口，*弗朗西斯·斯佩特号*船身一偏，尾部舷侧结结实实挨了浪冠全力一甩。下一个瞬间，船已陷进浪谷，下风的舷栏埋进水里，海面齐了舱口围板，一个浪又一个浪翻过迎风的舷栏，冰冷的洪流把甲板上还露在外头的一切扫荡一空。

===Original===
The men were out of hand, helpless and hopeless, stupid in their bewilderment and fear, and resolute only in that they would not obey orders. Some wailed, others clung silently in the weather shrouds, and still others muttered prayers or shrieked vile imprecations; and neither captain nor mate could get them to bear a hand at the pumps or at setting patches of sails to bring the vessel up to the wind and sea. Inside the hour the ship was over on her beam ends, the lubberly cowards climbing up her side and hanging on in the rigging. When she went over, the mate was caught and drowned in the after-cabin, as were two sailors who had sought refuge in the forecastle.
The mate had been the ablest man on board, and the captain was now scarcely less helpless than his men. Beyond cursing them for their worthlessness, he did nothing; and it remained for a man named Mahoney, a Belfast man, and a boy, O’Brien, of Limerick, to cut away the fore and main masts. This they did at great risk on the perpendicular wall of the wreck, sending the mizzentopmast overside along in the general crash. The *Francis Spaight* righted, and it was well that she was lumber laden, else she would have sunk, for she was already waterlogged. The mainmast, still fast by the shrouds, beat like a thunderous sledgehammer against the ship’s side, every stroke bringing groans from the men.
Day dawned on the savage ocean, and in the cold gray light all that could be seen of the *Francis Spaight* emerging from the sea were the poop, the shattered mizzenmast, and a ragged line of bulwarks. It was midwinter in the North Atlantic, and the wretched men were half-dead from cold. But there was no place where they could find rest. Every sea breached clean over the wreck, washing away the salt incrustations from their bodies and depositing fresh incrustations. The cabin under the poop was awash to the knees, but here at least was shelter from the chill wind, and here the survivors congregated, standing upright, holding on by the cabin furnishings, and leaning against one another for support.

===Chinese===
水手们全没了管束，无助又无望，慌乱和恐惧里一片麻木，只在一条上铁了心：绝不服从命令。有人嚎哭，有人闷声抱住迎风一侧的桅索，还有人喃喃祷告，或是尖声咒天骂地；船长也好，大副也好，都驱不动他们搭一把手去抽水，去撑起几片帆，让船迎住风浪。不出一个钟头，船已经整个儿歪倒在横梁尽头上，一群没出息的懦夫爬上船侧，吊在索具里。船翻过去的时候，大副困在后舱里淹死了，同死的还有两个躲进船头舱的水手。
大副本是船上最得力的人，如今船长比他的水手们也强不到哪儿去。除了骂他们一钱不值，他什么也没干；砍掉前桅和主桅的差事，落在一个姓马奥尼的贝尔法斯特人和一个利默里克的小伙子奥布里恩身上。他们冒着大险，在残骸那面直立的墙上抡斧砍了下去，轰隆一声，后桅顶桅也随着那场大塌方翻进了海里。*弗朗西斯·斯佩特号*正了过来。亏得船装的是木材，不然早沉了——船里已经灌满了水。主桅还连着桅索，像一柄打雷的大锤，一下一下砸着船舷，每一下都砸得众人一片呻吟。
白昼在狂暴的洋面上亮起来。寒冷的灰色天光里，*弗朗西斯·斯佩特号*露出海面的，只剩船尾楼、断了的后桅，和一线残破的舷墙。时值北大西洋的隆冬，这伙可怜人已经冻得半死。可他们寻不到一处安身的地方。每一个浪都把残骸整个浇透，把他们身上的盐壳冲掉，再敷上一层新盐壳。船尾楼底下的舱房，水没到膝盖，可这里至少躲得开寒风，活下来的人都聚在这里，直挺挺站着，抓着舱房里的家什，互相挤靠着撑住身子。

===Original===
In vain Mahoney strove to get the men to take turns in watching aloft from the mizzenmast for any chance vessel. The icy gale was too much for them, and they preferred the shelter of the cabin. O’Brien, the boy, who was only fifteen, took turns with Mahoney on the freezing perch. It was the boy, at three in the afternoon, who called down that he had sighted a sail. This did bring them from the cabin, and they crowded the poop rail and weather mizzen shrouds as they watched the strange ship. But its course did not lie near, and when it disappeared below the skyline, they returned shivering to the cabin, not one offering to relieve the watch at the mast head.
By the end of the second day, Mahoney and O’Brien gave up their attempt, and thereafter the vessel drifted in the gale uncared for and without a lookout. There were thirteen alive, and for seventy-two hours they stood knee-deep in the sloshing water on the cabin floor, half-frozen, without food, and with but three bottles of wine shared among them. All food and fresh water were below, and there was no getting at such supplies in the waterlogged condition of the wreck. As the days went by, no food whatever passed their lips. Fresh water, in small quantities, they were able to obtain by holding a cover of a tureen under the saddle of the mizzenmast. But the rain fell infrequently, and they were hard put. When it rained, they also soaked their handkerchiefs, squeezing them out into their mouths or into their shoes. As the wind and sea went down, they were even able to mop the exposed portions of the deck that were free from brine and so add to their water supply. But food they had none, and no way of getting it, though seabirds flew repeatedly overhead.
In the calm weather that followed the gale, after having remained on their feet for ninety-six hours, they were able to find dry planks in the cabin on which to lie. But the long hours of standing in the salt water had caused sores to form on their legs. These sores were extremely painful. The slightest contact or scrape caused severe anguish, and in their weak condition and crowded situation they were continually hurting one another in this manner. Not a man could move about without being followed by volleys of abuse, curses, and groans. So great was their misery that the strong oppressed the weak, shoving them aside from the dry planks to shift for themselves in the cold and wet. The boy, O’Brien, was specially maltreated. Though there were three other boys, it was O’Brien who came in for most of the abuse. There was no explaining it, except on the ground that his was a stronger and more dominant spirit than those of the other boys, and that he stood up more for his rights, resenting the petty injustices that were meted out to all the boys by the men. Whenever O’Brien came near the men in search of a dry place to sleep, or merely moved about, he was kicked and cuffed away. In return, he cursed them for their selfish brutishness, and blows and kicks and curses were rained upon him. Miserable as were all of them, he was thus made far more miserable; and it was only the flame of life, unusually strong in him, that enabled him to endure.

===Chinese===
马奥尼想叫大家轮流爬上后桅瞭望，看有没有过路的船只，白费了口舌。冰冷的狂风他们受不了，宁可挤在舱房里。十五岁的小伙子奥布里恩，同马奥尼轮班蹲那个冰冻的瞭望位。下午三点，正是这小伙子朝下喊，说望见了一张帆。这一下倒真把人从舱房里喊了出来，众人挤满船尾楼的栏杆和迎风的后桅索，望着那条陌生的船。可它的航线离得远，等它消失在天际线下，他们又抖抖索索缩回舱房，没有一个人提出接班到桅顶瞭望。
到第二天将尽的时候，马奥尼和奥布里恩撒手不干了；往后这条船就在大风里随波逐流，没人照管，也没了望。活着的还有十三个。七十二个钟头，他们站在舱底晃荡的水里，水深及膝，半冻着，没有吃的，一共只有三瓶酒大家分。粮食和淡水都在下面，船灌满了水，那样的存货够不着。日子一天天过去，一粒粮食也没进过他们的嘴。淡水，小口小口地，他们还能弄到一点：把汤盆的盖子扣在后桅的桅鞍底下接。可是雨下得稀，他们熬得苦。下雨的时候，他们还把手帕浸透，拧进嘴里，或是拧进鞋里。风浪息下去之后，他们甚至能把甲板上没沾盐的露面部分拖干，给水添点进项。可吃的一点没有，也没法子弄到，虽然海鸟一遍又一遍从头顶飞过。
风暴过后天气静了，站了九十六个钟头之后，他们总算在舱房里寻到几块干木板，能躺一躺了。可是长时辰泡在盐水里站着，腿上都生了疮。疮疼得钻心。轻轻一碰一蹭都是剧痛，他们身子又虚，地方又挤，就这样彼此不断戳着对方的痛处。谁挪一挪窝，背后就是一连串的叫骂、诅咒和呻吟。苦到这个地步，强的欺压弱的，把他们从干木板上推开，叫他们自己在又冷又湿里滚。小伙子奥布里恩格外受作践。船上另有三个孩子，挨骂挨打的大头偏偏落在奥布里恩身上。这没法解释，只能说他的性子比那几个孩子硬气、有主意，更肯挺住自己的权利，大人们派给孩子们种种小不公，他咽不下。奥布里恩每回凑近大人们，想寻块干地方睡，或者只是走动走动，就是一顿拳脚，被打发开。作为回敬，他骂他们自私自利、畜生一样，于是拳脚和咒骂加倍落到他头上。人人都苦，他偏苦上加苦；撑住他的，只有他胸腔里那团格外旺的生命之火。

===Original===
As the days went by and they grew weaker, their peevishness and ill-temper increased, which, in turn, increased the ill-treatment and sufferings of O’Brien. By the sixteenth day all hands were far gone with hunger, and they stood together in small groups, talking in undertones and occasionally glancing at O’Brien. It was at high noon that the conference came to a head. The captain was the spokesman. All were collected on the poop.
“Men,” the captain began, “we have been a long time without food﻿—two weeks and two days it is, though it seems more like two years and two months. We can’t hang out much longer. It is beyond human nature to go on hanging out with nothing in our stomachs. There is a serious question to consider: whether it is better for all to die, or for one to die. We are standing with our feet in our graves. If one of us dies, the rest may live until a ship is sighted. What say you?”
Michael Behane, the man who had been at the wheel when the *Francis Spaight* broached to, called out that it was well. The others joined in the cry.
“Let it be one of the b’ys!” cried Sullivan, a Tarbert man, glancing at the same time significantly at O’Brien.

===Chinese===
日子一天天过去，人越饿越虚，火气坏脾气越养越旺，这又反过来加重了奥布里恩的苦楚。到第十六天，所有的手都饿得脱了形，三三两两聚在一处低声说话，时不时朝奥布里恩瞟一眼。正午时分，这场密议见了分晓。发言人由船长充当。人都聚在船尾楼上。
"伙计们，"船长开口，"咱们断粮好久了——两周零两天了，虽说说起来像两年零两个月。咱们撑不了多久了。肚里空空还能撑下去，这不是人力办得到的。有一桩要紧的事得掂量：是大家一起死好，还是死一个好。咱们都已经半截入土了。咱们中间死一个，其余的兴许就能活到望见船。你们怎么说？"
*弗朗西斯·斯佩特号*横转出事时掌舵的迈克尔·贝恩高声叫好。旁人跟着一齐喊好。
"就叫哪个小的去罢！"沙利文喊道。他是塔伯特人，一面说，一面意味深长地瞟了奥布里恩一眼。

===Original===
“It is my opinion,” the captain went on, “that it will be a good deed for one of us to die for the rest.”
“A good deed! A good deed!” the men interjected.
“And it is my opinion that ’tis best for one of the boys to die. They have no families to support, nor would they be considered so great a loss to their friends as those who have wives and children.”
“ ’Tis right.” “Very right.” “Very fit it should be done,” the men muttered one to another.

===Chinese===
"依我看，"船长接着说，"咱们中间死一个，救其余的，是一桩善举。"
"善举！善举！"众人你一言我一语地插话。
"我还看，死一个孩子顶好。他们没有家小要养活，比起有老婆孩子的人，朋友们也不至于把他们的死看得那么重。"
"说得是。""很对。""正该这么办，"人们交头接耳。

===Original===
But the four boys cried out against the injustice of it.
“Our lives is just as dear to us as the rest iv yez,” O’Brien protested. “An’ our famblies, too. As for wives an’ childer, who is there savin’ meself to care for me old mother that’s a widow, as you know well, Michael Behane, that comes from Limerick? ’Tis not fair. Let the lots be drawn between all of us, men and b’ys.”
Mahoney was the only man who spoke in favour of the boys, declaring that it was the fair thing for all to share alike. Sullivan and the captain insisted on the drawing of lots being confined to the boys. There were high words, in the midst of which Sullivan turned upon O’Brien, snarling﻿—
“ ’Twould be a good deed to put you out of the way. You deserve it. ’Twould be the right way to serve you, an’ serve you we will.”

===Chinese===
可四个孩子齐声叫屈，骂这不公道。
"俺们的命，同你们大伙儿的命一样金贵，"奥布里恩抗议，"俺们也有家小。说到老婆孩子——俺家有个守寡的老娘，除了俺，哪个肯照应她？这你是知道的，迈克尔·贝恩，你也是利默里克人。这不公道。要抽签，就大人和孩子一齐抽。"
替孩子们说话的大人只有马奥尼一个，他说有福同享、有难同当才算公道。沙利文和船长咬定抽签只限孩子们。一场恶言相向，闹到半截，沙利文猛地转向奥布里恩，龇着牙——
"把你这小子结果了，才叫善举。你活该。这么办才是正经，俺们也就这么办。"

===Original===
He started toward O’Brien, with intent to lay hands on him and proceed at once with the killing, while several others likewise shuffled toward him and reached for him. He stumbled backwards to escape them, at the same time crying that he would submit to the drawing of the lots among the boys.
The captain prepared four sticks of different lengths and handed them to Sullivan.
“You’re thinkin’ the drawin’ll not be fair,” the latter sneered to O’Brien. “So it’s yerself’ll do the drawin’.”
To this O’Brien agreed. A handkerchief was tied over his eyes, blindfolding him, and he knelt down on the deck with his back to Sullivan.

===Chinese===
他朝奥布里恩扑过去，要当场动手，先结果了再说，另有几个也拖着脚步围上来，朝他伸手。他跌跌撞撞往后退，一面喊他愿意服从在孩子们中间抽签。
船长备下四根长短不同的签，交给沙利文。
"你准想着抽签不公道，"后者冲奥布里恩冷笑，"那就叫你自个儿来抽。"
这个奥布里恩依了。一条手帕蒙上他的眼睛，他背朝沙利文跪在甲板上。

===Original===
“Whoever you name for the shortest stick’ll die,” the captain said.
Sullivan held up one of the sticks. The rest were concealed in his hand so that no one could see whether it was the short stick or not.
“An’ whose stick will it be?” Sullivan demanded.
“For little Johnny Sheehan,” O’Brien answered.

===Chinese===
"你点名抽着短签的，就得死，"船长说。
沙利文举起一根签。其余的攥在手心里，谁也看不清是不是那根短的。
"这根归谁？"沙利文问。
"给小约翰尼·希汉，"奥布里恩答。

===Original===
Sullivan laid the stick aside. Those who looked could not tell if it were the fatal one. Sullivan held up another stick.
“Whose will it be?”
“For George Burns,” was the reply.
The stick was laid with the first one, and a third held up.

===Chinese===
沙利文把签放到一边。看的人辨不出那是不是催命的一根。沙利文又举起一根。
"这根归谁？"
"给乔治·伯恩斯，"这一声回答。
这根同头一根放在一处，又举起第三根。

===Original===
“An’ whose is this wan?”
“For myself,” said O’Brien.
With a quick movement, Sullivan threw the four sticks together. No one had seen.
“ ’Tis for yourself ye’ve drawn it,” Sullivan announced.

===Chinese===
"这根又是谁的？"
"给我自己，"奥布里恩说。
沙利文手快，把四根签一总混作一堆。谁也没瞧见。
"这签是你自个儿抽着的，"沙利文宣布。

===Original===
“A good deed,” several of the men muttered.
O’Brien was very quiet. He arose to his feet, took the bandage off, and looked around.
“Where is ut?” he demanded. “The short stick? The wan for me?”
The captain pointed to the four sticks lying on the deck.

===Chinese===
"善举啊，"人群里有几个咕哝。
奥布里恩非常安静。他站起身，解下手帕，四下里看了看。
"在哪儿？"他问。"短签呢？归我的那根呢？"
船长指了指躺在甲板上的四根签。

===Original===
“How do you know the stick was mine?” O’Brien questioned. “Did you see ut, Johnny Sheehan?”
Johnny Sheehan, who was the youngest of the boys, did not answer.
“Did you see ut?” O’Brien next asked Mahoney.
“No, I didn’t see ut.”

===Chinese===
"你怎么知道那根签是我的？"奥布里恩质问。"你看见了吗，约翰尼·希汉？"
约翰尼·希汉是孩子里顶小的，他没有答话。
"你看见了吗？"奥布里恩又问马奥尼。
"没，俺没看见。"

===Original===
The men were muttering and growling.
“ ’Twas a fair drawin’,” Sullivan said. “Ye had yer chanct an’ ye lost, that’s all iv ut.”
“A fair drawin’,” the captain added. “Didn’t I behold it myself? The stick was yours, O’Brien, an’ ye may as well get ready. Where’s the cook? Gorman, come here. Fetch the tureen cover, some of ye. Gorman, do your duty like a man.”
“But how’ll I do it,” the cook demanded. He was a weak-eyed, weak-chinned, indecisive man.

===Chinese===
众人嗡嗡地咕哝，隐隐地咆哮。
"抽得不偏不倚，"沙利文说。"机会给了你，你输了，就这么回事。"
"抽得不偏不倚，"船长帮腔。"俺不是亲眼瞅着的么？签是你的，奥布里恩，你趁早预备罢。厨子呢？戈尔曼，过来。你们哪个去把汤盆盖拿来。戈尔曼，拿出男人的样子，尽你的本分。"
"可叫俺怎么下手，"厨子反问。他是个眼神发虚、下巴发软、拿不定主意的人。

===Original===
“ ’Tis a damned murder!” O’Brien cried out.
“I’ll have none of ut,” Mahoney announced. “Not a bite shall pass me lips.”
“Then ’tis yer share for better men than yerself,” Sullivan sneered. “Go on with yer duty, cook.”
“ ’Tis not me duty, the killin’ of b’ys,” Gorman protested irresolutely.

===Chinese===
"这是该死的谋杀！"奥布里恩喊起来。
"俺一口也不沾，"马奥尼宣布。"休想有一口过俺的嘴唇。"
"那你那份就便宜比你强的爷们了，"沙利文冷笑。"干你的本分去罢，厨子。"
"杀孩子可不是俺的本分，"戈尔曼犹犹豫豫地抗辩。

===Original===
“If yez don’t make mate for us, we’ll be makin’ mate of yerself,” Behane threatened. “Somebody must die, an’ as well you as another.”
Johnny Sheehan began to cry. O’Brien listened anxiously. His face was pale. His lips trembled, and at times his whole body shook.
“I signed on as cook,” Gorman enounced. “An’ cook I wud if galley there was. But I’ll not lay me hand to murder. ’Tis not in the articles. I’m the cook﻿—”
“An’ cook ye’ll be for wan minute more only,” Sullivan said grimly, at the same moment gripping the cook’s head from behind and bending it back till the windpipe and jugular were stretched taut. “Where’s yer knife, Mike? Pass it along.”

===Chinese===
"你们不给俺们弄肉，俺们就拿你自己弄肉，"贝恩威胁道。"总得死一个，死谁不是死。"
约翰尼·希汉哭起来了。奥布里恩焦灼地听着。他脸色发白。嘴唇打颤，有时浑身上下都在抖。
"俺上船签的是厨子，"戈尔曼一字一顿地说。"有厨房，俺就当厨子。可俺的手不沾谋杀。合同上没这一条。俺是厨子——"
"你的厨子也就再当一分钟了，"沙利文狞声说道，同时从背后一把攥住厨子的脑袋，往后扳，扳得气管和颈静脉绷得笔直。"你的刀呢，迈克？递过来。"

===Original===
At the touch of the steel, Gorman whimpered.
“I’ll do ut, if yez’ll hold the b’y.”
The pitiable condition of the cook seemed in some fashion to nerve up O’Brien.
“It’s all right, Gorman,” he said. “Go on with ut. ’Tis meself knows yer not wantin’ to do ut. It’s all right, sir”﻿—this to the captain, who had laid a hand heavily on his arm. “Ye won’t have to hold me, sir. I’ll stand still.”

===Chinese===
钢刀一沾皮肉，戈尔曼呜咽起来。
"俺干，你们按住这孩子。"
厨子那副可怜相，倒像给了奥布里恩几分胆气。
"没事的，戈尔曼，"他说。"动手罢。俺知道你不是存心的。没事的，先生"——这一句是对船长说的，船长一只手沉沉压着他的胳膊。"你们不用按着我，先生。我站着不动。"

===Original===
“Stop yer blitherin’, an’ go an’ get the tureen cover,” Behane commanded Johnny Sheehan, at the same time dealing him a heavy cuff alongside the head.
The boy, who was scarcely more than a child, fetched the cover. He crawled and tottered along the deck, so weak was he from hunger. The tears still ran down his cheeks. Behane took the cover from him, at the same time administering another cuff.
O’Brien took off his coat and bared his right arm. His under lip still trembled, but he held a tight grip on himself. The captain’s penknife was opened and passed to Gorman.
“Mahoney, tell me mother what happened to me, if ever ye get back,” O’Brien requested.

===Chinese===
"嚎什么嚎，去把汤盆盖拿来，"贝恩喝令约翰尼·希汉，扬手照他脑袋侧面就是重重一巴掌。
那孩子实在还脱不了稚气，把盖子取来了。他一路在甲板上爬着、晃着，饿得只剩一把劲。眼泪还顺着腮帮子淌。贝恩从他手里接过盖子，顺手又赏他一巴掌。
奥布里恩脱下外衣，裸出右臂。下唇还在抖，可他把自身攥得紧紧的。船长的小折刀打开来，递给了戈尔曼。
"马奥尼，你要是能回去，把我的事告诉俺娘，"奥布里恩托付道。

===Original===
Mahoney nodded.
“ ’Tis black murder, black an’ damned,” he said. “The b’y’s flesh’ll do none iv yez anny good. Mark me words. Ye’ll not profit by it, none iv yez.”
“Get ready,” the captain ordered. “You, Sullivan, hold the cover﻿—that’s it﻿—close up. Spill nothing. It’s precious stuff.”
Gorman made an effort. The knife was dull. He was weak. Besides, his hand was shaking so violently that he nearly dropped the knife. The three boys were crouched apart, in a huddle, crying and sobbing. With the exception of Mahoney, the men were gathered about the victim, craning their necks to see.

===Chinese===
马奥尼点点头。
"这是黑心的谋杀，黑心、该下地狱，"他说。"这孩子的肉，对你们哪个也没有好处。记住俺的话。你们占不着便宜，一个也占不着。"
"预备，"船长下令。"你，沙利文，端着盖子——对——凑紧。一滴也别洒。这可是金贵东西。"
戈尔曼使了把劲。刀是钝的。人是虚的。何况那只手抖得厉害，险些把刀掉下去。三个孩子缩在一边，抱作一团，哭哭啼啼。除了马奥尼，人们都围在那个牺牲品四周，伸长脖子看。

===Original===
“Be a man, Gorman,” the captain cautioned.
The wretched cook was seized with a spasm of resolution, sawing back and forth with the blade on O’Brien’s wrist. The veins were severed. Sullivan held the tureen cover close underneath. The cut veins gaped wide, but no ruddy flood gushed forth. There was no blood at all. The veins were dry and empty. No one spoke. The grim and silent figures swayed in unison with each heave of the ship. Every eye was turned fixedly upon that inconceivable and monstrous thing, the dry veins of a creature that was alive.
“ ’Tis a warnin’,” Mahoney cried. “Lave the b’y alone. Mark me words. His death’ll do none iv yez anny good.”
“Try at the elbow﻿—the left elbow, ’tis nearer the heart,” the captain said finally, in a dim and husky voice that was unlike his own.

===Chinese===
"拿出男人样，戈尔曼，"船长提醒。
那可怜的厨子猛地横下一条心，拿刀刃在奥布里恩腕上来来回回地锯。血管割断了。沙利文把汤盆盖在底下紧紧接着。割开的血管豁着大口，却没有红潮涌出来。血一点儿也没有。血管又干又空。没有人说话。一具具阴沉沉默的身影，随着船身每一次颠簸一齐晃动。每一双眼睛都直勾勾盯在那件不可思议的怪东西上——一个活人身上干涸的血管。
"这是天意示警！"马奥尼喊道。"放这孩子去罢。记住俺的话。他的死，对你们哪个也没有好处。"
"试试肘弯——左肘弯，离心脏近些，"船长终于开口，嗓音低哑发闷，不像他自己的。

===Original===
“Give me the knife,” O’Brien said roughly, taking it out of the cook’s hand. “I can’t be lookin’ at ye puttin’ me to hurt.”
Quite coolly he cut the vein at the left elbow, but, like the cook, he failed to bring blood.
“This is all iv no use,” Sullivan said. “ ’Tis better to put him out iv his misery by bleedin’ him at the throat.”
The strain had been too much for the lad.

===Chinese===
"把刀给我，"奥布里恩粗声说，从厨子手里把刀拿了过去。"看着你们叫我受疼，我看不下去。"
他镇定自若地割开左肘的血管，可是同厨子一样，没能放出血来。
"这全白搭，"沙利文说。"不如割喉咙放血，送他早点解脱。"
这份煎熬，孩子熬不住了。

===Original===
“Don’t be doin’ ut,” he cried. “There’ll be no blood in me throat. Give me a little time. ’Tis cold an’ weak I am. Be lettin’ me lay down an’ slape a bit. Then I’ll be warm an’ the blood’ll flow.”
“ ’Tis no use,” Sullivan objected. “As if ye cud be slapin’ at a time like this. Ye’ll not slape, and ye’ll not warm up. Look at ye now. You’ve an ague.”
“I was sick at Limerick wan night,” O’Brien hurried on, “an’ the dochtor cudn’t bleed me. But after slapin’ a few hours an’ gettin’ warm in bed the blood came freely. It’s God’s truth I’m tellin’ yez. Don’t be murderin’ me!”
“His veins are open now,” the captain said. “ ’Tis no use leavin’ him in his pain. Do it now an’ be done with it.”

===Chinese===
"别割了，"他哭喊。"我喉咙里也不会有血的。给我点工夫。我又冷又虚。让我躺下睡一小会儿。等我焐暖了，血就下来了。"
"没用的，"沙利文反对。"这种时候你还睡得着？你睡不着的，也暖不过来。瞧瞧你这会儿。你都打摆子了。"
"有一晚俺在利默里克生病，"奥布里恩急急地说，"大夫就放不出俺的血。可睡了几个钟头，在床上焐暖了，血就痛痛快快地下来了。俺说的是上帝的真话。别杀俺！"
"他的血管已经开了，"船长说。"叫他这么干疼着也没用。这就动手，办完了事。"

===Original===
They started to reach for O’Brien, but he backed away.
“I’ll be the death iv yez!” he screamed. “Take yer hands off iv me, Sullivan! I’ll come back! I’ll haunt yez! Wakin’ or slapin’, I’ll haunt yez till you die!”
“ ’Tis disgraceful!” yelled Behane. “If the short stick’d ben mine, I’d a-let me mates cut the head off iv me an’ died happy.”
Sullivan leaped in and caught the unhappy lad by the hair. The rest of the men followed, O’Brien kicked and struggled, snarling and snapping at the hands that clutched him from every side. Little Johnny Sheehan broke out into wild screaming, but the men took no notice of him. O’Brien was bent backward to the deck, the tureen cover under his neck. Gorman was shoved forward. Some one had thrust a large sheath-knife into his hand.

===Chinese===
他们又朝奥布里恩伸手，他直往后退。
"我要你们的命！"他尖叫。"撒手，沙利文！我会回来的！我做鬼也缠着你们！醒着睡着，我都缠着你们，缠到你们死！"
"真不要脸！"贝恩吼道。"短签要是抽着俺，俺早叫伙计们把俺脑袋剁了，死得痛痛快快。"
沙利文扑上去，一把揪住那不幸孩子的头发。其余的人跟着一拥而上。奥布里恩又踢又挣，朝四面八方攥住他的手又咬又啃。小约翰尼·希汉放声尖哭，没人理他。奥布里恩被仰面按倒在甲板上，汤盆盖垫在他脖子底下。戈尔曼被推上前来。不知是谁往他手里塞了一柄大号鞘刀。

===Original===
“Do yer duty! Do yer duty!” the men cried.
The cook bent over, but he caught the boy’s eyes and faltered.
“If ye don’t, I’ll kill ye with me own hands,” Behane shouted.
From every side a torrent of abuse and threats poured in upon the cook. Still he hung back.

===Chinese===
"尽你的本分！尽你的本分！"众人喊。
厨子弯下腰去，可是撞上了孩子的目光，手软了。
"你不干，俺就亲手宰了你，"贝恩吼道。
辱骂和威吓像急流，从四面八方朝厨子浇下来。他兀自退缩着。

===Original===
“Maybe there’ll be more blood in his veins than O’Brien’s,” Sullivan suggested significantly.
Behane caught Gorman by the hair and twisted his head back, while Sullivan attempted to take possession of the sheath-knife. But Gorman clung to it desperately.
“Lave go, an’ I’ll do ut!” he screamed frantically. “Don’t be cuttin’ me throat! I’ll do the deed! I’ll do the deed!”
“See that you do it, then,” the captain threatened him.

===Chinese===
"他血管里的血，兴许比奥布里恩的多呢，"沙利文意味深长地提点。
贝恩揪住戈尔曼的头发，把他的头拧向后面，沙利文趁势要夺那柄鞘刀。可戈尔曼死命攥住不放。
"撒手，俺干！"他发了疯似的尖叫。"别割俺的喉咙！俺干就是！俺干就是！"
"那你可得说到做到，"船长威吓他。

===Original===
Gorman allowed himself to be shoved forward. He looked at the boy, closed his eyes, and muttered a prayer. Then, without opening his eyes, he did the deed that had been appointed him. O’Brien emitted a shriek that sank swiftly to a gurgling sob. The men held him till his struggles ceased, when he was laid upon the deck. They were eager and impatient, and with oaths and threats they urged Gorman to hurry with the preparation of the meal.
“Lave ut, you bloody butchers,” Mahoney said quietly. “Lave ut, I tell yez. Ye’ll not be needin’ anny iv ut now. ’Tis as I said: ye’ll not be profitin’ by the lad’s blood. Empty ut overside, Behane. Empty ut overside.”
Behane, still holding the tureen cover in both his hands, glanced to windward. He walked to the rail and threw the cover and contents into the sea. A full-rigged ship was bearing down upon them a short mile away. So occupied had they been with the deed just committed, that none had had eyes for a lookout. All hands watched her coming on﻿—the brightly coppered forefoot parting the water like a golden knife, the headsails flapping lazily and emptily at each downward surge, and the towering canvas tiers dipping and curtsying with each stately swing of the sea. No man spoke.
As she hove to, a cable length away, the captain of the *Francis Spaight* bestirred himself and ordered a tarpaulin to be thrown over O’Brien’s corpse. A boat was lowered from the stranger’s side and began to pull toward them. John Gorman laughed. He laughed softly at first, but he accompanied each stroke of the oars with spasmodically increasing glee. It was this maniacal laughter that greeted the rescue boat as it hauled alongside and the first officer clambered on board.

===Chinese===
戈尔曼由着众人把他推上前。他望了那孩子一眼，闭上眼，念念有词地祷告了一句。然后，眼睛也没睁，他做完了派给他的那件事。奥布里恩迸出一声惨叫，很快低下去，成了咯咯的呜咽。人们按着他，直到他不再挣扎，才把他平放在甲板上。他们又急又馋，连咒带吓，催着戈尔曼赶紧把那顿饭预备出来。
"放下，你们这群血屠夫，"马奥尼平静地说。"放下，俺告诉你们。这会儿你们用不着它了。俺早说了：这孩子的血，你们占不着便宜。倒到海里去，贝恩。倒到海里去。"
贝恩双手还端着那只汤盆盖，朝上风望了一眼。他走到舷墙边，把盖子和里面的东西一齐抛进了海里。一艘全帆装的船正朝他们压过来，离着不过一英里。方才众人的心神全在那桩勾当上，谁也没顾上瞭望。所有人都望着它开来——亮闪闪的铜包皮船首破开水面，像一柄金刀；头帆随着船身每一次下坠，懒懒地、空空地拍打着；一层高过一层的帆布，随着海面庄严的每一次起伏，点着头，屈着膝。没有一个人说话。
那船在一链之外顶风停住，*弗朗西斯·斯佩特号*的船长忙活起来，吩咐拿一块柏油帆布盖上奥布里恩的尸身。一条小艇从陌生船的舷边放下，朝他们划来。约翰·戈尔曼笑起来。起初是低低地笑，可桨手每划一桨，他的笑就一阵紧似一阵地发作。救命的小艇靠上船舷、大副攀上甲板的时候，迎接他们的，就是这一阵疯笑。

## A Curious Fragment / 一篇奇特的残稿

===Original===
> [The capitalist, or industrial oligarch, Roger Vanderwater, mentioned in the narrative, has been identified as the ninth in the line of the Vanderwaters that controlled for hundreds of years the cotton factories of the South. This Roger Vanderwater flourished in the last decades of the twenty-sixth century after Christ, which was the fifth century of the terrible industrial oligarchy that was reared upon the ruins of the early Republic.
> From internal evidences we are convinced that the narrative which follows was not reduced to writing till the twenty-ninth century. Not only was it unlawful to write or print such matter during that period, but the working-class was so illiterate that only in rare instances were its members able to read and write. This was the dark reign of the overman, in whose speech the great mass of the people were characterized as the “herd animals.” All literacy was frowned upon and stamped out. From the statute-books of the times may be instanced that black law that made it a capital offence for any man, no matter of what class, to teach even the alphabet to a member of the working-class. Such stringent limitation of education to the ruling class was necessary if that class was to continue to rule.
> One result of the foregoing was the development of the professional storytellers. These storytellers were paid by the oligarchy, and the tales they told were legendary, mythical, romantic, and harmless. But the spirit of freedom never quite died out, and agitators, under the guise of storytellers, preached revolt to the slave class. That the following tale was banned by the oligarchs we have proof from the records of the criminal police court of Ashbury, wherein, on January 27, 2734, one John Tourney, found guilty of telling the tale in a boozing-ken of labourers, was sentenced to five years’ penal servitude in the borax mines of the Arizona Desert.]
> — Editor’s Note

===Chinese===
> ［文中提到的资本家、亦即工业寡头罗杰·范德沃特，已经考定是范德沃特世家第九代；这个世家垄断南方各棉纺厂数百年。这位罗杰·范德沃特显达于基督纪元二十六世纪末叶，那正是可怕的工业寡头政治的第五个世纪——这一寡头政治，建立在那早期共和国的废墟之上。
> 依据内在证据，我们确信下面这篇记事直到二十九世纪才落成文字。在那个时期，书写或印刷此类文字不但违法，而且工人阶级目不识丁，其成员能读会写的百中无一。那是"超人"的黑暗统治，在他们的言谈里，黎民百姓被唤作"群畜"。一切识字都遭白眼，遭扑灭。当时的法典里可以举出那样一条黑色法律：无论何等出身，凡把哪怕字母表教给工人阶级一员的，一律处死。要把教育如此严苛地圈禁在统治阶级之内，那个阶级要继续统治，就非这样不可。
> 上述情形的一个结果，是职业说书人的兴盛。这些说书人受寡头豢养，所讲的故事不是传说、神话，便是风月闲篇，于人无害。然而自由的精神从未死绝，鼓动家们借着说书人的幌子，向奴隶阶级传布造反。下面这个故事曾遭寡头查禁，我们有阿什伯里刑事法庭的档案为证：2734 年 1 月 27 日，约翰·图尔尼被查明在劳工酒窟（boozing-ken）讲说此篇，判处五年苦役，发往亚利桑那沙漠的硼砂矿。］
> ——编者按

===Original===
Listen, my brothers, and I will tell you a tale of an arm. It was the arm of Tom Dixon, and Tom Dixon was a weaver of the first class in a factory of that hellhound and master, Roger Vanderwater. This factory was called “Hell’s Bottom”﻿ ﻿… by the slaves who toiled in it, and I guess they ought to know; and it was situated in Kingsbury, at the other end of the town from Vanderwater’s summer palace. You do not know where Kingsbury is? There are many things, my brothers, that you do not know, and it is sad. It is because you do not know that you are slaves. When I have told you this tale, I should like to form a class among you for the learning of written and printed speech. Our masters read and write and possess many books, and it is because of that that they are our masters, and live in palaces, and do not work. When the toilers learn to read and write﻿—all of them﻿—they will grow strong; then they will use their strength to break their bonds, and there will be no more masters and no more slaves.
Kingsbury, my brothers, is in the old State of Alabama. For three hundred years the Vanderwaters have owned Kingsbury and its slave pens and factories, and slave pens and factories in many other places and States. You have heard of the Vanderwaters﻿—who has not?﻿—but let me tell you things you do not know about them. The first Vanderwater was a slave, even as you and I. Have you got that? He was a slave, and that was over three hundred years ago. His father was a machinist in the slave pen of Alexander Burrell, and his mother was a washerwoman in the same slave pen. There is no doubt about this. I am telling you truth. It is history. It is printed, every word of it, in the history books of our masters, which you cannot read because your masters will not permit you to learn to read. You can understand why they will not permit you to learn to read, when there are such things in the books. They know, and they are very wise. If you did read such things, you might be wanting in respect to your masters, which would be a dangerous thing﻿ ﻿… to your masters. But I know, for I can read, and I am telling you what I have read with my own eyes in the history books of our masters.
The first Vanderwater’s name was not Vanderwater; it was Vange﻿—Bill Vange, the son of Yergis Vange, the machinist, and Laura Carnly, the washerwoman. Young Bill Vange was strong. He might have remained with the slaves and led them to freedom; instead, however, he served the masters and was well rewarded. He began his service, when yet a small child, as a spy in his home slave pen. He is known to have informed on his own father for seditious utterance. This is fact. I have read it with my own eyes in the records. He was too good a slave for the slave pen. Alexander Burrell took him out, while yet a child, and he was taught to read and write. He was taught many things, and he was entered in the secret service of the Government. Of course, he no longer wore the slave dress, except for disguise at such times when he sought to penetrate the secrets and plots of the slaves. It was he, when but eighteen years of age, who brought that great hero and comrade, Ralph Jacobus, to trial and execution in the electric chair. Of course, you have all heard the sacred name of Ralph Jacobus, but it is news to you that he was brought to his death by the first Vanderwater, whose name was Vange. I know. I have read it in the books. There are many interesting things like that in the books.

===Chinese===
听着，弟兄们，我给你们讲一条胳膊的故事。那是汤姆·迪克逊的胳膊。汤姆·迪克逊是头等织工，在一座工场里给那个地狱恶鬼、那个主子罗杰·范德沃特做活。那座工场叫"地狱底"……这么叫它的，正是在里头做牛做马的奴隶们，我想他们最有资格知道；它坐落在金斯伯里，同范德沃特的夏宫隔着整整一座城。你们不知道金斯伯里在哪儿？弟兄们，你们不知道的事多着呢，这就可悲。正因为你们不知道，你们才是奴隶。等我把这故事讲完，我想在你们中间开个班，教大家学手写和印刷的文字。咱们的主子们识文断字，坐拥许多书，正因为这个，他们才是咱们的主子，住宫殿，不干活。做工的人等全都识字了——一个不落——就会强大起来；到那时他们会拿这力气挣断枷锁，世上就再没有主人，也再没有奴隶。
金斯伯里，弟兄们，在古老的亚拉巴马州。三百年了，范德沃特家占着金斯伯里，占着它的奴隶营和工场，还占着别处许多地方许多州的奴隶营和工场。范德沃特家你们听说过——谁没听说过？——可让我来讲点你们不知道的。头一个范德沃特是个奴隶，同你我一样。听清了么？他是个奴隶，那是三百多年前的事。他爹是奴隶营里的机器匠，给亚历山大·伯勒尔干活；他娘是同一座奴隶营里的洗衣妇。这毫无疑问。我讲的是真话。这是历史。一字一句都印着，印在咱们主子们的历史书里——那些书你们读不了，因为主子们不许你们学认字。书里存着这样的东西，你们就明白他们为什么不许你们认字了。他们心里有数，他们精明得很。你们要是读了这样的东西，对主子们只怕就欠缺恭敬了，那可是桩危险的事……对主子们危险。可我知道，因为我识字，主子们历史书里的话，我是亲眼读过的，如今讲给你们听。
头一个范德沃特，本来不姓范德沃特；他姓范奇——比尔·范奇，机器匠耶吉斯·范奇和洗衣妇劳拉·坎利的儿子。小比尔·范奇身强力壮。他本可以留在奴隶们中间，领着他们奔自由；可他没有，他投靠了主子们，得了厚赏。他还是个小娃娃，就开始给主子当差，在自家的奴隶营里做坐探。有案可查，他曾告发自己的亲爹出言犯上。这是事实。档案里写着，我亲眼读过。做奴隶，他做得太出色了。亚历山大·伯勒尔把他从奴隶营里挑出来，从小教他读书写字。他学了一身本事，进了政府的特务机构。当然，他从此不穿奴隶的衣裳了，只有化装混进奴隶堆里、刺探秘密阴谋的时候才穿。正是他，在十八岁上，把那位大英雄、好同志拉尔夫·雅各布斯送上法庭，送进了电椅。拉尔夫·雅各布斯这个神圣的名字你们都听说过，可置他于死地的，正是头一个范德沃特，那时他姓范奇——这一层你们就不知道了。我知道。我在书里读到过。书里这样有意思的事多着呢。

===Original===
And after Ralph Jacobus died his shameful death, Bill Vange’s name began the many changes it was to undergo. He was known as “Sly Vange” far and wide. He rose high in the secret service, and he was rewarded in grand ways, but still he was not a member of the master class. The men were willing that he should become so; it was the women of the master class who refused to have Sly Vange one of them. Sly Vange gave good service to the masters. He had been a slave himself, and he knew the ways of the slaves. There was no fooling him. In those days the slaves were braver than now, and they were always trying for their freedom. And Sly Vange was everywhere, in all their schemes and plans, bringing their schemes and plans to naught and their leaders to the electric chair. It was in 2255 that his name was next changed for him. It was in that year that the Great Mutiny took place. In that region west of the Rocky Mountains, seventeen millions of slaves strove bravely to overthrow their masters. Who knows, if Sly Vange had not lived, but that they would have succeeded? But Sly Vange was very much alive. The masters gave him supreme command of the situation. In eight months of fighting, one million and three hundred and fifty thousand slaves were killed. Vange, Bill Vange, Sly Vange, killed them, and he broke the Great Mutiny. And he was greatly rewarded, and so red were his hands with the blood of the slaves that thereafter he was called “Bloody Vange.” You see, my brothers, what interesting things are to be found in the books when one can read them. And, take my word for it, there are many other things, even more interesting, in the books. And if you will but study with me, in a year’s time you can read those books for yourselves﻿—ay, in six months some of you will be able to read those books for yourselves.
Bloody Vange lived to a ripe old age, and always, to the last, was he received in the councils of the masters; but never was he made a master himself. He had first opened his eyes, you see, in a slave pen. But oh, he was well rewarded! He had a dozen palaces in which to live. He, who was no master, owned thousands of slaves. He had a great pleasure yacht upon the sea that was a floating palace, and he owned a whole island in the sea where toiled ten thousand slaves on his coffee plantations. But in his old age he was lonely, for he lived apart, hated by his brothers, the slaves, and looked down upon by those he had served and who refused to be his brothers. The masters looked down upon him because he had been born a slave. Enormously wealthy he died; but he died horribly, tormented by his conscience, regretting all he had done and the red stain on his name.
But with his children it was different. They had not been born in the slave pen, and by the special ruling of the Chief Oligarch of that time, John Morrison, they were elevated to the master class. And it was then that the name of Vange disappears from the page of history. It becomes Vanderwater, and Jason Vange, the son of Bloody Vange, becomes Jason Vanderwater, the founder of the Vanderwater line. But that was three hundred years ago, and the Vanderwaters of today forget their beginnings and imagine that somehow the clay of their bodies is different stuff from the clay in your body and mine and in the bodies of all slaves. And I ask you, Why should a slave become the master of another slave? And why should the son of a slave become the master of many slaves? I leave these questions for you to answer for yourselves, but do not forget that in the beginning the Vanderwaters were slaves.

===Chinese===
拉尔夫·雅各布斯蒙羞而死之后，比尔·范奇的名字就开始了它此后的几番更改。他得名"狡范奇"，名扬四方。他在特务机构里步步高升，赏赐丰厚，可他仍旧算不得统治阶级。男人们倒愿意收他进去；是统治阶级的女人们不肯让"狡范奇"厕身其间。"狡范奇"给主子们办的差事，办得漂亮。他当过奴隶，奴隶的路数他一清二楚。糊弄不了他。那个年月，奴隶们比现在有血性，时时刻刻图谋自由。而"狡范奇"无处不在，钻进他们的一切计谋筹划，把计谋筹划搅成泡影，把领头的送进电椅。到 2255 年，他的名字又替他改了一回。就在那一年，大暴动爆发了。落基山脉以西，一千七百万奴隶奋起，要推翻他们的主人。谁说得准呢，倘若没有"狡范奇"，他们岂不就成了？可"狡范奇"活得好好的。主子们授予他全权，主持大局。八个月的战斗，一百三十五万奴隶被杀。范奇，比尔·范奇，"狡范奇"，杀了他们，也把大暴动扑灭了。他得了重赏，他那双手叫奴隶的血染得那样红，从此人称"血范奇"。弟兄们，你们瞧瞧，识了字，书里有多少有意思的东西。信我的话，书里还有更有意思的呢。只要肯跟我学，一年工夫，你们就能自己读那些书了——嗐，用不了，你们中间有人六个月就能自己读那些书了。
"血范奇"活到高寿，到末了，主子们的议事厅里始终有他一座。可他始终没做成主子。你们想，他睁开眼看见的头一眼，是奴隶营。可他得的赏赐真厚！他有一打宫殿好住。他这个算不得主子的人，占有几千奴隶。他在海上有一艘大游艇，一座浮动的宫殿；还占着海里一整座岛，岛上一万奴隶在他的咖啡种植园里做苦工。可是到了晚年他孤单了。他离群索居，奴隶兄弟们恨他，他效过力的人家看不起他，不肯认他做兄弟。主子们看不起他，因为他生在奴隶营。他死的时候富可敌国，可死得可怕，受着良心的煎熬，悔着生前的一切，悔着名字上那摊洗不掉的血渍。
轮到他的儿女就不同了。他们不是生在奴隶营里的；凭着当时大寡头约翰·莫里森的特旨，他们被擢入了统治阶级。也正是在那时，范奇这个姓氏从史册上消失了。它变成了范德沃特，"血范奇"之子杰森·范奇，变成了杰森·范德沃特，范德沃特世家的开山鼻祖。可那是三百年前的事了，如今的范德沃特们忘了自己的来历，只当自家身上的泥，同你我身上的泥、同一切奴隶身上的泥，是两样东西。我倒要问你们：一个奴隶，凭什么去做另一个奴隶的主子？一个奴隶的儿子，凭什么去做千千万万奴隶的主子？这两个问题，留给你们自己去答，只是别忘了，当初起头的时候，范德沃特家也是奴隶。

===Original===
And now, my brothers, I come back to the beginning of my tale to tell you of Tom Dixon’s arm. Roger Vanderwater’s factory in Kingsbury was rightly named “Hell’s Bottom,” but the men who toiled in it were men, as you shall see. Women toiled there, too, and children, little children. All that toiled there had the regular slave rights under the law, but only under the law, for they were deprived of many of their rights by the two overseers of Hell’s Bottom, Joseph Clancy and Adolph Munster.
It is a long story, but I shall not tell all of it to you. I shall tell only about the arm. It happened that, according to the law, a portion of the starvation wage of the slaves was held back each month and put into a fund. This fund was for the purpose of helping such unfortunate fellow-workmen as happened to be injured by accidents or to be overtaken by sickness. As you know with yourselves, these funds are controlled by the overseers. It is the law, and so it was that the fund at Hell’s Bottom was controlled by the two overseers of accursed memory.
Now, Clancy and Munster took this fund for their own use. When accidents happened to the workmen, their fellows, as was the custom, made grants from the fund; but the overseers refused to pay over the grants. What could the slaves do? They had their rights under the law, but they had no access to the law. Those that complained to the overseers were punished. You know yourselves what form such punishment takes﻿—the fines for faulty work that is not faulty; the overcharging of accounts in the Company’s store; the vile treatment of one’s women and children; and the allotment to bad machines whereon, work as one will, he starves.

===Chinese===
如今，弟兄们，我回到故事的开头，来讲汤姆·迪克逊那条胳膊。罗杰·范德沃特在金斯伯里的工场，起"地狱底"这个名字，起得名副其实；可在里头做活的，是人，你们往下就知道了。在那里做活的还有女人，还有孩子，小小的孩子。照法律，在那里做活的都享有正规的奴隶权利——也只是照法律罢了，因为他们许多的权利，都叫"地狱底"那两个监工，约瑟夫·克兰西和阿道夫·芒斯特，剥夺得干干净净。
说来话长，我不全讲了。我只讲那条胳膊。事情是这样的：照法律，奴隶们那点饿不死的工钱，每月要扣下一部分，存进一笔基金。这笔基金，原是为着救济那些不幸遭了工伤、染了疾病的工友。你们自己也知道，这些基金全攥在监工手里。这是法律；"地狱底"的这笔基金，也就攥在那两个万人唾骂的监工手里。
克兰西和芒斯特呢，把这笔基金挪去自己享用。工友出了工伤，照老规矩，工友们从基金里拨给补助；可监工不肯从基金里发放。奴隶们有什么法子？法律上他们有权利，可他们够不着法律。到监工面前诉苦的，都要受罚。那是什么性质的惩罚，你们自己知道——活计挑不出毛病，偏罚你做工有毛病；公司商店里的账，抬高了价钱坑你；你家女人孩子受尽糟践；还有把你派到坏机器上，任你拼死拼活地做，也只有饿死一条路。

===Original===
Once, the slaves of Hell’s Bottom protested to Vanderwater. It was the time of the year when he spent several months in Kingsbury. One of the slaves could write; it chanced that his mother could write, and she had secretly taught him as her mother had secretly taught her. So this slave wrote a round robin, wherein was contained their grievances, and all the slaves signed by mark. And, with proper stamps upon the envelope, the round robin was mailed to Roger Vanderwater. And Roger Vanderwater did nothing, save to turn the round robin over to the two overseers. Clancy and Munster were angered. They turned the guards loose at night on the slave pen. The guards were armed with pick handles. It is said that next day only half of the slaves were able to work in Hell’s Bottom. They were well beaten. The slave who could write was so badly beaten that he lived only three months. But before he died, he wrote once more, to what purpose you shall hear.
Four or five weeks afterward, Tom Dixon, a slave, had his arm torn off by a belt in Hell’s Bottom. His fellow-workmen, as usual, made a grant to him from the fund, and Clancy and Munster, as usual, refused to pay it over from the fund. The slave who could write, and who even then was dying, wrote anew a recital of their grievances. And this document was thrust into the hand of the arm that had been torn from Tom Dixon’s body.
Now it chanced that Roger Vanderwater was lying ill in his palace at the other end of Kingsbury﻿—not the dire illness that strikes down you and me, brothers; just a bit of biliousness, mayhap, or no more than a bad headache because he had eaten too heartily or drunk too deeply. But it was enough for him, being tender and soft from careful rearing. Such men, packed in cotton wool all their lives, are exceeding tender and soft. Believe me, brothers, Roger Vanderwater felt as badly with his aching head, or *thought* he felt as badly, as Tom Dixon really felt with his arm torn out by the roots.

===Chinese===
有一回，"地狱底"的奴隶们向范德沃特告了状。那时节，他一年里要在金斯伯里住上几个月。奴隶里有一个识字的——他娘恰好识字，悄悄教了他，如同她娘当年悄悄教她。于是这个奴隶写了一封联名信，把大家的冤屈都写在里头，全体奴隶画了押。信封上贴足了邮票，寄给了罗杰·范德沃特。罗杰·范德沃特什么也没做，只把联名信转给了那两个监工。克兰西和芒斯特动了怒。他们半夜里放开看守，叫看守们打进奴隶营。看守们手里是镐把。据说第二天，"地狱底"里只有一半奴隶做得动工。他们挨了好一顿打。那个识字的奴隶，打得只剩一口气，熬了三个月。可咽气之前，他又写了一回；写来做何用场，你们往下听。
四五个星期之后，奴隶汤姆·迪克逊在"地狱底"里叫皮带绞掉了一条胳膊。工友们照老规矩，从基金里拨给他补助；克兰西和芒斯特照老规矩，拒绝从基金里发放。那个识字的、眼看只剩一口气的奴隶，又把众人的冤屈写成了一纸陈情。这份文书，就塞进了那条从汤姆·迪克逊身上绞下来的胳膊的手里。
偏巧这时候，罗杰·范德沃特正躺在金斯伯里另一头的府里养病——弟兄们，不是你我害的那种凶险大病；多半是胆汁闹了点乱子，再不就是贪嘴贪杯，害了一场偏头痛。可这对他也就够了，他娇生惯养，细皮嫩肉。这种人一辈子裹在棉花胎里，细嫩得了不得。信我的话，弟兄们，罗杰·范德沃特害着他那头疼——或者只是*以为*自己害得难受——那份难受，同汤姆·迪克逊连根绞掉一条胳膊的难受，不相上下。

===Original===
It happened that Roger Vanderwater was fond of scientific farming, and that on his farm, three miles outside of Kingsbury, he had managed to grow a new kind of strawberry. He was very proud of that new strawberry of his, and he would have been out to see and pick the first ripe ones, had it not been for his illness. Because of his illness he had ordered the old farm slave to bring in personally the first box of the berries. All this was learned from the gossip of a palace scullion, who slept each night in the slave pen. The overseer of the plantation should have brought in the berries, but he was on his back with a broken leg from trying to break a colt. The scullion brought the word in the night, and it was known that next day the berries would come in. And the men in the slave pen of Hell’s Bottom, being men and not cowards, held a council.
The slave who could write, and who was sick and dying from the pick-handle beating, said he would carry Tom Dixon’s arm; also, he said he must die anyway, and that it mattered nothing if he died a little sooner. So five slaves stole from the slave pen that night after the guards had made their last rounds. One of the slaves was the man who could write. They lay in the brush by the roadside until late in the morning, when the old farm slave came driving to town with the precious fruit for the master. What of the farm slave being old and rheumatic, and of the slave who could write being stiff and injured from his beating, they moved their bodies about when they walked, very much in the same fashion. The slave who could write put on the other’s clothes, pulled the broad-brimmed hat over his eyes, climbed upon the seat of the wagon, and drove on to town. The old farm slave was kept tied all day in the bushes until evening, when the others loosed him and went back to the slave pen to take their punishment for having broken bounds.
In the meantime, Roger Vanderwater lay waiting for the berries in his wonderful bedroom﻿—such wonders and such comforts were there that they would have blinded the eyes of you and me who have never seen such things. The slave who could write said afterward that it was like a glimpse of Paradise! And why not? The labour and the lives of ten thousand slaves had gone to the making of that bedchamber, while they themselves slept in vile lairs like wild beasts. The slave who could write brought in the berries on a silver tray or platter﻿—you see, Roger Vanderwater wanted to speak with him in person about the berries.

===Chinese===
偏巧罗杰·范德沃特喜好科学种田，在金斯伯里城外三英里的农庄上，他培植出了一种新草莓。他宝贝那新草莓，要不是害着病，早亲自去摘头一茬熟的了。因为害着病，他吩咐老园圃奴隶，头一匣草莓务必亲自送进城来。这些都是府里一个洗碗小厮传出来的闲话，他每夜回奴隶营睡。本该种植园的管事送草莓进来，可他试一匹小马驹，摔断了腿，正躺着起不来。小厮夜里捎来了信，都知道第二天草莓进城。"地狱底"奴隶营里的汉子们是汉子，不是懦夫，连夜开了会。
那个识字的、叫镐把打得半死不活的奴隶说，汤姆·迪克逊的胳膊由他来抱；他还说，横竖是个死，早死几天晚死几天，算不得什么。于是当夜，看守查完最后一遍岗，五个奴隶溜出了营。其中一个，就是那识字的。他们在路边的草丛里伏到后半晌，等老园圃奴隶赶着车、载着献给主子的时鲜果子进城来。那园圃奴隶年迈风湿，识字的奴隶挨了打，周身僵痛，两个人走起路来，扭动身子的架势竟一模一样。识字的奴隶换上对方的衣裳，把宽檐帽压到眼皮上，爬上马车座，赶车进了城。老园圃奴隶被拴在灌木丛里困了一整天，到晚上才由同伙解开，其余的人回奴隶营收惩罚去了——谁叫他们越了界。
这时候，罗杰·范德沃特正躺在那间妙不可言的卧房里等草莓——那房间里的种种妙处、种种安逸，你我这样没见过世面的人，看一眼要晃瞎眼睛。那识字的奴隶后来说，那就像望见了天堂一角！为什么不呢？一万奴隶的劳力、一万条性命，堆成了那一间卧房，他们自己却睡在野兽窝一样的臭窟里。识字的奴隶用银托盘——或者银盘子——把草莓端了进来。你们看，罗杰·范德沃特要亲自就草莓的事问他的话。

===Original===
The slave who could write tottered his dying body across the wonderful room and knelt by the couch of Vanderwater, holding out before him the tray. Large green leaves covered the top of the tray, and these the body-servant alongside whisked away so that Vanderwater could see. And Roger Vanderwater, propped upon his elbow, saw. He saw the fresh, wonderful fruit lying there like precious jewels, and in the midst of it the arm of Tom Dixon as it had been torn from his body, well washed, of course, my brothers, and very white against the bloodred fruit. And also he saw, clutched in the stiff, dead fingers, the petition of his slaves who toiled in Hell’s Bottom.
“Take and read,” said the slave who could write. And even as the master took the petition, the body-servant, who till then had been motionless with surprise, struck with his fist the kneeling slave upon the mouth. The slave was dying anyway, and was very weak, and did not mind. He made no sound, and, having fallen over on his side, he lay there quietly, bleeding from the blow on the mouth. The physician, who had run for the palace guards, came back with them, and the slave was dragged upright upon his feet. But as they dragged him up, his hand clutched Tom Dixon’s arm from where it had fallen on the floor.
“He shall be flung alive to the hounds!” the body-servant was crying in great wrath. “He shall be flung alive to the hounds!”

===Chinese===
识字的奴隶拖着将死的身子，一路摇晃着穿过那间妙房，跪在范德沃特的卧榻边，把托盘举在身前。大片绿叶盖满盘面，立在一旁的贴身仆人一把掀开，好叫范德沃特看。罗杰·范德沃特用胳膊肘撑起身子，看见了。他看见鲜灵灵的奇珍果子摆在那里，像一匣宝石；果子当中，是汤姆·迪克逊那条胳膊，同它从身上绞下来时一个样子——当然洗干净了，弟兄们——衬着血红的果子，白得刺眼。他还看见，那条胳膊僵死的指头紧攥着的，是他"地狱底"众奴隶的陈情书。
"拿去念罢，"识字的奴隶说。主子刚接过陈情书，那直到方才还惊得发怔的贴身仆人，一拳打在跪着的奴隶嘴上。那奴隶横竖是死的人了，虚弱得很，也不在意。他没有出声，侧身倒下去，静静地躺在那里，嘴上的伤口淌着血。跑去叫府卫的医生领着卫兵回来了，奴隶被拖起来架直。可拖他起来的时候，他的手一把攥住了汤姆·迪克逊那条胳膊——它方才掉在了地板上。
"要把他活活扔给猎狗！"贴身仆人怒气冲冲地嚎。"要把他活生生扔给猎狗！"

===Original===
But Roger Vanderwater, forgetting his headache, still leaning on his elbow, commanded silence, and went on reading the petition. And while he read, there was silence, all standing upright, the wrathful body-servant, the physician, the palace guards, and in their midst the slave, bleeding at the mouth and still holding Tom Dixon’s arm. And when Roger Vanderwater had done, he turned upon the slave, saying﻿—
“If in this paper there be one lie, you shall be sorry that you were ever born.”
And the slave said, “I have been sorry all my life that I was born.”
Roger Vanderwater looked at him closely, and the slave said﻿—

===Chinese===
可罗杰·范德沃特忘了他的头痛，仍旧撑着胳膊肘，喝令肃静，接着念陈情书。他念的时候，满屋寂静，人人直挺挺站着：狂怒的贴身仆人、医生、府卫，还有他们当中的那个奴隶，嘴角淌着血，手里仍旧抱着汤姆·迪克逊的胳膊。罗杰·范德沃特念完了，转向那奴隶，说道——
"这纸里倘若有一句假话，叫你悔恨爹娘生下你来。"
那奴隶说："我悔了一辈子了。"
罗杰·范德沃特凑近端详他，那奴隶说——

===Original===
“You have done your worst to me. I am dying now. In a week I shall be dead, so it does not matter if you kill me now.”
“What do you with that?” the master asked, pointing to the arm; and the slave made answer﻿—
“I take it back to the pen to give it burial. Tom Dixon was my friend. We worked beside each other at our looms.”

===Chinese===
"你顶毒的手段，都使到我身上了。我如今是死定了。不出一个星期就是死；这会儿杀不杀我，没什么两样。"
"你抱着那个做什么？"主子指着那条胳膊问；那奴隶答道——
"我抱它回营去，好生安葬。汤姆·迪克逊是我的朋友。我们织机挨着织机，并排做活。"

===Original===
There is little more to my tale, brothers. The slave and the arm were sent back in a cart to the pen. Nor were any of the slaves punished for what they had done. Indeed, Roger Vanderwater made investigation and punished the two overseers, Joseph Clancy and Adolph Munster. Their freeholds were taken from them. They were branded, each upon the forehead, their right hands were cut off, and they were turned loose upon the highway to wander and beg until they died. And the fund was managed rightfully thereafter for a time﻿—for a time only, my brothers; for after Roger Vanderwater came his son, Albert, who was a cruel master and half mad.
Brothers, that slave who carried the arm into the presence of the master was my father. He was a brave man. And even as his mother secretly taught him to read, so did he teach me. Because he died shortly after from the pick-handle beating, Roger Vanderwater took me out of the slave pen and tried to make various better things out of me. I might have become an overseer in Hell’s Bottom, but I chose to become a storyteller, wandering over the land and getting close to my brothers, the slaves, everywhere. And I tell you stories like this, secretly, knowing that you will not betray me; for if you did, you know as well as I that my tongue will be torn out and that I shall tell stories no more.
